Stationary wave solutions to two dimensional viscous shallow water equations: theory of small and large solutions Thanks: N. Stevenson was supported by an NSF Graduate Research Fellowship Thanks: I. Tice was supported by an NSF Grant (DMS #2204912).
Noah Stevenson, Ian Tice

Abstract
. We study a system of forced viscous shallow water equations with nontrivial bathymetry in two spatial dimensions. We develop a well-posedness theory for small but arbitrary forcing data, as well as for a fixed data profile but large amplitude. In the latter case, solutions may actually fail to exist for large amplitude, but in this case we prove that one of three physically meaningful breakdown scenarios occurs. Through the use of implicit function theorem techniques and a priori estimates, we construct both spatially periodic and solitary (non-periodic but spatially localized) solutions. The solitary case is substantially more complicated, requiring a delicate analysis in weighted Sobolev spaces. To the best of our knowledge, these results constitute the first general construction of stationary wave solutions, large or otherwise, to the viscous shallow water equations and the first general analysis of large solitary wave solutions to any viscous free boundary fluid model.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35Q35, 35C07, 35B30; Secondary 47J07, 76A20, 35M30

1.
Introduction

1.1.
The shallow water equations with applied force and bathymetry

In this paper we study a system of shallow water equations in two spatial dimensions that incorporates the effects of gravity, capillarity, viscosity, laminar drag, applied forces, and variable bathymetry (bottom topography):

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}h+\nabla\cdot((h-b)v)=0,\\
\partial_{t}((h-b)v)+\nabla\cdot((h-b)v\otimes v)+\alpha v-\mu\nabla\cdot((h-b)\mathbb{S}v)+(h-b)(g-\sigma\Delta)\nabla h={\upkappa}F,\\
F=\int_{b}^{h}f(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y+(h-b)\nabla(\varphi(\cdot,h))+(\varphi I-\Xi)(\cdot,h)\nabla h+\xi(\cdot,h).\end{cases}

Here the unknowns are the tangential fluid velocity field
v:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the fluid height
h:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. The bottom topography is modeled via the bathymetry function
b:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. We will always assume that the fluid height lies above the bathymetry,
h>b
, which means there are no ‘dry regions’. The coefficient of laminar friction is
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the viscosity is
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and the shallow water viscous stress tensor is

(1.2)

\mathbb{S}v=\nabla v+\nabla v^{\mathrm{t}}+2(\nabla\cdot v)I.

The gravitational acceleration in the vertical direction is
g\geqslant 0
and the surface tension coefficient is
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. The vector field
F:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with units of length, is the profile of the forcing applied to the system while the coefficient
\upkappa\in\mathbb{R}
, with units of acceleration, indicates the strength of the applied forcing.

The system (
1.1
) is a viscous variant of the classical Saint-Venant equations (
\sigma=\mu={\upkappa}=0
). Its solutions approximate those of the free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes system in the regimes in which either the fluid depth is very small or the solution has large characteristic wavelength. As a result, the model is physically, mathematically, and practically relevant in describing the flow of a thin fluid film over the graphical surface determined by
b
. For details of the derivation of (
1.1
) from the Navier-Stokes system see Appendix
B
.

There are formal similarities between system (
1.1
) and the barotropic compressible Navier-Stokes equations with density-dependent viscosity, in which
h-b
and
v
play the roles of the density and velocity, respectively. This correspondence dictates how we shall refer to the individual equations in the shallow water system. The first equation in (
1.1
) is dubbed the continuity equation, and it describes how the surface height
h
is transported and stretched by flow. The second vector equation is called the momentum equation, and it asserts a balance of the advection term
\partial_{t}((h-b)v)+\nabla\cdot((h-b)v\otimes v)
by the sum of laminar drag
-\alpha v
, dissipation
\mu\nabla\cdot((h-b)\mathbb{S}v)
, gravity-capillary effects
-(h-b)(g-\sigma\Delta)\nabla h
, and applied force
{\upkappa}F
. Notice that the role played by the bathymetry
b
is only to modify the fluid depth
h-b
, which shows up as a sort of ‘variable coefficient’. We emphasize that system (
1.1
) is quasilinear and of mixed-type, as it involves both dissipative and hyperbolic-dispersive structures.

The third equation of (
1.1
) shows that the term
F
is a function of both the bathymetry and the height. The precise form recorded there is derived by taking the shallow water limit of the free boundary Navier-Stokes equations acted on by a bulk force and surface stress. The function
f:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is derived from the tangential part of the bulk force, while the functions
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\Xi:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}
, and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
are pieces of a surface stress tensor. Again, we refer the reader to Appendix
B
for the precise details of the derivation.

Figure 1.

Depicted here are the height data from numerical simulations of periodic stationary solutions to (
1.1
), in contour plot form (top) and graph form (bottom), for three values of applied forcing strength
{\upkappa}\in\{{20000,90000,280000}\}
. The bathymetry
b
(shown in orange on the bottom) is a fixed half-ellipse with base at height
-2
and maximum at height
0
. The forcing profile
F
is a component of gravity in the
e_{1}
direction, i.e.
\varphi=0
,
\Xi=0
,
\xi=0
, and
f=e_{1}
. The surface satisfies
h=1+h_{0}
for an average zero perturbation
h_{0}
. The other physical parameters are
\alpha=0.65
,
g=0.5
,
\mu=0.3
, and
\sigma=1.1
. This is meant to depict stationary shallow water flow over an inclined bathymetry.

Our principal goal in this paper is to develop a well-posedness theory for stationary (time independent) solutions to (
1.1
). We will construct both spatially periodic and non-periodic but localized and decaying solutions; we will refer to the former as the ‘periodic case’ and the latter as the ‘solitary case’. We will first construct small solutions for arbitrary data in a fixed open neighborhood of zero and then employ global implicit function theorem techniques to study how solutions behave as the amplitude of a fixed forcing profile is increased. In the latter case we will derive quantitative ‘alternatives’ that show that either the curves of solutions can be continued for all forcing data amplitudes or else degenerate in a specific, physically meaningful, manner.

Although we consider general
F
of the form specified in (
1.1
), several specific choices warrant mentioning. One can model gravity-driven flow in the direction
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
down an incline with the choice
F=(h-b)\nu
, i.e.
f=\nu
,
\varphi=0
,
\Xi=0
, and
\xi=0
. This scenario is depicted in Figures
1
and
2
. On the other hand, the choice
F=(h-b)\nabla p
, which is achieved by
f=0
,
\varphi=p
,
\Xi=pI
, and
\xi=0
for some scalar surface pressure
p:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, models a local change in pressure above the free surface.

1.2.
Equilibria and stationary reformulation

We now aim to identify the equilibrium solutions to (
1.1
) and then reformulate (
1.1
) perturbatively about these equilibria in a nondimensional fashion. When
F=0
and
b
is a bounded function, the admissible steady solutions are constant and given by

(1.3)

v_{\mathrm{eq}}=0,\quad h_{\mathrm{eq}}=\mathrm{H}+\sup b\quad\text{for any choice of }\mathrm{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Fixing an arbitrary choice of
\mathrm{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we may nondimensionalize by selecting units with the effective normalization
(\mathrm{H},\mu,\sigma)\mapsto(1,1,1)
. This is a convenient choice, as positive viscosity and capillarity are crucial for our analytic techniques. Define the length and time

(1.4)

\mathrm{L}=\mu^{2}/\sigma,\quad\mathrm{T}=\mu^{3}/\sigma^{2}

and the new (negative) bathymetry
\beta
, velocity
\upupsilon
, and free surface height
\upeta
as follows:

(1.5)

b(x)=\sup b-\mathrm{H}\beta(x/\mathrm{L}),\quad v(t,x)=(\mathrm{L}/\mathrm{T})\upupsilon(t/\mathrm{T},x/\mathrm{L}),\quad h(t,x)=\mathrm{H}(1+\upeta(t/\mathrm{T},x/\mathrm{L}))+\sup b.

In turn, define the new data profile
\Upphi
, inverse slip coefficient
A
, capillary number
G
, and inverse Froude number
\kappa
via

(1.6)

F(t,x)=\mathrm{H}\Upphi(t/\mathrm{T},x/\mathrm{L}),\quad A=\frac{\alpha\mathrm{T}}{\mathrm{H}}=\frac{\alpha\mu^{3}}{\mathrm{H}\sigma^{2}},\quad G=\frac{g\mathrm{H}\mathrm{T}^{2}}{\mathrm{L^{2}}}=\frac{g\mathrm{H}\mu^{2}}{\sigma^{2}},\quad\kappa=\frac{{\upkappa}\mathrm{T}^{2}}{\mathrm{L}}=\frac{{\upkappa}\mu^{4}}{\sigma^{3}}.

Notice that
\beta\geqslant 0
with
\inf\beta=0
; we work with the nonnegative quantity
\beta
here for convenience, but one should think of
-\beta
as the nondimensional description of the bathymetry. The positive fluid depth condition
h>b
translates in these new variables to
1+\upeta+\beta>0
. In terms of the quantities (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), system (
1.1
) becomes

(1.7)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\upeta+\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\upeta)\upupsilon)=0,\\
\partial_{t}((1+\beta+\upeta)\upupsilon)+\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\upeta)\upupsilon\otimes\upupsilon)+A\upupsilon-\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\upeta)\mathbb{S}\upupsilon)+(1+\beta+\upeta)(G-\Delta)\nabla\upeta=\kappa\Upphi.\end{cases}

As previously mentioned, our focus in this work is the not the full time dependent system (
1.7
), but rather the particular class of stationary (and thus global-in-time) solutions generated by generic time independent forcing. We are therefore led to make the stationary ansatz
\partial_{t}\upeta=0
,
\partial_{t}\upupsilon=0
, and
\partial_{t}\Upphi=0
, which we formulate for new stationary unknowns as
\upeta(t,x)=\boldsymbol{\eta}(x)
,
\upupsilon(t,x)=\boldsymbol{u}(x)
, and
\Upphi(t,x)=\Phi(x)
. In terms of the new unknowns, the system (
1.7
) becomes

(1.8)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\boldsymbol{u})=0,\\
\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u})+A\boldsymbol{u}-\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\mathbb{S}\boldsymbol{u})+(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})(G-\Delta)\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}=\kappa\Phi.\end{cases}

The final form of the momentum equation source term
\Phi
in (
1.8
), which we formally write as

(1.9)

\Phi=\phi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\eta})+\nabla((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\psi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\eta}))+\tau(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\eta})\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},

not only depends on
\boldsymbol{\eta}
but also involves the generic functions
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\tau:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}
. This captures the general structure of the forcing present in the third equation of (
1.1
) after the rescaling and the stationary ansatz. See Section
1.4.1
for a more thorough discussion on these relationships.

We now discuss the necessity of nontrivial forcing in (
1.8
) for the generation of nontrivial solutions. As part of the proof of the later result Proposition
5.2
, we establish the following power-dissipation identity for solutions to system (
1.8
) with trivial forcing (
\kappa=0
):

(1.10)

\int A|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}+(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})(2^{-1}\char 12\relax{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathrm{t}}}^{2}+2\char 12\relax{\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}}^{2})=0.

Here the domain of integration is understood as
\mathbb{R}^{2}
when the solutions are solitary and as a fundamental domain when the solutions are periodic. When
A>0
and
1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}>0
, identity (
1.10
) implies that
\boldsymbol{u}=0
. In turn from (
1.8
) we may then deduce that
\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}=0
, and hence the solution is trivial. This tells us that nontrivial solitary or periodic stationary solutions to (
1.8
) cannot exist unless they are generated by a nontrivial stationary applied force or stress, meaning
\kappa\Phi\neq 0
.

1.3.
Previous work

A number of closely related variants of our shallow water system have been derived and studied in the literature: see, for instance
[
41
,
50
,
40
,
25
,
5
,
6
,
8
,
36
]
. The model we employ is most similar to those derived in the articles of Marche
[
43
]
, Bresch
[
9
]
, and Mascia
[
44
]
; the article
[
9
]
also contains a very thorough survey of the dynamic problem for (
1.1
) and related models with flat bathymetry. As our paper’s focus is the stationary wave problem over smooth bottom topography, we will focus this literature review on work that accounts for bathymetry or studies time independent solutions.

Intimately related to the problem of stationary waves is that of traveling waves. Within the context of the shallow water equations (both inviscid and viscous) there is a large existing literature analyzing special types of one dimensional traveling waves down an incline, known as roll waves. Dressler
[
20
]
gives the first construction of discontinuous roll wave solutions to the inviscid Saint-Venant equations. Merkin and Needham
[
47
,
46
]
augment to Dressler’s model an energy dissipation term and study the resulting roll waves; Hwang and Chang
[
28
]
provide further analyses. Chang, Cheng, and Prokopiou
[
53
]
include capillary effects and produce solitary roll waves. A complete theory of linear and nonlinear stability of one dimensional roll wave solutions to both the inviscid and viscous shallow water equations is developed in the works
[
32
,
4
,
3
,
31
,
56
,
30
]
. The existence of two dimensional viscous gravity-capillary roll waves is addressed by Stevenson
[
60
]
. We also mention that Stevenson and Tice
[
61
]
establish the well-posedness for the small amplitude solitary traveling wave problem for system (
1.1
) over trivial bathymetry; however large or stationary waves were left unaddressed.

We next discuss the shallow water system with explicit bathymetric effects. To the best of our knowledge there are no results for our specific shallow water model (
1.1
); nevertheless, there are some results on related models. Cherevko and Chupakhin
[
15
,
16
]
study shallow water equations on a rotating sphere and provide stationary solutions. De Valeriola and Van Schaftingen
[
18
]
study point vortex type stationary solutions and their desingularization within a system of shallow water equations with bathymetry called the lake equations; this is extended to multiple vortices by Cao and Liu
[
12
]
. Marangos and Porter
[
42
]
study linear shallow water theory over structured bathymetry. Ketcheson and Quezada de Luna
[
54
]
study the dispersive properties of linearized water waves over a periodic bathymetry and compare numerically with the Saint-Venant equations.

As the shallow water equations are derived from the free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, our work is also linked to the stationary and traveling wave problems for free boundary fluids. For the inviscid free boundary Euler equations, the water wave problem has been the target of intense study for well over a century. The interested reader is referred to the survey articles of Toland
[
66
]
, Groves
[
26
]
, Strauss
[
65
]
, and Haziot, Hur, Strauss, Toland, Wahlén, Walsh, and Wheeler
[
27
]
.

In contrast, the study of stationary and traveling wave solutions to viscous free boundary problems has only recently commenced. An essential feature of these variants of the problem is that waves are generated by the application of stationary or traveling external forces and stresses. For experimental studies regarding these types of traveling waves we refer, for instance, to the work of Akylas, Cho, Diorio, and Duncan
[
17
,
19
]
, Duncan and Masnadi
[
45
]
, and Cho and Park
[
51
,
52
]
.

Leoni and Tice
[
39
]
give the first mathematical construction of strictly traveling wave solutions to the free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations. This result is generalized by Stevenson and Tice
[
63
,
62
]
to incompressible fluids with multiple layers and to compressible free boundary flows, and by Koganemaru and Tice
[
34
,
35
]
to periodic or inclined fluids and flows obeying the Navier slip boundary condition. Nguyen and Tice
[
49
]
construct traveling wave solutions to free boundary Darcy flow and, in the periodic case, analyze their stability. The existence of solitary stationary waves requires more difficult analysis than the strictly traveling cases; Stevenson and Tice
[
64
]
give the first construction of solitary stationary traveling wave solutions to the free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes system. All of the aforementioned results on stationary and traveling waves in viscous fluids regard small amplitude solutions. The first general constructions of large amplitude traveling wave solutions are carried out by Nguyen
[
48
]
and Brownfield and Nguyen
[
10
]
for free boundary Darcy flow.

Within the context of these previous results on viscous traveling and stationary waves, one sees that the current paper furthers the theory by settling two outstanding questions about the shallow water system. First, in the case of both Reynolds and Bond numbers finite we resolve the problem of well-posedness for small amplitude stationary waves. Second, we provide the first study of generic large stationary wave solutions, further expanding the study of viscous traveling wave problems past the realm of small solutions. Another key feature of our results is that we handle both the cases of periodic and solitary waves. For the latter case our work stands as the first construction of large solitary waves solutions within the viscous stationary and traveling wave problem literature.

1.4.
Main results, discussion, and outline

We now come to the statement of our main theorems, the proofs of which can be found in Section
6
. Our results split into two categories based on whether we assume the solutions are periodic or not, with the latter case resulting in spatially localized solitary waves. Within each category we prove two results, one on small solutions and one on large solutions. The former establishes the well-posedness of the system (
1.8
) with a fixed bathymetry
\beta
and
\kappa=1
, and forcing
\Phi
generic and small (near
0
). The large data result then explores what happens for a fixed but arbitrary choice of forcing data profile but variable forcing strength
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. Before stating any of these, a discussion of how we handle the forcing term
\Phi
in (
1.8
) is required.

1.4.1.
Data technicalities

Equation (
1.9
) shows that the forcing profile
\Phi
of the second equation in (
1.8
) depends nonlinearly on the free surface function
\boldsymbol{\eta}
. As we discuss in Appendix
B
, this dependence is not arbitrary and has a specific functional form
\boldsymbol{\eta}\mapsto\Phi
derived from the base free boundary Navier-Stokes system. In specifying this dependence we run into some technical issues that merit discussion.

The first issue we encounter is that we would like to impose just enough regularity on
\Phi
to produce strong solutions, say
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in H^{3}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\Phi\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Given that
\Phi
contains terms of the form
\phi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\eta})
, we then run into the usual headache of dealing with (partial) composition with maps that are no better than measurable on a product. Of course, this is an old problem, and a typical solution is to force the maps defining
\Phi
to be Carathéodory functions, which imposes a.e. continuity in the second variable.

The second issue is that we aim to produce solutions with the implicit function theorem for maps between Banach spaces: the standard local one for small solutions, and a specialized global one for large solutions. At a minimum, this requires continuous differentiability for the (Nemytskii) map
\mathcal{X}\supseteq U\ni\boldsymbol{\eta}\mapsto\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}
, where
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are (to be determined) Banach spaces of functions and
U\subseteq\mathcal{X}
is an open set. Again, this is not a new problem, and one solution is to impose enough regularity on the maps defining the dependence of
\Phi
on
\boldsymbol{\eta}
to invoke some version of the omega lemma (e.g. Lemma 2.4.18 in
[
1
]
, or the main results of
[
29
]
) to get the needed continuous differentiability between
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
. For example, for the component term
\boldsymbol{\eta}\mapsto\phi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\eta})
we would expect to need
\phi
to be at least continuously differentiable in its second argument, surpassing the Carathéodory mandate.

It turns out that the nonlinear structure of (
1.8
) allows us to formulate solutions as the zeros of a real analytic map between Banach spaces. Exploiting this analytic structure is advantageous, as it allows us to employ an analytic global implicit function theorem of Chen, Walsh, and Wheeler
[
14
]
(see also Theorem
5.1
below), which gives us stronger information than other global implicit function theorems (e.g. Section II.6 in
[
33
]
). Furthermore, real analyticity also presents a convenient, if somewhat tricky to explain, mechanism for dealing with the above two issues. We now aim to explain our technique by focusing on the simple case
\Phi=\phi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\eta})
.

Fix the map
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times I\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
for some interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. The discussion above suggests that we want
\phi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\eta})\in\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the map
\mathcal{X}\supseteq U\ni\boldsymbol{\eta}\mapsto\phi(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\eta})\in\mathcal{Y}
to be real-analytic. This is relatively easy to do if we assume that
\phi
has a special structure. Indeed, if we assume
\phi
is continuous and a polynomial in its final variable, say
\phi(x,y)=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}g_{j}(x)y^{j}
for
g_{j}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap\mathcal{Y}
, then the pointwise evaluation
\Phi(x)=\phi(x,\boldsymbol{\eta}(x))
presents no trouble if we assume
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathcal{X}
for
\mathcal{X}
a unital Banach subalgebra of
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
, and we can obtain analyticity of the map
\boldsymbol{\eta}\mapsto\Phi
by noting that
\mathcal{X}\ni\boldsymbol{\eta}\mapsto\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}g_{j}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{j}\in\mathcal{Y}
is analytic when
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y})
via the pointwise product.

This special polynomial form of
\phi
is too restrictive, but the analysis presents a natural generalization. We first Curry the map
\phi
in its second argument to think of
\phi(x,y)=\hat{\phi}(y)(x)
for a given analytic map
\hat{\phi}:I\to\mathcal{Y}
. There is now some technical hassle in making sense of the formal diagonal composition
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni x\mapsto\hat{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\eta}(x))(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
in the general cases in which we only have the embedding
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and pointwise evaluation is discontinuous. We circumvent this issue and more in Appendix
A.1
by developing a variant of the holomorphic functional calculus that allows us to
uniquely
(subject to some mild technical assumptions) define a ‘Nemytskii map’
\Upomega_{\hat{\phi}}(\boldsymbol{\eta})\in\mathcal{Y}
in such a way that the function
\mathcal{X}\supseteq U\ni\boldsymbol{\eta}\mapsto\Upomega_{\hat{\phi}}(\boldsymbol{\eta})\in\mathcal{Y}
is analytic and also satisfies:
\Upomega_{\hat{\phi}}(\boldsymbol{\eta})=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}g_{j}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{j}
whenever
\hat{\phi}(y)=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}g_{j}y^{j}
is a polynomial with
g_{j}\in\mathcal{Y}
; and
[{\Upomega_{\hat{\phi}}(\boldsymbol{\eta})}](x)=\hat{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\eta}(x))(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
when
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

The upshot of this discussion is that in the following theorem statements we will take
\Phi
in (
1.8
), whose formal expression we initially gave as (
1.9
), to be rigorously defined as

(1.11)

\Phi=\Upomega_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\eta})+\nabla((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\Upomega_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\eta}))+\Upomega_{\tau}(\boldsymbol{\eta})\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}

where
\Upomega
is the Nemytskii map constructed by the holomorphic function calculus in Appendix
A.1
as described above, and
\phi
,
\psi
,
\tau
are generically selected real analytic maps defined on an interval subset of the real line with values in certain Banach spaces of locally square summable functions.

1.4.2.
Results for periodic functions

Our first two theorems consider solutions to system (
1.8
) in spaces of periodic functions. We fix a tuple of period lengths
L=(L_{1},L_{2})\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{2}
and let
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}=(L_{1}\mathbb{T})\times(L_{2}\mathbb{T})
denote the
2
-torus of side lengths
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
. Given any
s\in\mathbb{N}
and a finite dimensional real vector space
V
we let
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};V)
denote the standard Sobolev space of
V
-valued
L
-periodic functions; also we let
\mathring{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};V)\subset H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};V)
denote the closed subspace of functions with vanishing mean.

To properly state our first theorem on small periodic solutions, we now need to specify the appropriate Banach space for the data
\phi
,
\psi
, and
\tau
of (
1.11
) (whose role is discussed in Section
1.4.1
). The precise definition is a bit technical (see Section
6.1
, and in particular equation (
6.1
)), so for now we shall content ourselves with denoting the data space

(1.12)

\boldsymbol{Y}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})=\boldsymbol{A}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times\boldsymbol{A}(H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}))\times\boldsymbol{A}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}))

and mentioning that
\boldsymbol{A}(\mathcal{Y})
, for
\mathcal{Y}\in\{{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}),L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}})}\}
, is a Banach space of
\mathcal{Y}-
valued real analytic functions defined on a real interval, containing at least every entire function.

Theorem 1

(Proved in Theorem
6.2
)
.

Let
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
satisfy
\min\beta=0
,
A>0
, and
G\geqslant 0
. There exists positive radii
r_{\mathrm{soln}},r_{\mathrm{data}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and nonempty open balls
B_{\mathrm{data}}=B(0,r_{\mathrm{data}})\subset\boldsymbol{Y}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
and
B_{\mathrm{soln}}=B(0,r_{\mathrm{soln}})\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
with the property that for all
(\phi,\psi,\tau)\in B_{\mathrm{data}}
there exists a unique
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})\in B_{\mathrm{soln}}
solving the system of equations (
1.8
) with
\kappa=1
and
\Phi
determined by (
1.11
). Moreover, the mapping
(\phi,\psi,\tau)\mapsto(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})
is real analytic.

Theorem
1
establishes the well-posedness of equations (
1.8
) in the class of small amplitude and periodic functions. Our next main result studies large solutions in the periodic setting. In this case we make some further (mild) restrictions on the data: we require a bit more smoothness on the
\tau
component and that the domain of analyticity is unbounded from above (so that the composition with tall free surfaces
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is well-defined). The hypotheses on the bathymetry
\beta
are the same as in Theorem
1
.

Theorem 2

(Proved in Theorem
6.3
)
.

Let
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
satisfy
\min\beta=0
,
A>0
, and
G\geqslant 0
. Set
a_{\beta}=1+\max\beta
, fix any choice of real analytic functions

(1.13)

(\phi,\psi,\tau):(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times(L^{\infty}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}),

and define the set of admissible solutions (corresponding to the above fixed data profile and variable forcing strength) as

(1.14)

\boldsymbol{S}=\{{(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},\kappa)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;\min(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})>0,\;\text{system~\eqref{stationary nondimensionalized equations} is satisfied with~\eqref{percolate propane pulvarized paintbrush}}}\}.

Then there exists a locally analytic curve
\mathscr{S}\subseteq\boldsymbol{S}
, with
0\in\mathscr{S}
, that is parametrized by the continuous mapping
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(\boldsymbol{u}(s),\boldsymbol{\eta}(s),\kappa(s))\in\mathscr{S}
and satisfies
0\mapsto 0
as well as the limits

(1.15)

\lim_{|s|\to\infty}\Big[{\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\frac{1}{\min(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}(s))}+|\kappa(s)|}\Big]=\infty.

Note that in Theorems
1
and
2
the periodicity allows us to set the nondimensionalized constant
G=0
; as one can see from the definition in (
1.6
), this can occur due to vanishing gravitational field or other ratios vanishing. The limits (
1.15
) indicate that for a fixed forcing profile, either there are solutions in
\mathscr{S}
for an unbounded set of forcing amplitudes
\kappa
, or else
\kappa
remains bounded but the solutions degenerate by the free surface touching the bottom or diverging in supremum norm. See Figures
1
and
2
for depictions of some of the periodic solutions considered by Theorem
2
.

Figure 2.
Shown here are are numerical simulations of the divergence (top row) and curl (bottom row) of the tangential velocity
v
of gravity driven shallow water flow over a fixed, but randomly generated bathymetry
b
(shown on the left). These correspond to stationary solutions to system (
1.1
) with forcing
F
determined by
f=e_{1}
,
\varphi=0
,
\Xi=0
, and
\xi=0
. The forcing strength varies in the set
{\upkappa}\in\{{400000,1000000,2700000}\}
while the other parameters
\alpha
,
g
,
\mu
,
\sigma
, and equilibrium height are all set to
1
.

1.4.3.
Results for solitary functions

Our final two main results are the analogs of Theorems
1
and
2
but in solitary (i.e. decaying at infinity) function spaces. The functional framework becomes significantly more complicated due to the fact that in this setting we must overcome a certain low-mode degeneracy in the estimates for the free surface. We achieve this by working with Sobolev spaces built on weighted Lebesgue spaces. The full details of these function spaces can be found in Sections
2.1
and
2.2
, but for now we shall content ourselves with the following abbreviated definitions. Given
1\leqslant q\leqslant\infty
,
0<\delta<1
, and a finite dimensional real vector space
V
, we let
L^{q}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};V)
denote the set of
f\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2};V)
for which
\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2};V)
, where
X:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
denotes the identity map and
\langle\cdot\rangle
is the bracket (
1.25
). Given
s\in\mathbb{N}
, we then define the weighted Sobolev space
W^{s,q}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};V)
to be the collection of
f\in L^{q}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};V)
such that for all multiindices
|\alpha|\leqslant s
we have
\partial^{\alpha}f\in L^{q}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};V)
. When
q=2
we write
H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};V)
in place of
W^{s,2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};V)
.

These weighted Sobolev spaces are appropriate container spaces for the velocity
\boldsymbol{u}
and the data, but they are not quite adequate for the free surface function
\boldsymbol{\eta}
. For it we also need to introduce the weighted gradient Sobolev spaces, denoted by
\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
0<\delta<1
, and consisting of
f\in L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\nabla f\in H^{s-1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, normed by the latter inclusion. These spaces satisfy the embeddings
H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow(H^{s}_{\delta}+W^{\infty,2/\delta})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

The solitary case necessitates imposing some admissibility conditions on the bathymetry profile
\beta
. Namely, we need its derivatives to be localized, which we impose by assuming that there exists
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

(1.16)

\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\;\nabla\beta\in H^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}),\;\text{and}\;\inf\beta=0.

We now come to our third main theorem, which considers small solitary solutions. In place of (
1.12
), the solitary data space is

(1.17)

\boldsymbol{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\boldsymbol{A}(L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times\boldsymbol{A}(H^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times\boldsymbol{A}(L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}})),

whose precise definition is given at the start of Section
6.1
.

Theorem 3

(Proved in Theorem
6.2
)
.

Let
\beta
be as in (
1.16
),
A>0
, and
G>0
. There exists positive radii
\rho_{\mathrm{soln}},\rho_{\mathrm{data}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and nonempty open balls
B_{\mathrm{data}}=B(0,\rho_{\mathrm{data}})\subset\boldsymbol{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
B_{\mathrm{soln}}=B(0,\rho_{\mathrm{soln}})\subset H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with the property that for all
(\phi,\psi,\tau)\in B_{\mathrm{data}}
there exists a unique
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})\in B_{\mathrm{soln}}
solving (
1.8
) with
\kappa=1
and
\Phi
determined by (
1.11
). Moreover, the mapping
(\phi,\psi,\tau)\mapsto(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})
is real analytic.

Theorem
3
establishes the well-posedness of equations (
1.8
) in a class of small amplitude and solitary functions. Our final main theorem examines large solutions in the solitary case. As in the periodic case, we make some further minor restrictions on the class of data considered.

Theorem 4

(Proved in Theorem
6.3
)
.

Let
\beta
be as in (
1.16
),
A>0
, and
G>0
. Set
a_{\beta}=1+\sup\beta
, fix any choice of real analytic functions

(1.18)

(\phi,\psi,\tau):(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times(L^{\infty}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}\cap H^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}),

and define the set of admissible solutions (corresponding to the above fixed data profile and variable forcing strength) as

(1.19)

\boldsymbol{S}=\{{(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},\kappa)\in H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;\inf(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})>0,\;\text{system~\eqref{stationary nondimensionalized equations} with~\eqref{percolate propane pulvarized paintbrush} is satisfied}}\}.

There exists a locally analytic curve
\mathscr{S}\subseteq\boldsymbol{S}
, with
0\in\mathscr{S}
, that is parametrized by the continuous mapping
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(\boldsymbol{u}(s),\boldsymbol{\eta}(s),\kappa(s))\in\mathscr{S}
and satisfies
0\mapsto 0
as well as the limits

(1.20)

\lim_{|s|\to\infty}\Big[{\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\min\{{\lVert\boldsymbol{u}(s)\rVert_{L^{q}},\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)\rVert_{L^{r}}}\}+\frac{1}{\inf(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}(s))}+|\kappa(s)|}\Big]=\infty

for every choice of
q\in(4/3,2)
and
r\in[4,\infty)
.

Note that in the stationary case we must set
G>0
to produce solutions. Again, the limits (
1.20
) indicate that either solutions exist for an unbounded set of
\kappa
, or else they degenerate in a particular way. The blowup scenarios from the periodic case remain but are augmented with a new option: the blowup of
\min\{{\lVert\boldsymbol{u}(s)\rVert_{L^{q}},\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)\rVert_{L^{r}}}\}\to\infty
when
\limsup_{s\to\pm\infty}\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}<\infty
, which indicates a sort of delocalization of both the velocity and free surface functions. We included this new option for purely technical reasons stemming from the a priori estimates developed in Proposition
5.7
; in principle, it might be possible to rule out this scenario by improving this proposition, but at the moment we lack the tools to do so.

1.4.4.
Discussion of techniques

We now turn to a discussion of our techniques for proving the main theorems. At the highest level of abstraction, our general methods are the same in the periodic and solitary cases; what differs between the cases is the precise reformulation of (
1.8
) and the specific functional framework. The main goal in reformulating the equations is to
semilinearize
them, which we take to mean rewrite abstractly as

(1.21)

P\Xi=N(\Xi)+\kappa F(\Xi)

for an unknown
\Xi
,
P
a linear differential operator,
N
a nonlinear map acting at lower order than
P
, and
F
given data also acting at lower order. We aim to do this in such a way that the operator
P
is invertible, which then allows for the abstract fixed-point reformulation

(1.22)

(I+\mathcal{K})(\Xi)=\Xi+\mathcal{K}(\Xi)=\kappa\mathcal{F}(\Xi)\text{ for }\mathcal{K}=P^{-1}\circ N\text{ and }\mathcal{F}=P^{-1}\circ F.

The global implicit function theorems (GIFT) we are aware of all require some form of Fredholm condition on the derivative of the nonlinear map, and the
I+\mathcal{K}
functional form above is convenient for verifying this by checking that
\mathcal{K}
is a compact map. Moreover, this form lets us readily eliminate a number of the alternatives provided by the particular GIFT we use (see Theorem
5.1
). The specific forms of
P
and
N
(and thus
\mathcal{K}
) differ in the periodic and solitary cases for a number of technical reasons, which we now discuss.

In both cases we assume that the fluid depth
1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}
is everywhere positive, which is convenient in that it permits us to arrive at systems equivalent to (
1.8
) by dividing each equation by the fluid depth. Once this is done for the momentum equation, one notices that the highest order term for
\boldsymbol{u}
is
\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}\boldsymbol{u}
, while the highest order term for
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is
-\Delta\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}
. As these are both
linear
, we have successfully semilinearized the momentum equation. Unfortunately, this procedure does not so nicely semilinearize the continuity equation. The problem is that, while the top order contribution is indeed the linear term
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}
, the function space in which the equation must hold is a subset of the range of the divergence and hence is not closed under multiplication by smooth functions. Fortunately, in the periodic case there is a simple workaround for this issue: in Lemma
3.3
we establish that the continuity equation of (
1.8
) holds if and only if

(1.23)

\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}+P_{0}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}))=0,

where
P_{0}
is the projection onto the space of mean-zero functions. Equation (
1.23
) is a valid semilinear reformulation of the continuity equation that respects the target space. Unfortunately, in the solitary case the projection operator
P_{0}
is not even defined in our functional framework, so the above does not admit an analogous reformulation.

To semilinearize in the solitary case, we instead take an alternate route that does not start with dividing the equations by the depth, but rather changes the unknowns. Instead of considering the tangential velocity
\boldsymbol{u}
as the fundamental unknown we swap to the vector field
u=(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\boldsymbol{u}
, which is sometimes called the ‘discharge’ in the shallow water literature. Then the continuity equation greatly simplifies to
\nabla\cdot u=0
, which we can encode into the domain function space for
u
by restricting to the closed subspace of solenoidal fields. Upon turning our attention to the momentum equation in (
1.8
), we see that the ‘depth dependent viscosity’ nonlinearity in the highest order term for
\boldsymbol{u}
, namely
(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}\boldsymbol{u}
crucially semilinearizes under the discharge swap to
\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u
. The remaining issue is that the highest order term for
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is manifestly nonlinear:
-(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\Delta\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}
. To semilinearize this capillary contribution, we now exploit a feature of the solitary case that is not reflected in the periodic setting: the function space for the free surface is a Banach algebra. This permits us to make the
nonlinear
but invertible change of unknown

(1.24)

\eta=((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})^{2}-(1+\beta)^{2})/2

and reformulate the free surface contributions in terms of the new unknown
\eta
. The upshot is that, up to lower order terms, the aforementioned capillary contribution transforms into the
linear
expression
-\Delta\nabla\eta
.

The above reformulations provide the semilinear operator encoding for both cases of the system (
1.8
) as in (
1.21
). We then select
P
to be the entirety of the linear part so that
DN(0)=0
, meaning that
N
is an at least quadratic and lower order nonlinearity. By implementing Fourier multiplier and Fredholm techniques, we establish the invertibility of the operator
P
, which achieves the compact perturbation of the identity form as in (
1.22
). After selecting an appropriate functional framework, we are then in a position to read off the small data well-posedness results of Theorems
1
and
3
. These follow from a direct application of the implicit function theorem, the hypotheses of which are easily verified thanks to the identity (
1.22
). Note that the small data theory does not require compactness of
\mathcal{K}
or even that it is lower order; all we need is that
\mathcal{K}
is at least quadratic.

We now turn our attention to the issue of how to select a functional framework appropriate for use in the abstract semilinear operator formulation. The equations have a natural energy structure that suggests working in
L^{2}-
based Sobolev spaces. The problem with this is that the equations fail to provide good control of the low-Fourier-mode part of the free surface function; indeed, the best one can hope for is control over its gradient. In the periodic setting this issue is readily dispatched with the Poincaré inequality by forcing
\boldsymbol{\eta}
to have vanishing average. On the other hand, in the solitary setting there is no such simple resolution, and the lack of control of the low-mode part of
\eta
leads to disastrous problems: for example, the lack of
L^{\infty}
bounds in the standard
L^{2}-
theory for
\eta
would make the PDE coefficients unbounded or possibly degenerate, and the natural container space for
\eta
fails to be complete, which negates the ability to use implicit function theorem techniques. We are thus forced to abandon the idea of working in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-based spaces entirely. Instead, we turn to function spaces that provide stronger control at spatial infinity than the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
norm. Our solution in this paper is to work in
weighted
Sobolev spaces based on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mathrm{m}_{1})
where
\mathrm{m}_{1}
is the absolutely continuous measure with density
\langle X\rangle
. These weighted spaces still maintain a deep connection to the natural energy structure and make certain Fourier analytic techniques available. In principle, the low mode degeneracy could also be overcome by working in unweighted
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-based Sobolev spaces for
1<p<2
; however, the weighted
L^{2}
spaces have additional technical advantages that we crucially exploit in the next component of our analysis, which makes them an indispensable tool for our use here.

As previously mentioned, we rely heavily on the compactness of
\mathcal{K}
(and also
\mathcal{F}
) to construct large solutions to (
1.8
) in both the periodic and solitary cases. In the periodic case compactness comes from the Rellich–Kondrachov theorem and the fact that
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{F}
are lower order operators. In the solitary case Rellich-Kondrachov compactness is unavailable due to the unboundedness of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and translation invariance of standard Sobolev norms. However, weighted Sobolev spaces enjoy a weaker form of the compactness theorem that suffices for our purposes. The full details of this result are found in Proposition
2.8
, but a rough summary is: the embedding
W^{k_{1},r}_{\delta_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow W^{k_{0},r}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is compact whenever the regularity and weight strength indices satisfy
k_{1}>k_{0}
and
\delta_{1}>\delta_{0}
. In other words, compactness is achieved by paying both in regularity and localization. It turns out that the nonlinear structure of
N
(and hence
\mathcal{K}
) actually helps make this payment in localization since the product of weighted Sobolev functions actually obeys a
stronger
weighted condition. For example, whenever
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\in[1,\infty]
satisfy
k>2/r
and
\delta,\rho\geqslant 0
the product map
W^{k,r}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times W^{k,r}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to W^{k,r}_{\delta+\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is continuous (see Remark
2.9
). Thus, the map
\mathcal{K}
, which is at least quadratic, is naturally more localized than its argument. Crucially, this is what makes it possible for us to invoke the modified compactness theorem and deduce that
\mathcal{K}
is compact in the solitary setting, and the compactness of
\mathcal{F}
follows similarly.

With the abstract form (
1.22
) established with
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{F}
compact, we are then in position to apply a GIFT. Luckily, in our case the maps
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{F}
turn out to be real analytic, which permits the application of a particularly strong form of the GIFT, Theorem
5.1
, which is a minor modification of a GIFT proved by Chen, Walsh, and Wheeler
[
14
]
. This provides a maximal locally analytic curve of solutions, parametrized by the continuous map
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(\Xi(s),\kappa(s))
, to the fixed point reformulation (
1.22
) with
0\mapsto(0,0)
. This curve is either a closed loop, or else for each of the limits
s\to\pm\infty
one of the following terminal scenarios occurs:
(1)
blowup,
(2)
loss of compactness, or
(3)
loss of Fredholm index zero. The uniqueness of the trivial solution, which is essentially established via (
1.10
), prohibits the closed loop scenario. In turn, the compactness of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{F}
allows us to rule out alternatives
(2)
and
(3)
, showing that blowup must occur. More precisely, this means that as
s\to\pm\infty
, either the solution curve approaches the boundary of the domain of
\Xi
or
|\kappa(s)|+\lVert\Xi(s)\rVert\to\infty
.

The final thrust in completing the large solution analysis of Theorems
2
and
4
is transforming the solution curves
s\mapsto(\Xi(s),\kappa(s))
to the abstract reformulation (
1.22
) back into solution curves
s\mapsto(\boldsymbol{u}(s),\boldsymbol{\eta}(s),\kappa(s))
for the system (
1.8
) with (
1.11
) and extracting refined information on their blowup. The latter is accomplished with a host of a priori estimates for the full nonlinear system (
1.8
), which allow us to conclude that blowup occurs due to growth in a coarser quantity (see (
1.15
) and (
1.20
)) than the norm used to define the function spaces. The discrepancy in the blowup quantities between the periodic and stationary cases stems from the fact that a priori estimates in weighted Sobolev spaces are more delicate than the simple energy estimates available in the periodic setting.

The blowup of each quantity in (
1.15
) and (
1.20
) admits a distinct physical interpretation. The blowup
|\kappa(s)|\to\infty
indicates that for the given forcing profile, solutions exist regardless of the forcing amplitude. The blowup
1/\inf(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}(s))\to\infty
means the free surface gets arbitrarily close to touching the bottom bathymetry profile. The blowup of the remaining quantity indicates that specific integral norms (involving no derivatives) of the solution grow arbitrarily large.

1.4.5.
Outline

After providing our notational conventions in Section
1.5
, the remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
develops tools for using weighted Sobolev spaces (Section
2.1
) and the weighted gradient Sobolev spaces (Section
2.2
). Section
3
studies the linear operator
P
in the semilinearization (
1.21
) for the periodic and solitary cases in tandem. Section
4
works out the details of the semilinearization. Section
5
applies the analytic global implicit function theorem (Section
5.1
), produces large solutions, and refines the blowup scenarios in the periodic (Section
5.2
) and solitary (Section
5.3
) cases separately. Section
6
records the proofs of the main theorems. The remainder of the paper is appendices. In Appendix
A
we record useful tools from analysis. Appendix
B
contains the details of the derivation of the shallow water equations from the free boundary Navier-Stokes system over general bathymetry.

1.5.
Conventions of notation

We write
\mathbb{N}=\{{0,1,2,\dots}\}
for the naturals,
\mathbb{N}^{+}=\mathbb{N}\setminus\{{0}\}
, and
\mathbb{R}^{+}=(0,\infty)
. The notation
\mathfrak{a}\lesssim\mathfrak{b}
indicates that there exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on the parameters mentioned in context, for which
\mathfrak{a}\leqslant C\mathfrak{b}
; to add emphasis on the dependence of
C
on one or more particular parameters
\mathfrak{X},\dots,\mathfrak{Y}
, we will write
\mathfrak{a}\lesssim_{\mathfrak{X},\dots,\mathfrak{Y}}\mathfrak{b}
. The expression of the equivalence of quantities
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}
is written
\mathfrak{a}\asymp\mathfrak{b}
and means that both
\mathfrak{a}\lesssim\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{b}\lesssim\mathfrak{a}
are true. For
d\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
the bracket is the function
\langle\cdot\rangle:\mathbb{C}^{d}\to[1,\infty)
with action given by

(1.25)

\langle x\rangle=(1+\char 12\relax{x_{1}}^{2}+\cdots+\char 12\relax{x_{d}}^{2})^{1/2}\text{ for }x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{C}^{d}.

If
\mathfrak{f}:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
, we denote its image by
\mathrm{img}(\mathfrak{f})=\{{\mathfrak{f}(\mathfrak{a})\in\mathfrak{B}\;:\;\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{A}}\}
. If
E
is a subset of a topological space, the closure of
E
is written
\overline{E}
. The Fourier transform and its inverse on the space of tempered distributions
\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, which are normalized to be unitary on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, are denoted by
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{-1}
, respectively. For functions
\chi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
we write
\chi(D)
to be the Fourier multiplication operator with symbol
\xi\mapsto\chi(\xi)
.

The
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-gradient is denoted by
\nabla=(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{d})
and the divergence of a vector field
U:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\nabla\cdot U=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{j}(U\cdot e_{j})
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{{\infty}\}
we let
C^{k}(\mathfrak{A};\mathfrak{B})
denote the set of
k
-times continuously differentiable functions on
\mathfrak{A}
with values in
\mathfrak{B}
.

2.
Analysis in weighted spaces

The purpose of this section is to introduce two types of weighted spaces that play a role in our analysis of system (
1.8
) in solitary function spaces. These are the weighted Sobolev spaces and the weighted gradient spaces. The benefits of working in weighted spaces are two-fold. Firstly, weighted estimates on the gradient of the free surface overcome the low mode degeneracies present in the equations (as discussed in Section
1.4.4
) and provide global control in the supremum norm. Secondly, the product of functions belonging to weighted spaces has better decay at infinity than either of the factors; this leads us to important compactness in the mapping properties of our nonlinearities.

We are including this concise development of material here for the readers’ convenience. However, we are not asserting that any of the content in this section will be a surprise to experts in the area and hence such readers are encouraged to skip directly to the following section.

2.1.
Weighted Sobolev spaces

Let us begin with the definition of the weighted Sobolev spaces of functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Recall that our notation is that
X:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the identity map.

Definition 2.1

(Weighted Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional real or complex vector space. Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in[0,\infty)
, and
p\in[1,\infty]
. We define

(2.1)

W^{s,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)=\{{f\in W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\;:\;\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f\in W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)}\},\quad\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}_{\delta}}=\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f\rVert_{W^{s,p}}.

When
s=0
we shall write
L^{p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
in place of
W^{0,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
and if
p=2
, we write
H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
in place of
W^{s,2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
.

Remark 2.2

(Completeness)
.

The linear mapping
W^{s,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\ni f\mapsto\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f\in W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
is an isometric isomorphism and so the weighted Sobolev spaces inherit completeness. Moreover, by the observation that this mapping preserves the collection of smooth functions with compact support, we readily deduce density of this space in
W^{s,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
whenever
p<\infty
.

The main cases of interest in studying the weighted Sobolev spaces (for our application) are when
p\in\{{2,\infty}\}
, but we shall occasionally make use of the broader scale. Our first two results of this subsection study equivalent norms.

Proposition 2.3

(Equivalent norm on the weighted Sobolev spaces)
.

Suppose that
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in[0,\infty)
, and
p\in[1,\infty]
. For all
f\in W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
it holds that
f\in W^{s,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
if and only if for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
|\alpha|\leqslant s
we have
\partial^{\alpha}f\in L^{p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
; moreover, the following norm equivalence holds

(2.2)

\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}_{\delta}}\asymp\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert^{p}_{L^{p}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/p}

with the obvious modifications when
p=\infty
and with implicit constants depending only on the dimension,
\delta
,
p
, and
s
. Moreover, if
\varepsilon>0
then we have the continuous embedding
W^{s,p}_{\delta+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\hookrightarrow W^{s,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
.

Proof.

The continuous embedding
L^{p}_{\delta+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\hookrightarrow L^{p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
is trivial, which means that the second assertion follows immediately from the first. We will now prove the first assertion.

Suppose initially that
f\in W^{s,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
. We shall show that
\partial^{\alpha}f\in L^{p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
and the ‘
\gtrsim
’ bound in (
2.2
) holds for all
|\alpha|\leqslant s
by employing a finite induction. For
k\in\{{0,\dots,s}\}
let
\mathbb{P}(k)
denote the proposition that

(2.3)

\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant k}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert_{L^{p}_{\delta}}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}_{\delta}}

for an implicit constant depending only on
k
,
\delta
,
p
, and the dimension. The truth of the base case
\mathbb{P}(0)
is straightforward since

(2.4)

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}_{\delta}}=\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f\rVert_{L^{p}}\leqslant\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f\rVert_{W^{s,p}}=\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}_{\delta}}.

Now let us fix
k\in\{{0,\dots,s-1}\}
and assume that
\mathbb{P}(k)
holds. As
s\geqslant k+1
, we have that

(2.5)

\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|=k+1}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}(\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f)\rVert_{L^{p}}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}_{\delta}}.

For any
|\alpha|=k+1
we have by the Leibniz rule and the triangle inequality

(2.6)

\lVert\partial^{\alpha}(\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f)\rVert_{L^{p}}\geqslant\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\delta}\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert_{L^{p}}-\sum_{\beta<\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\lVert\partial^{\alpha-\beta}(\langle X\rangle^{\delta})\partial^{\beta}f\rVert_{L^{p}}.

The weight
\langle X\rangle^{\delta}
has the property that
|\partial^{\gamma}(\langle X\rangle^{\delta})|\leqslant C_{\gamma}\langle X\rangle^{\delta-|\gamma|}\leqslant C_{\gamma}\langle X\rangle^{\delta}
and hence from (
2.6
) we deduce that for some
C
depending on
k
and
d

(2.7)

\lVert\partial^{\alpha}(\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f)\rVert_{L^{p}_{\delta}}\geqslant\lVert\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert_{L^{p}_{\delta}}-C\sum_{\beta<\alpha}\lVert\partial^{\beta}f\rVert_{L^{p}_{\delta}}.

We combine (
2.5
) and (
2.7
) with the induction hypothesis to deduce

(2.8)

c\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|=k+1}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert^{p}_{L^{p}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/p}-C\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}}\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}}

for some suitably universal
c,C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. This shows that
\mathbb{P}(k+1)
is true, and so finite induction shows that
\mathbb{P}(s)
is true, as desired.

We now prove the opposite inequality, assuming that
f\in W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
satisfies
\partial^{\alpha}f\in L^{p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
for every
|\alpha|\leqslant s
. Then, thanks to the triangle inequality and the Leibniz rules we have

(2.9)

\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f\rVert_{W^{s,p}}\lesssim\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\sum_{\beta\leqslant\alpha}\lVert\partial^{\alpha-\beta}(\langle X\rangle^{\delta})\partial^{\beta}f\rVert_{L^{p}}.

Again we use the properties of the weights mentioned above to deduce
|\partial^{\alpha-\beta}(\langle X\rangle^{\delta})|\lesssim\langle X\rangle^{\delta}
and hence the ‘
\lesssim
’ inequality in (
2.2
) is established and so too is the inclusion
f\in W^{s,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
.
∎

When
p=2
the weighted Sobolev spaces of Definition
2.1
are Hilbert and based on
L^{2}
. Therefore, Fourier methods can be expected to play a significant role. Our next result gives a particularly useful choice of inner product and norm for Fourier analytic tasks. In order to state the following result, we need to recall a choice of inner product on non integer indexed Sobolev spaces.

Definition 2.4

(A choice of Sobolev inner product)
.

Let
V
be a complex and finite dimensional inner product space and
\delta\in[0,\infty)
. We define the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H^{\delta}}^{\star}
on
H^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
via

(2.10)

\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{\delta}}^{\star}=\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\langle\partial^{\alpha}f(x),\partial^{\beta}g(x)\rangle_{V}+\sigma\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{\langle\partial^{\alpha}f(x+h)-\partial^{\alpha}f(x),\partial^{\alpha}g(x+h)-\partial^{\alpha}g(x)\rangle_{V}}{|h|^{d+2\sigma}}\;\mathrm{d}h\Big)\;\mathrm{d}x

where
\delta=m+\sigma
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in[0,1)
. For more information on Besov spaces and non-integer order Sobolev spaces (and their norms) we refer the reader to Leoni
[
37
,
38
]
, specifically Chapter 17 in the former and Part 2 in the latter.

Remark 2.5

(Pseudolocality)
.

The inner product from Definition
2.4
has the property that if
f,g\in H^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
satisfy
\mathrm{dist}(\mathrm{supp}(f),\mathrm{supp}(g))>2
, then
\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{\delta}}^{\star}=0
.

Proposition 2.6

(Equivalent norm and inner product on weighted square summable Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional complex inner product space and suppose that
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in[0,\infty)
. The following hold.

(1)

For
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
we have that
f\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
if and only if for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
|\alpha|\leqslant s
we have
\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f]\in H^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
; moreover we have the norm equivalence

(2.11)

\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\asymp\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\lVert\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f]\rVert^{2}_{H^{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}

for implicit constants depending only on the dimension,
s
, and
\delta
.

(2)

H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
is Hilbert and the following defines an inner product

(2.12)

\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{s}_{\delta}}^{{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}=\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\langle\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f],\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}g]\rangle^{\star}_{H^{\delta}}

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H^{\delta}}^{\star}
is given in Definition
2.4
.

(3)

If
f,g\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
satisfy
\mathrm{dist}(\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[f],\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[g])>2
, then
\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{s}_{\delta}}^{{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}=0
.

Proof.

It suffices to justify the first item, since the second and third items will then follow by Definition
2.4
and Remark
2.5
. Thanks to Proposition
2.3
we are assured that

(2.13)

\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\asymp\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}.

Now by the Fourier space characterization of Sobolev spaces, we know that

(2.14)

\mathscr{F}:L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\to H^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)

is a bounded linear isomorphism and so the claimed norm equivalence in (
2.11
) now follows.
∎

Next, we study products of weighted Sobolev functions.

Proposition 2.7

(Weighted space product estimates)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
. Suppose that
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
\delta,\rho\in[0,\infty)
, and
1\leqslant p_{0},p_{1},q_{0},q_{1},t\leqslant\infty
satisfy (
A.47
). Let
f_{0}\in(L^{q_{0}}_{\rho}\cap W^{s,p_{0}}_{\rho})(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
and
f_{1}\in(L^{q_{1}}_{\delta}\cap W^{s,p_{1}}_{\delta})(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
. Then, the pointwise product
f_{0}f_{1}
belongs to
W^{s,t}_{\rho+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
and obeys the estimate

(2.15)

\lVert f_{0}f_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,t}_{\rho+\delta}}\lesssim\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{L^{q_{0}}_{\rho}}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,p_{1}}_{\delta}}+\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{W^{s,p_{0}}_{\rho}}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{L^{q_{1}}_{\delta}}.

for an implicit constant depending only on
d
,
s
,
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
,
q_{0}
,
q_{1}
,
t
,
\rho
, and
\delta
.

Proof.

This is a simple application of Theorem
A.8
on the product
\langle X\rangle^{\rho+\delta}f_{0}f_{1}=(\langle X\rangle^{\rho}f_{0})(\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f_{1})
.
∎

To conclude this subsection, we record a number of useful embedding results.

Proposition 2.8

(Embeddings of weighted Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional real or complex vector space,
p_{0},p_{1}\in[1,\infty]
,
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}\in[0,\infty)
satisfy
p_{1}\leqslant p_{0}
,
s_{0}\leqslant s_{1}
, and
\delta_{0}\leqslant\delta_{1}
. Further suppose that

(2.16)

\frac{1}{p_{1}}-\frac{s_{1}-s_{0}}{d}\leqslant\frac{1}{p_{0}},\text{ and }\frac{1}{p_{1}}-\frac{s_{1}-s_{0}}{d}<0\text{ if }(p_{0},p_{1})\in\{{\infty}\}\times[1,\infty).

Then the following hold.

(1)

We have the continuous embedding
W^{s_{1},p_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\hookrightarrow W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
.

(2)

If
\delta_{0}<\delta_{1}
and the left hand inequality in (
2.16
) is strict, then the embedding from the previous item is compact.

Proof.

If
p_{0}=p_{1}
then the embedding
W^{s_{1},p_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\hookrightarrow W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
is a trivial consequence of Proposition
2.3
. Note that if
s_{0}=s_{1}
and the hypotheses inequalities are satisfied then necesarily we have
p_{0}=p_{1}
. So it remains to consider the case that
p_{1}<p_{0}
and
s_{0}<s_{1}
. In this case we observe that if
Y
is a Banach space such that
W^{s_{1},p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\hookrightarrow Y
, then the injective map

(2.17)

W^{s_{1},p_{1}}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\ni f\mapsto\langle X\rangle^{\delta}f\in Y

is continuous.

The embedding from the first item then follows by combining observation (
2.17
), interpolation, Proposition
2.3
, the Morrey inequality when
1/p_{1}-(s_{1}-s_{0})/d<0
and
p_{0}\leqslant\infty
, the Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev inequality when
p_{0}<\infty
and
1/p_{1}-(s_{1}-s_{0})/d>0
, and the critical Sobolev embedding when
1/p_{1}-(s_{1}-s_{0})/d=0
and
p_{0}<\infty
. This completes the proof of the first item.

We now turn to the proof of the second item, which requires strict inequality on the left of (
2.16
). Note that this requires
s_{0}<s_{1}
, as
s_{0}=s_{1}
in the inequality implies that
p_{0}<p_{1}
, which is not allowed.

To prove the second item it suffices to show that the closed unit ball
B\subset W^{s_{1},p_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
is totally bounded in
W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
, i.e. that for every
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
we have that

(2.18)

B\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}B_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}}(f_{i},\varepsilon)\text{ for some }m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\text{ and }f_{1},\dotsc,f_{m}\in W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V).

To this end, fix
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(B(0,2))
satisfy
0\leqslant\varphi\leqslant 1
and
\varphi=1
on
\overline{B(0,1)}
. For
R\in[1,\infty)
we write
\varphi_{R}(x)=\varphi(x/R)
.

If
f\in B
then we may use the first item to estimate

(2.19)

\lVert(1-\varphi_{R})f\rVert_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}}\lesssim\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\delta_{0}-\delta_{1}}(1-\varphi_{R})\rVert_{W^{s_{0},\infty}}\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{1}}}\\
\lesssim\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\delta_{0}-\delta_{1}}(1-\varphi_{R})\rVert_{W^{s_{0},\infty}}\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s_{1},p_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}}\lesssim R^{\delta_{0}-\delta_{1}}\leqslant\varepsilon/3,

provided that
R\gtrsim 1+(1/\varepsilon)^{1/(\delta_{1}-\delta_{0})}
. Assume that
R
is fixed and satisfies this condition, and define the set

(2.20)

F=\{{\varphi_{R}f|_{B(0,2R)}\;:\;f\in B}\}\subset W^{s_{1},p_{1}}_{[0]}(B(0,2R);V)=\overline{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(B(0,2R);V)},

where closure on the right is with respect to the
W^{s_{1},p_{1}}(B(0,2R);V)
norm. Note that the nonstandard subscript
[0]
on the right hand side is meant to distinguish the notation for this standard space from our weighted space notation. We have the trivial bound

(2.21)

\sup_{f\in F}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{s_{1},p_{1}}_{[0]}}\lesssim 1,

and the Rellich-Kondrachov theorem assures that the embedding
W^{s_{1},p_{1}}_{[0]}(B(0,2R);V)\hookrightarrow W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{[0]}(B(0,2R);V)
is compact, which together imply that the set
F
is totally bounded in the latter space. Therefore, if we let
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
denote the embedding constant for
W_{[0]}^{s_{0},p_{0}}(B(0,2R);V)\hookrightarrow W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
(where the subspace identification is made via zero extension, which by minor abuse of notation we do not indicate with an operator) then we have the existence of
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and functions
\{{g_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset F
such that

(2.22)

F\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}B_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{[0]}}(g_{i},\varepsilon/3C)\hookrightarrow\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}B_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}}(g_{i},\varepsilon/3).

By definition, for each
i\in\{{1,\dots,m}\}
there exists
f_{i}\in B
such that
g_{i}=\varphi_{R}f_{i}|_{B(0,2R)}
, which in turn means the zero extension of
g_{i}
is
\varphi_{R}f_{i}
.

Now let
f\in B
. By (
2.22
) there exists
i\in\{{1,\dots,m}\}
such that
\varphi_{R}f\in B_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}}(\varphi_{R}f_{i},\varepsilon/3)
. Using this and the tail estimates of (
2.19
), we may then bound

(2.23)

\lVert f-f_{i}\rVert_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}}\leqslant\lVert\varphi_{R}f-\varphi_{R}f_{i}\rVert_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}}+\lVert(1-\varphi_{R})f\rVert_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}}+\lVert(1-\varphi_{R})f_{i}\rVert_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}}<\varepsilon/3+\varepsilon/3+\varepsilon/3=\varepsilon.

Thus, the covering (
2.18
) is established, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 2.9

(Algebra property of weighted spaces)
.

By combining Propositions
2.7
and
2.8
we find that the pointwise product map

(2.24)

W^{s,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})\times W^{s,p}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})\ni(f,g)\mapsto fg\in W^{s,p}_{\delta+\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})

is continuous whenever
s>d/p
,
\delta,\rho\geqslant 0
.

2.2.
Weighted gradient Sobolev spaces

This subsection analyzes the functional framework for the free surface unknown in the solitary case of (
1.8
). Note that in contrast with Section
2.1
, we are specializing here to dimension
d=2
and integrability exponent
p=2
.

Definition 2.10

(Weighted gradient Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
. Given
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\delta\in(0,1)
we define

(2.25)

\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})=\{{f\in L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\;:\;\nabla f\in H^{s-1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})}\},\quad\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}=\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}.

Our first task is to establish completeness of the weighted gradient Sobolev spaces. The main tool that helps us here is the following weighted estimate for the Riesz potential operator.

Lemma 2.11

(Weighted estimates for Riesz potential operators)
.

Let

(2.26)

\mathcal{I}_{1}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{f(x-y)}{2\pi|y|}\;\mathrm{d}y

denote the Riesz potential operator of order
1
and let
\delta\in(0,1)
,
0\leqslant\widetilde{\delta}<\delta
, and
q=2(\delta-\widetilde{\delta})^{-1}
. Then

(2.27)

\mathcal{I}_{1}:L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\to L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}).

is a bounded linear operator.

Proof.

The claimed operator bound is a special consequence of the more general estimates of Proposition 2.5 in Duarte and Silva
[
21
]
. There the authors establish that the fractional integration operators
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}:L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}:v)\to L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}:w)
(where these are Lebesgue spaces over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
equipped with the absolutely continuous measures
v\;\mathrm{d}x
and
w\;\mathrm{d}x
respectively) are bounded whenever
1<p\leqslant q<\infty
,
0<\alpha<d
,
(v,w)\in A^{\alpha}_{p,q}
, and
v^{1/(p-1)},\;w\in A_{\infty}
(with
A^{\alpha}_{p,q}
and
A_{\infty}
denoting certain classes of measures satisfying Muckenhoupt-type conditions).

We invoke this result in the special case of
d=2
,
\alpha=1
,
p=2
,
q=2(\delta-\widetilde{\delta})^{-1}
,
v=\langle X\rangle^{2\delta}
, and
w=\langle X\rangle^{q\widetilde{\delta}}
(so that
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}:v)=L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2}:w)=L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
). We are assured that the hypothesis
(v,w)\in A^{1}_{2,q}
is satisfied thanks to Lemma 3.3 in
[
21
]
, where Duarte and Silva characterize which inhomogeneous power type measures belong to
A^{\alpha}_{p,q}
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

Thanks to the computations in Chapter 4 Section B in Folland
[
23
]
we may identify the convolution operator
\mathcal{I}_{1}
from Lemma
2.11
with the Fourier multiplication operator
|\nabla|^{-1}
. As a result we have the operator identity
\mathcal{I}_{1}\nabla=\mathcal{R}
where
\mathcal{R}=(\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2})
is the vector of Riesz transforms.

With the weighted estimates for Riesz potentials in hand, we now prove completeness.

Proposition 2.13

(Completeness of the weighted gradient spaces)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. The following hold.

(1)

We have the embedding
\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
.

(2)

\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is complete.

Proof.

If
f\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
, then
\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}
. Now we apply the Riesz potential operator to
\nabla f
and use Lemma
2.11
(with
\widetilde{\delta}=0
) and the fact that
\mathcal{I}_{1}\nabla=\mathcal{R}
(see Remark
2.12
). This gives
\lVert\mathcal{R}f\rVert_{L^{2/\delta}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}
. Now we use the identity
\mathcal{R}\cdot\mathcal{R}=-I
and apply
\mathcal{R}\cdot
to
\mathcal{R}f
and use the boundedness of Riesz transforms on
L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
to conclude the proof of the first item.

Now suppose that
\{{f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is Cauchy. Thanks to the embedding of the first item and the completeness of
L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
we are assured of the existence of
f\in L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
for which
f_{n}\to f
as
n\to\infty
in the norm on
L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
. On the other hand, thanks to the definition of the norm on
\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
, we also know that the sequence
\{{\nabla f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{s-1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})
is Cauchy. Remark
2.2
establishes completeness of this weighted Sobolev space and so there exists
g\in H^{s-1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})
such that
\nabla f_{n}\to g
as
n\to\infty
in this norm. By taking the limits in the sense of distributions, we find that
\nabla f=g
and so
f\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
. Finally, we conclude by noting that
\lVert f-f_{n}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}=\lVert g-\nabla f_{n}\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
∎

Our next tasks are to develop an important equivalent norm on the weighted gradient spaces and analyze refined low mode embeddings.

Proposition 2.14

(Equivalent norms and low mode embeddings)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(B(0,2))
be a radial function satisfying
\varphi=1
on
\overline{B(0,1)}
. The following hold for
f\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
.

(1)

\nabla\varphi(D)f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})
and
(1-\varphi(D))f\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
; moreover, we have the norm equivalence

(2.28)

\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\asymp(\lVert\varphi(D)\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}^{2}+\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{\delta}})^{1/2}

for implicit constants depending only on
s
,
\delta
, and
\varphi
.

(2)

Given
\widetilde{\delta}\in[0,\delta)
and
q=2(\delta-\widetilde{\delta})^{-1}
we have that
\varphi(D)f\in W^{\infty,q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
; moreover, for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
we have the estimate

(2.29)

\lVert\varphi(D)f\rVert_{W^{t,q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\delta}},

for an implicit constant depending only on
\delta
,
\widetilde{\delta}
,
t
, and
\varphi
.

Proof.

Let us begin by proving ‘
\gtrsim
’ in (
2.28
). For
j\in\{{1,2}\}
we define the multipliers

(2.30)

m_{j}(\xi)=(1-\varphi(\xi))\frac{\xi_{j}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}|\xi|^{2}}\quad\text{which satisfy}\quad\sum_{j=1}^{2}m_{j}(D)\partial_{j}=(1-\varphi(D)).

We use Proposition
A.16
, the fact that As
[m_{j}]_{1,-1}<\infty
(see (
A.79
)), and Remark
A.14
) to deduce the estimate

(2.31)

\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{2}\lVert m_{j}(D)\partial_{j}f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}=\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}.

On the other hand, the low mode estimate is even easier, since we need only use the first item of Proposition
A.11
followed by Proposition
2.8
to deduce that

(2.32)

\lVert\varphi(D)\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}=\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}.

Next, we turn our attention to the ‘
\lesssim
’ inequality of (
2.28
). We begin with the triangle inequality

(2.33)

\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\leqslant\lVert\nabla\varphi(D)f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}+\lVert(1-\varphi(D))\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}.

For the low mode piece above we need only look to the second item of Proposition
A.3
, which permits the estimate
\lVert\nabla\varphi(D)f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}
. For the high mode piece in (
2.33
) we instead quote the boundedness of
\nabla:H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\to H^{s-1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})
from Proposition
A.16
and Remark
A.14
. This completes the proof of the first item.

We now prove the second item. We first establish the estimate (
2.29
) with
t=0
. Combining the weighted bounds for Riesz potential operators from Lemma
2.11
with the first item, we find that

(2.34)

\lVert\mathcal{I}_{1}\nabla\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\delta}}.

Now, by arguing as in the proof of Proposition
2.13
we know that
\mathcal{I}_{1}\nabla=\mathcal{R}
. We again would like to use
\mathcal{R}\cdot\mathcal{R}+I=0
to then relate the above estimate to one on
\varphi(D)f
itself. To achieve this we require an auxiliary result on the boundedness of the Riesz transform operators on certain weighted Lebesgue spaces. Namely, we claim that

(2.35)

\mathcal{R}_{j}:L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\to L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\text{ is a bounded linear operator for }j\in\{{1,2}\}.

To establish the claim we first note that
L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2}:\langle X\rangle^{q\widetilde{\delta}})
