, where the space on the right is the Lebesgue space equipped with the measure
\langle X\rangle^{q\widetilde{\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}x
. As a consequence of the first corollary in Section 4.2 in Chapter 5 of Stein
[
59
]
, we have that (
2.35
) holds provided that
\langle X\rangle^{q\widetilde{\delta}}\in A_{q}
, where
A_{q}
denotes the space of Muckenhoupt measures. Thanks to the first equation in Section 3.2 in Duarte and Silva
[
21
]
, we deduce that this condition holds as soon as
\widetilde{\delta}<2(1-1/q)
, but this latter condition is indeed satisfied since
\widetilde{\delta}<1
and
q>2
by hypothesis. Therefore, the claim (
2.35
) holds, and together with (
2.34
) provides the estimate

(2.36)

\lVert\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}=\lVert\mathcal{R}\cdot\mathcal{R}\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert\mathcal{I}_{1}\nabla\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\delta}}.

We now establish estimate (
2.29
) for
t>0
. Let
\widetilde{\varphi}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(B(0,4))
be another radial function such that
\widetilde{\varphi}=1
on
\overline{B(0,2)}
. Notice that
\varphi(D)f=\widetilde{\varphi}(D)\varphi(D)f=K\ast(\varphi(D)f)
where
K=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[\widetilde{\varphi}]
. Since
\widetilde{\varphi}
is a Schwartz function, we have that
K\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
(and all of its derivatives are Schwartz as well). We then deduce that
\varphi(D)f
is smooth and for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
we have
\partial^{\alpha}\varphi(D)f=(\partial^{\alpha}K)\ast(\varphi(D)f)
. For any
\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{F})
the linear map
L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\ni g\mapsto g\ast\psi\in L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is easily seen to be continuous, and so we deduce (using also (
2.36
)) that
\lVert\partial^{\alpha}\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\delta}}
for an implicit constant depending on
K
,
\delta
,
\widetilde{\delta}
,
q
, and
\alpha
. Estimate (
2.29
) now follows after heeding to Proposition
2.3
.
∎

Remark 2.15

(Embedding of the weighted Sobolev spaces)
.

By using the equivalent norm from the first item of Proposition
2.14
and the multiplier estimate from the first item of Proposition
A.11
it is straightforward to deduce the embedding
H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
.

We now provide a weighted gradient space analog of Proposition
2.8
.

Proposition 2.16

(Inclusion relations)
.

Let
s,s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\delta,\delta_{0},\delta_{1}\in(0,1)
. The following hold.

(1)

If
s_{0}\leqslant s_{1}
and
\delta_{0}\leqslant\delta_{1}
then
\widetilde{H}^{s_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{s_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
.

(2)

If
s_{0}<s_{1}
and
\delta_{0}<\delta_{1}
then the embedding from the first item is compact.

(3)

If
s\geqslant 2
and
r\in[2,\infty)
the embedding
\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is compact.

Proof.

The first item follows from the inclusions established in Proposition
2.3
. We now prove the second item. Suppose that
\{{f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\widetilde{H}^{s_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is a bounded sequence. Thanks to the definition of the norm on this space we have that the sequence of gradients
\{{\nabla f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{s_{1}-1}_{\delta_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})
is also bounded; moreover, from the first item and Proposition
2.13
we have that
\{{f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset L^{2/\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is also a bounded sequence. Due to the reflexivity of this Lebesgue space and Proposition
2.8
we are assured the existence of
f\in L^{2/\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
and
g\in H^{s_{0}-1}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
with the property that (along a subsequence we neglect to relabel)

(2.37)

f_{n}\rightharpoonup f\;\text{in }L^{2/\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla f_{n}\to g\;\text{in }H^{s_{0}-1}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})

as
n\to\infty
, where the former convergence is weak and the latter is strong. By considering distributional limits, we deduce that
g=\nabla f
and so
f\in\widetilde{H}^{s_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
. The strong convergence in (
2.37
) paired with the definition of the norm on
\widetilde{H}^{s_{0}}_{\delta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
implies that
f_{n}\to f
strongly in this space as
n\to\infty
.

We now prove the third item. Let
\{{f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
be a bounded sequence. We split into a low mode and low mode compliment sequence with
\varphi
as in Proposition
2.14
. By this result (and also the second item of Proposition
A.11
) we find then that the sequences
\{{\varphi(D)f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{\delta/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
,
\{{\nabla\varphi(D)f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})
, and
\{{(1-\varphi(D))f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
are all bounded. We have that the embeddings
W^{1,\infty}_{\delta/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
,
H^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
are all compact by Proposition
2.8
; therefore, we can extract a (non-labeled) subsequence with
\{{\varphi(D)f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
Cauchy in
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
and
\{{(1-\varphi(D))f_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
Cauchy in
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
. By summing and passing to the limit, we obtain the sought after compact embedding.
∎

We next record an important density result.

Proposition 2.17

(Density of functions of bounded support)
.

For
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
we have that
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\subset\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is dense.

Proof.

Fix
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
radial with
\mathrm{supp}(\varphi)\subset B(0,2)
and
\varphi=1
on
\overline{B(0,1)}
. We begin by arguing that the subspace of functions of bounded support is dense. To this end, fix
f\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
. For any
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
we may use Propositions
2.7
and
2.14
to deduce that the pointwise product
\psi f
belongs to
\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
, as

(2.38)

\lVert\psi f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\leqslant\lVert\psi\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}+\lVert\varphi(D)f\nabla\psi\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}+\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\nabla\psi\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert\psi\rVert_{W^{s-1,\infty}}\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}\\
+\lVert\varphi(D)f\rVert_{W^{s-1,2/\delta}}\lVert\nabla\psi\rVert_{W^{s-1,2/(1-\delta)}_{\delta}}+\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}\lVert\nabla\psi\rVert_{W^{s-1,\infty}}\lesssim_{\psi}\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}.

Now, given
R\in[1,\infty)
we take
\psi=\varphi_{R}
, where
\varphi_{R}=\varphi(x/R)
. The above shows that
\varphi_{R}f\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
and is a function of bounded support for every
R
. Now we shall study the convergence of
\varphi_{R}f
to the function
f
as
R\to\infty
. Initially, we have

(2.39)

\lVert f-\varphi_{R}f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\leqslant\lVert(1-\varphi_{R})\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}+\lVert\varphi(D)f\nabla\varphi_{R}\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}+\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\nabla\varphi_{R}\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}.

From the support conditions on
\varphi
, we have
\nabla\varphi_{R}=(1-\varphi_{R/2})\nabla\varphi_{R}
and so we can give this additional factor to the
f
contributions in the penultimate term above. We then use the product estimates of Proposition
2.7
with the norm equivalence of the first item of Proposition
2.14
and the bounds

(2.40)

\lVert\nabla\varphi_{R}\rVert_{W^{s-1,2/(1-\delta)}_{\delta}}\lesssim_{\varphi}1\text{ and }\lVert\nabla\varphi_{R}\rVert_{W^{s-1,\infty}}\lesssim_{\varphi}R^{-1}

to deduce that

(2.41)

\lVert f-\varphi_{R}f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert(1-\varphi_{R})\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}+\lVert(1-\varphi_{R/2})\varphi(D)f\rVert_{W^{s-1,2/\delta}}+R^{-1}\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}.

Now we have an expression in which the vanishing limit as
R\to\infty
is clear, since
f\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
implies that
\nabla f\in H^{s-1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})
and (by the second item of Proposition
2.14
)
\varphi(D)f\in W^{s-1,2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
. The density of functions of bounded support now follows.

We conclude by showing the density of smooth and compactly supported functions. Given any
f\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
and an
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the above argument grants us the existence of
g\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
such that
g
has bounded support and
\lVert f-g\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}<\varepsilon/2
. For any
\mu>0
we then let
g_{\mu}=g\ast\upvarphi_{\mu}
, with
\upvarphi_{\mu}(x)=(\mu^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi)^{-1}\varphi(x/\mu)
denoting the standard sequence of mollifiers generated by
\varphi
. Since
g\in L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
by the first item of Proposition
2.13
, we know that
g_{\mu}\in L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
as well. Moreover, the compactness of the supports of
\varphi
and
g
implies that
g_{\mu}
also has compact support. Finally, since
\nabla g_{\mu}=(\nabla g)\ast\upvarphi_{\mu}
and
\nabla g\in H^{s-1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})
, standard properties of mollification operators imply that
\nabla g_{\mu}\to\nabla g
in
H^{s-1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})
as
\mu\to 0
. In particular,
g_{\mu}\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
and there exists
\upmu>0
such that
\lVert g-g_{\upmu}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}<\varepsilon/2
. It then follows that
\lVert f-g_{\upmu}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}<\varepsilon
.
∎

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to nonlinear analysis in the weighted gradient spaces.

Proposition 2.18

(Product estimates in weighted gradient spaces)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
. The following hold.

(1)

Given any
\widetilde{\delta}\in[0,\delta)
we have the embedding
\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
.

(2)

\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is a Banach algebra under pointwise multiplication. In fact, for any
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\max\{{\delta,\rho}\}\leqslant\mu<\min\{{1,\rho+\delta}\}
the pointwise product map

(2.42)

\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\times\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\ni(f,g)\mapsto fg\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})

is continuous.

(3)

Given any
\widetilde{\delta}\in[0,\delta)
and
\rho\in[0,\infty)
the pointwise product map

(2.43)

\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\times H^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\ni(f,g)\mapsto fg\in H^{s}_{\rho+\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})

is continuous.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(B(0,2))
be a radial function with
\varphi=1
on
\overline{B(0,1)}
. The first item follows from Propositions
2.8
and
2.14
:

(2.44)

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\leqslant\lVert\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}+\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi(D)f\rVert_{W^{2,q}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}+\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\rVert_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}},

where we have taken
q=2(\delta-\widetilde{\delta})^{-1}
. This gives the first item.

Next, we shall prove the third item - so fix
f\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
and
g\in H^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
. We split
f
into low and high modes and invoke Proposition
2.7
(and Remark
2.9
) to bound

(2.45)

\lVert fg\rVert_{H^{s}_{\rho+\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi(D)f\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lVert g\rVert_{H^{s}_{\rho}}+\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lVert g\rVert_{H^{s}_{\rho}}.

Then, by Propositions
2.14
and
2.8
we have

(2.46)

\lVert\varphi(D)f\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\delta}},\quad\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}.

Synthesize (
2.45
) and (
2.46
) to get (
2.43
).

Finally, we prove the second item. We shall frequently use Remark
2.15
. For
f\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
and
g\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
it holds

(2.47)

\lVert fg\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi(D)fg\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}}+\lVert(1-\varphi(D))fg\rVert_{H^{s}_{\mu}}.

The final term in (
2.47
) can be given a suitable upper bound by using the continuity of (
2.43
) combined with Proposition
2.8
and the first item of Proposition
2.14
:

(2.48)

\lVert(1-\varphi(D))fg\rVert_{H^{s}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lVert g\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\lVert g\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}}.

On the other hand, for the penultimate term of (
2.47
) we split
g
into high and low modes and apply the same argument as (
2.48
) to the high mode piece

(2.49)

\lVert\varphi(D)fg\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi(D)f\varphi(D)g\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}}+\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\lVert g\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}}.

We are thus left considering the product of the low mode pieces. Thanks to the second item of Proposition
A.11
, the fact that
\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[\varphi(D)f\varphi(D)g]\subset B(0,4)
, Hölder’s inequality, and the embedding from the first item we have

(2.50)

\lVert\varphi(D)f\varphi(D)g\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi(D)f\nabla\varphi(D)g\rVert_{L^{2}_{\mu}}+\lVert\nabla\varphi(D)f\varphi(D)g\rVert_{L^{2}_{\mu}}\\
\lesssim\lVert\varphi(D)f,\varphi(D)g\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}\times L^{\infty}_{\widetilde{\rho}}}\lVert\varphi(D)f,\varphi(D)g\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\delta}\times\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\lVert g\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}}

where
\widetilde{\delta}\in(0,\delta)
and
\widetilde{\rho}\in(0,\rho)
satisfy
\mu\leqslant\min\{{\widetilde{\delta}+\rho,\delta+\widetilde{\rho}}\}
. This completes the proof.
∎

For technical convenience when dealing with analytic composition, we would like to make a minimal addition to the weighted gradient Sobolev spaces in order to obtain a unital Banach algebra. Therefore, we introduce the following variation on Definition
2.10
.

Definition 2.19

(Extended weighted gradient Sobolev spaces)
.

Given
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
we define

(2.51)

{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})=\{{f\in\mathbb{F}+L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\;:\;\nabla f\in H^{s-1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F}^{2})}\},\quad\lVert f\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}=(|\mathbf{c}(f)|^{2}+\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{s-1}_{\delta}}^{2})^{1/2}

where
\mathbf{c}:\mathbb{F}+L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\to\mathbb{F}
is the unique function satisfying
f-\mathbf{c}(f)\in L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
for all
f\in\mathbb{F}+L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
. When
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
we shall write
{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in place of
{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
.

Basic properties of the extended weighted gradient spaces are enumerated in the next result.

Proposition 2.20

(Properties of extended weighted gradient spaces)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. The following hold.

(1)

The map

(2.52)

{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\ni f\mapsto(\mathbf{c}(f),f-\mathbf{c}(f))\in\mathbb{F}\times\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})

is a linear isometric isomorphism.

(2)

{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is a Banach space and
\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
as a codimension 1 subspace.

(3)

If
s\geqslant 2
, then
{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
.

(4)

If
s\geqslant 2
, then
{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
is a unital Banach algebra where the constant function
1
is the unit; moreover for all
f,g\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{F})
we have
\mathbf{c}(fg)=\mathbf{c}(f)\mathbf{c}(g)
.

Proof.

The first and second items are trivial given Definition
2.10
and Proposition
2.13
. Similarly, the third and fourth items follow immediately from Proposition
2.18
.
∎

The final result of this subsection computes the spectrum of elements of the extended weighted gradient spaces and records mapping properties of the resolvent.

Proposition 2.21

(Admissibility of the extended weighted gradient spaces)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2
,
\delta\in(0,1)
. The unital Banach algebra
{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
is admissible in the sense of Definition
A.3
.

Proof.

The third and fourth items of Proposition
2.20
show that the first item of Definition
A.3
is satisfied. Now it is elementary using these embeddings to deduce that for all
f\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
the inclusion
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{spec}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)}
we omit the details for the sake of brevity.

We shall fix
f\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
and
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)}
and prove (via induction on
s
) that the pointwise inverse
(z-f)^{-1}
belongs to
\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
and there exists a locally bounded function
\mathbf{C}_{s}:[0,\infty)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
increasing in both arguments such that we may estimate

(2.53)

\lVert(z-f)^{-1}\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\leqslant\mathbf{C}_{s}(\mathrm{dist}_{\mathbb{C}}(\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)},z)^{-1},\lVert f\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}).

The base case is
s=2
. Set
\varepsilon=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathbb{C}}(\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)},z)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Firstly we calculate that
\lVert(z-f)^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
and
\lVert(z-f)^{-1}-(z-\mathbf{c}(f))^{-1}\rVert_{L^{2/\delta}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-2}\lVert f-\mathbf{c}(f)\rVert_{L^{2/\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}\lVert f-\mathbf{c}(f)\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}}
(thanks to the first item of Proposition
2.13
). So to prove that
(z-f)^{-1}\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
(with the correct form of the estimates), we need only estimate
\nabla((z-f)^{-1})
in the space
H^{1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2})
- which reduces to estimating the following three inclusions for all
i,j\in\{{1,2}\}
:

(2.54)

(z-f)^{-2}\partial_{i}f,\;(z-f)^{-2}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f,\;(z-f)^{-3}\partial_{i}f\partial_{j}f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C}).

The first two of these are very simple to estimate (using implicitly Proposition
2.3
):

(2.55)

\lVert(z-f)^{-1}\partial_{i}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}+\lVert(z-f)^{-2}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2}\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{H^{1}_{\delta}}=\varepsilon^{-2}\lVert f\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}}.

The final inclusion of (
2.54
) is a tad more complicated to estimate. We shall use the identity

(2.56)

\partial_{i}f\partial_{j}f=\partial_{i}\partial_{j}(f^{2}/2)-f\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f

and reduce to estimating the resulting two terms. For the former we use again Proposition
2.3
and also the fourth item of Proposition
2.20
,

(2.57)

\lVert(z-f)^{-3}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}(f^{2}/2)\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-3}\lVert f^{2}\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-3}\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}}.

For the latter inclusion and estimate of (
2.56
) we also invoke the third item of Proposition
2.20
:

(2.58)

\lVert(z-f)^{-3}f\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-3}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert f\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-3}\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}.

By synthesizing the above information and estimates we indeed find that
(z-f)^{-1}\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
with
\mathbf{c}((z-f)^{-1})=(z-\mathbf{c}(f))^{-1}
and with the resolvent estimate

(2.59)

\lVert(z-f)^{-1}\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}+\varepsilon^{-2}\lVert f\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}}+\varepsilon^{-3}\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}}

with implicit constants depending only on
\delta
. This obeys the necessary structure of (
2.53
) and hence the base case is established.

Now let us fix
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2
and suppose the induction hypotheses and estimate (
2.53
) hold at regularity index
s
; we endeavor to prove the same for
s+1
. So let
f\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s+1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
and
z\not\in\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)}
. The previous argument has already established that
(z-f)^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}+L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
and therefore we need only verify that
\nabla((z-f)^{-1})\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2})
with a structured estimate. Given
i\in\{{1,2}\}
we check that

(2.60)

\partial_{i}((z-f)^{-1})=((z-f)^{-2}-(z-\mathbf{c}(f))^{-2})\partial_{i}f+(z-\mathbf{c}(f))^{-2}\partial_{i}f.

By the induction hypothesis, the fourth item of Proposition
2.20
, and Definition
2.19
we know that
(z-f)^{-2}-(z-\mathbf{c}(f))^{-2}\in\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
. Hence for the first term on the right hand side of (
2.60
) we may invoke the third item of Proposition
2.18
, the fourth item of Proposition
2.20
, and the induction hypothesis to estimate

(2.61)

\lVert((z-f)^{-2}-(z-\mathbf{c}(f))^{-2})\partial_{i}f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert(z-f)^{-1}\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}}^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s+1}_{\delta}}\leqslant\mathbf{C}_{s}(\mathrm{dist}_{\mathbb{C}}(\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)},z)^{-1},\lVert f\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}})^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{s+1}_{\delta}}.

For the final term in (
2.60
) there is nothing to do since
(z-\mathbf{c}(f))^{-2}\in\mathbb{C}
and so a correctly structured estimate is clearly obtainable. Synthesizing the above estimates shows that the structured bound (
2.53
) with
s
replaced by
s+1
holds for
\mathbf{C}_{s+1}
given by

(2.62)

\mathbf{C}_{s+1}(\theta,\phi)=\theta+c_{0}(\mathbf{C}_{s}(\theta,c_{1}\phi)^{2}+\theta^{2})\phi

for a constants
c_{0},c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
depending only on
s
and
\delta
. It is easily verified that
\mathbf{C}_{s+1}
is locally bounded and increasing in both arguments. The induction, and with it the proof, is complete.
∎

3.
Linear analysis

This section analyzes a trivial forcing linearization of system (
1.8
) in both periodic and solitary function spaces. Our strategy is to decouple the bathymetric contributions as a compact remainder from the main translation invariant linear part. We then establish well-posedness of this latter part through energy arguments and multiplier theorems. The contribution of the bathymetric part is then accounted for with Fredholm theory.

3.1.
Principal part analysis

In this subsection we study the linear system of equations

(3.1)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot u=\psi,\\
Au-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u+(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta=\phi,\end{cases}

for the unknown vector and scalar fields
u:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\eta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
with the data scalar
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and vector
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Here
A,G\geqslant 0
are parameters, and we shall always assume
A>0
and we take
G>0
as well in the solitary case. The analysis of this subsection corresponds in a sense to the linearization of system (
1.8
) with trivial bathymetry.

Our first result establishes the well-posedness of system (
3.1
) in spaces of periodic functions.

Proposition 3.1

(Principal part analysis - periodic case)
.

Assume that
A>0
,
G\geqslant 0
, and
s\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
(\psi,\phi)\in\mathring{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{T}_{L}^{2})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}_{L}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
there exists a unique
(u,\eta)\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{T}_{L}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{T}_{L}^{2})
such that system (
3.1
) is satisfied.

Proof.

Let us first establish uniqueness. Due to the linearity of the equations, it is sufficient to prove that if
(\psi,\phi)=0
then
(u,\eta)=0
. We achieve this through a simple integration by parts argument. By testing the second equation in (
3.1
) with
u
in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{L}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
inner product and integrating by parts, we see that

(3.2)

0=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}(Au-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u+(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta)\cdot u=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}A|u|^{2}+2^{-1}\char 12\relax{\nabla u+\nabla u^{\mathrm{t}}}^{2}+2\char 12\relax{\nabla\cdot u}^{2}\geqslant A\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}|u|^{2}.

As
A>0
, we learn that
u=0
. Now the second equation in (
3.1
) reduces to
(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta=0
. Since
G\geqslant 0
and
\mathscr{F}[\eta](0)=0
, we deduce that
\eta=0
.

Now we verify the existence of solutions. Given
(\psi,\phi)\in\mathring{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
we define
\eta\in\mathring{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
and
u\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
according to

(3.3)

\eta=(G-\Delta)^{-1}\Delta^{-1}(\nabla\cdot\phi-(A-4\Delta)\psi)

and

(3.4)

u=(A-\Delta)^{-1}(\phi+3\nabla\psi-(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta).

We compute

(3.5)

\nabla\cdot u=(A-\Delta)^{-1}(3\Delta\psi+(A-4\Delta)\psi)=\psi,

which verifies that the first equation in (
3.1
) is satisfied. Next, we compute

(3.6)

\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u=\Delta u+3\nabla\nabla\cdot u=\Delta u+3\nabla\psi.

Therefore,

(3.7)

Au-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u=(A-\Delta)u-3\nabla\psi=\phi-(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta

so the second equation in (
3.1
) is satisfied as well. Existence has been verified, and with that the proof is complete.
∎

Our next result establishes the well-posedness of system (
3.1
) when
\psi=0
in spaces of functions whose members vanish at infinity. The first equation in (
3.1
) is just the linear constraint that the velocity vector has vanishing divergence or, equivalently, is
solenoidal
. It is therefore convenient (both here and in what follows) to encode this condition into the function spaces via the introduction of the notation

(3.8)

{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})=\{{u\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\;:\;\nabla\cdot u=0}\},\quad s\in\mathbb{N},\quad\delta\geqslant 0

and focus just on the second equation in (
3.1
).

The proof, while in spirit is the same as that of Proposition
3.1
, is complicated by the fact that we are essentially barred by the subsequent nonlinear analysis from using the standard
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces. We instead establish well-posedness in weighted
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces as to ensure enough control is gained over the free surface unknown. The reader is referred to Sections
2.1
and
2.2
for the definitions and properties of the weighed Sobolev and weighted gradient Sobolev spaces, respectively.

Proposition 3.2

(Principal part analysis - solitary case)
.

Assume that
A,G>0
,
s\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
. For each
\phi\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
there exists a unique
(u,\eta)\in{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{3+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that system (
3.1
) is satisfied with
\psi=0
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
3.1
, we begin by establishing uniqueness of solutions; assume that system (
3.1
) is satisfied by
(u,\eta)\in{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\phi=0
and
\psi=0
. Our plan is to establish the analog of (
3.2
), but now we have to be slightly more careful as
\eta\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in general. By testing the equation with
u
and integrating by parts we again derive the identity

(3.9)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}A|u|^{2}+2^{-1}|\nabla u+\nabla u^{\mathrm{t}}|^{2}+2\char 12\relax{\nabla\cdot u}^{2}=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot u\Big|.

Next, we argue that the right hand side of the above vanishes identically. To achieve this we fix
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and employ
Proposition
2.17
to obtain
\widetilde{\eta}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{3+s}_{\delta}}\leqslant\varepsilon
. Then we are free write
\eta=\widetilde{\eta}+(\eta-\widetilde{\eta})
in the right hand side of (
3.9
) and integrate by parts in the
\widetilde{\eta}
term to exploit
\nabla\cdot u=0
. Hence,

(3.10)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot u\Big|\leqslant\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(G-\Delta)\nabla(\eta-\widetilde{\eta})\cdot u\Big|\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}},

and since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary we find that the expression on the left must be zero. In turn, thanks to (
3.9
) we deduce that
u=0
and then
(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta=0
. As
G>0
and
\nabla\eta\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, it follows from Plancherel’s theorem that
\nabla\eta=0
and hence
\eta
is a constant. The first item of Proposition
2.13
shows that
\widetilde{H}^{3+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
embeds into
L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, so it must follow that
\eta=0
.

We now prove the existence of solutions. Fix
\phi\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Our goal is to make sense of the formulas (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) (with
\psi=0
) but in the context of the weighted spaces. The former is a bit trickier and requires a more complicated approach than in the periodic case. We first define the auxiliary function

(3.11)

q=(G-\Delta)^{-1}(\Delta^{-1}\nabla\nabla\cdot\phi)=m_{G}(D)\phi\quad\text{with}\quad m_{G}(\xi)=\frac{1}{G+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\frac{\xi\otimes\xi}{|\xi|^{2}}.

Since
G>0
the (components of the) multiplier
m_{G}
satisfy the hypotheses of Proposition
A.16
with
k=1
and
r=-2
; therefore, we can invoke this result (taking heed of Remark
A.14
) to deduce that
q\in H^{2+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, noting that
q
is real-valued since
\overline{m_{G}(-\xi)}=m_{G}(\xi)
.

We now aim to define
\eta
to be the function in the weighted gradient Sobolev space whose gradient agrees with
q
. Indeed, upon noting
\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathcal{R}\cdot=\Delta^{-1}\nabla\cdot
(see Remark
2.12
) we set

(3.12)

\eta=\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathcal{R}\cdot q.

Due to the embedding
H^{2+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
(proved in Proposition
2.8
), the boundedness of
\mathcal{R}\cdot:L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(proved in Proposition
A.16
and Remark
A.14
), and the boundedness of
\mathcal{I}_{1}:L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(proved in Lemma
2.11
), we see that (
3.12
) defines
\eta\in L^{2/\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Since we already know that
q\in H^{2+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we obtain
\eta\in\widetilde{H}^{3+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as soon as we verify that

(3.13)

\nabla\eta=\Delta^{-1}\nabla\nabla\cdot q=(G-\Delta)^{-1}(\Delta^{-1}\nabla\nabla\cdot)^{2}\phi=q.

Next, we define the velocity according to

(3.14)

u=(A-\Delta)^{-1}(\phi-(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta).

By repeated applications of Proposition
A.16
and Remark
A.14
, together with an observation as above that the relevant symbols preserve realness, we deduce from the inclusions
\phi\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\nabla\eta\in H^{2+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
that
u\in H^{2+s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. It is now a simple calculation to verify that
\nabla\cdot u=0
,
(A-\Delta)u=Au-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u
, and the second equation in (
3.1
) is satisfied. This completes the existence proof.
∎

3.2.
Inclusion of bathymetric remainders

The goal of this subsection is again to study the linearization of system (
1.8
) but, in contrast with Section
3.1
, we shall allow for general bathymetry. The form of this linearization is slightly different in the periodic and solitary cases due to a change of unknowns made in the latter case.

The following lemma is crucial for our analysis of the periodic case. We use the notation
P_{0}f=f-(L_{1}L_{2})^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}f
to denote the projection onto the subspace of functions of vanishing average.

Lemma 3.3

(Divergence equation reformulation)
.

Suppose that
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
f\in(C^{0}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
, and
g\in\mathring{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
solve the equation

(3.15)

\nabla\cdot v+P_{0}(v\cdot\nabla f)=g.

Then

(3.16)

\frac{1}{L_{1}L_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}v\cdot\nabla f=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}e^{f}\Big)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}ge^{f}

Proof.

We compute

(3.17)

\nabla\cdot(e^{f}v)=e^{f}(\nabla\cdot v+v\cdot\nabla f)=e^{f}\Big(g-\frac{1}{L_{1}L_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}v\cdot\nabla f\Big).

The result then follows by integrating over
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
, applying the divergence theorem, and rearranging.
∎

We now arrive at the main linear analysis result for the periodic case.

Proposition 3.4

(Linear analysis with bathymetry - periodic case)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
A>0
,
G\geqslant 0
, and
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
with
\min\beta=0
. The following hold.

(1)

The linear map
\mathcal{P}:H^{2+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to\mathring{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
given via

(3.18)

\mathcal{P}(u,\eta)=(\nabla\cdot u,Au-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u+(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta)

is a bounded linear isomorphism.

(2)

The linear map
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}:H^{1+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{2+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathring{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
given via

(3.19)

\mathcal{L}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=(P_{0}(u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta)),-A\beta(1+\beta)^{-1}u-\mathbb{S}u\nabla\log(1+\beta))

is bounded.

(3)

The map
(\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}):H^{2+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to\mathring{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a bounded linear isomorphism.

Proof.

The first item is an operator-theoretic restatement of Proposition
3.1
.

To prove the second item, we first note that
\beta\geqslant 0
is smooth, and hence
(1+\beta)^{-1}
and
\log(1+\beta)
are smooth functions as well. Using this and simple applications of Theorem
A.8
shows that each term boundedly maps into the stated space.

We now turn to the proof of the third item. The Rellich–Kondrachov theorem implies that the inclusion map
H^{2+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\hookrightarrow H^{1+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{2+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
is compact; from this and the mapping properties established in the second item, we find that
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
restricted to the former domain is a compact operator. We combine this fact with the first item to deduce that

(3.20)

\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}:H^{2+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to\mathring{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})

is a Fredholm operator with index zero; hence, it is a linear isomorphism if and only if
\mathrm{ker}(\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta})=\{{0}\}
.

To complete the proof of the third item, we will now show that
\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
is injective. Suppose that
(u,\eta)\in\mathrm{ker}(\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta})
. Simple algebraic manipulations then reveal that
(u,\eta)
satisfy

(3.21)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot u+P_{0}(u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta))=0,\\
A(1+\beta)^{-1}u+(1+\beta)^{-1}\nabla\cdot((1+\beta)\mathbb{S}u)+(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta=0.\end{cases}

We can use Lemma
3.3
to simplify the first equation in system (
3.21
): we find that
P_{0}(u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta))=u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta)
, and hence the first equation is equivalent to

(3.22)

\nabla\cdot((1+\beta)u)=0.

Therefore, if we test the second equation in (
3.21
) with
(1+\beta)u
and integrate by parts, we learn that

(3.23)

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}A|u|^{2}+(1+\beta)(2^{-1}|\nabla u+\nabla u^{\mathrm{t}}|^{2}+2|\nabla\cdot u|^{2})=0,

and thus
u=0
. In turn, this implies that
(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta=0
, and since
G\geqslant 0
and
\mathscr{F}[\eta](0)=0
we must have
\eta=0
as well, and injectivity is proved.
∎

To conclude this section we give the main result on the linear analysis in the solitary case. Recall the definition of the extended weighted gradient spaces (and the function
\mathbf{c}
) from Definition
2.19
and the notation for the space of solenoidal vector fields introduced in (
3.8
). We shall also use the following quadratic form notation for
m\in\{{1,2}\}
:

(3.24)

Q_{m}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}},\quad Q_{m}(v,w)=m(v\cdot w)I+v\otimes w+w\otimes v,\text{ and }Q_{m}(v)=Q_{m}(v,v).

Proposition 3.5

(Linear analysis with bathymetry - solitary case)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
A,G>0
,
\delta,\rho,\mu\in(0,1)
satisfy
\rho-\delta<\mu<\rho
, and let
\beta\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\inf\beta=0
. The following hold.

(1)

The linear map
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}:{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2+s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{3+s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to H^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
given via

(3.25)

\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=A_{\beta}u-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u+(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta,\text{ for }A_{\beta}=\frac{A}{1+\mathbf{c}(\beta)},

is a bounded linear isomorphism.

(2)

The linear map
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}:H^{1+s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{2+s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to H^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
given via

(3.26)

\mathcal{L}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=-\frac{A(\beta-\mathbf{c}(\beta))}{(1+\beta)(1+\mathbf{c}(\beta))}u+\nabla\cdot Q_{2}(\nabla\log(1+\beta),u)\\
-((1+\beta)^{-1}(G-\Delta)\nabla(1+\beta))\eta+\nabla\cdot Q_{1}(\nabla(1+\beta),\nabla((1+\beta)^{-1}\eta))

is well-defined and continuous.

(3)

The map
(\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}):{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2+s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{3+s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to H^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a bounded linear isomorphism.

Proof.

A simple application of Proposition
2.3
reveals that the mapping (
3.25
) is well-defined and continuous. This map is invertible thanks to Proposition
3.2
, so the first item is proved.

To prove the second item we begin by recording some facts. By combining the first and second items of Proposition
2.14
with Proposition
2.8
, we deduce the continuous embedding
\widetilde{H}^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow W^{\infty,\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
\widetilde{\delta}\in[0,\delta)
. On the other hand, since
\beta\geqslant 0
we combine Proposition
2.21
with Corollary
A.6
to deduce the inclusions
\log(1+\beta),(1+\beta)^{-1},\beta(1+\beta)^{-1}\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The first item of Proposition
2.20
implies that
\beta-\mathbf{c}(\beta)\in\widetilde{H}^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, while its fourth item tells us that
\widetilde{H}_{\delta}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\subset{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is an ideal.

There are four terms in the expression (
3.26
) for
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
that we need to estimate. The first two of these can be felled with the aforementioned facts and Proposition
2.7
. Indeed (taking
\widetilde{\delta}=\rho-\mu<\delta
):

(3.27)

\Big\lVert\frac{A(\beta-\mathbf{c}(\beta))}{(1+\beta)(1+\mathbf{c}(\beta))}u\Big\rVert_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\lesssim\Big\lVert\frac{A(\beta-\mathbf{c}(\beta))}{(1+\beta)(1+\mathbf{c}(\beta))}\Big\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{s}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{s}_{\mu}}

and

(3.28)

\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{2}(\nabla\log(1+\beta),u)\rVert_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\log(1+\beta)\rVert_{W^{1+s,\infty}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1+s}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1+s}_{\mu}}.

To estimate the third term in the expression for
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
we split
\eta=\varphi(D)\eta+(1-\varphi(D))\eta
with a frequency cut-off function as in Proposition
2.14
. For the low mode contribution, we use that
\varphi(D)\eta\in W^{\infty,\infty}_{\widetilde{\mu}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(with
\max\{{0,\rho-\delta}\}<\widetilde{\mu}<\mu
) and
\nabla\beta\in H^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

(3.29)

\lVert((1+\beta)^{-1}(G-\Delta)\nabla(1+\beta))\varphi(D)\eta\rVert_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert(1+\beta)^{-1}\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}}\lVert(G-\Delta)\nabla\beta\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lVert\varphi(D)\eta\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}_{\widetilde{\mu}}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2+s}_{\mu}}.

For the high mode contribution we instead take
\nabla\beta\in W^{s,\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
(1-\varphi(D))\eta\in H^{2+s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, which grants us the estimate

(3.30)

\lVert((1+\beta)^{-1}(G-\Delta)\nabla(1+\beta))(1-\varphi(D))\eta\rVert_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert(1+\beta)^{-1}\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}}\lVert(G-\Delta)\nabla\beta\rVert_{W^{s,\infty}_{\delta}}\lVert(1-\varphi(D))\eta\rVert_{H^{s}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2+s}_{\mu}}.

In order to estimate the final term in (
3.26
) we argue as follows. By the second item of Proposition
2.18
we get

(3.31)

\lVert(1+\beta)^{-1}\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2+s}_{\mu}}\lesssim(|(1+\mathbf{c}(\beta))^{-1}|+\lVert(1+\beta)^{-1}-(1+\mathbf{c}(\beta))^{-1}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2+s}_{\delta}})\lVert\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2+s}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2+s}_{\mu}}.

In turn, we may then bound

(3.32)

\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{1}(\nabla(1+\beta),\nabla((1+\beta)^{-1}\eta))\rVert_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\beta\rVert_{W^{1+s,\infty}_{\delta}}\lVert\nabla((1+\beta)^{-1}\eta)\rVert_{H^{1+s}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2+s}_{\mu}}.

By combining the above estimates, we then find that
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
obeys the mapping properties stated in the second item.

Finally, to prove the third item we will use Fredholm theory as in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. Due to Propositions
2.8
and
2.16
, the inclusion map

(3.33)

H^{2+s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{3+s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow H^{1+s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{2+s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

is compact and so from the mapping properties of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
established in the second item we find that the restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
to the space on the left of (
3.33
) is a compact linear operator. Therefore, the sum
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
is a Fredholm operator of index zero (as
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}
is invertible by the first item), which means that
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
is invertible if and only if it has trivial kernel. Suppose then that
(u,\eta)\in\mathrm{ker}(\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta})
. The following auxiliary identities hold:

(3.34)

A_{\beta}-\frac{A(\beta-\mathbf{c}(\beta))}{(1+\beta)(1+\mathbf{c}(\beta))}=\frac{A}{1+\beta},

(3.35)

-\mathbb{S}u+Q_{2}(\nabla\log(1+\beta),u)=(1+\beta)\mathbb{S}((1+\beta)^{-1}u),

and for
\alpha=1+\beta
and
\zeta=(1+\beta)^{-1}\eta
:

(3.36)

\Delta\nabla\eta=\Delta\nabla(\alpha\zeta)=\alpha\Delta\nabla\zeta+\zeta\Delta\nabla\alpha+\nabla\cdot Q_{1}(\nabla\alpha,\nabla\zeta).

Thus,
(\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta})(u,\eta)=0
if and only if

(3.37)

A(1+\beta)^{-1}u-\nabla\cdot((1+\beta)\mathbb{S}((1+\beta)^{-1}u))+(1+\beta)(G-\Delta)\nabla((1+\beta)^{-1}\eta)=0,

but the identity
\nabla\cdot u=0
is built into the domain of
u
. We then test the above equation with
(1+\beta)^{-1}u
and integrate by parts to deduce that

(3.38)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}A|(1+\beta)^{-1}u|^{2}\leqslant\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(G-\Delta)\nabla((1+\beta)^{-1}\eta)\cdot u\Big|.

We next use the inclusion
(1+\beta)^{-1}\eta\in\widetilde{H}_{\rho}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the same density argument employed in the proof of Proposition
3.2
to deduce that the right hand side of (
3.38
) vanishes. In turn, since
A>0
we learn that
u=0
and
(G-\Delta)\nabla((1+\beta)^{-1}\eta)=0
. Since
G>0
and
\nabla((1+\beta)^{-1}\eta)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we then learn that
(1+\beta)^{-1}\eta
is a constant function that also belongs to
L^{2/\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and so must be trivial. This completes the proof that
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
is injective, and hence the proof of the third item.
∎

4.
Semilinearization

The goal of this section is to show that the system (
1.8
) with (
1.11
) is equivalent to a modified system of equations that are semilinear in the sense that the equations are the sum of a linear part and a lower order nonlinear remainder. As it turns out, this linear part is the one we have already studied in Section
3.2
. Once this is established, we make yet another reformulation of the equations that will be useful in our subsequent analysis. By inverting the linear part of the semilinearization, we obtain a representation of solutions as zeros of a compact perturbation of the identity map on an appropriate Banach space.

4.1.
Domains and forcing

The purpose of this subsection is to introduce open sets for the collection of free surfaces in both the periodic and solitary cases. We then encode the forcing function (
1.11
) within these spaces and verify basic mapping properties.

Lemma 4.1

(Free surface domain - periodic case)
.

Let
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
with
\min\beta=0
. The following hold.

(1)

The sets
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}
and, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}
given by

(4.1)

\mathbf{O}_{\beta}=\{{\eta\in C^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\;:\;\min(1+\beta+\eta)>0}\},\quad\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}=\{{\eta\in\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\;:\;\min(1+\beta+\eta)>\varepsilon}\}

are open in
C^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
and satisfy
\bigcup_{0<\varepsilon<1}\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{O}_{\beta}
.

(2)

Given any
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2
, the sets
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
and
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
are open in
\mathring{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
.

(3)

The sets
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
and
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
are open in
(C^{0}\cap\mathring{H}^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
.

Proof.

The first item and third items are elementary. The second item follows from the fact that
s>1
allows for the supercritical Sobolev embedding
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\hookrightarrow C^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
.
∎

In the following result we let
\Upomega
denote the composition operator of Corollary
A.5
.

Proposition 4.2

(Data map - periodic case)
.

Let
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
with
\min\beta=0
and set
a_{\beta}=1+\max\beta
. Fix a trio of (vector valued) real analytic mappings

(4.2)

\phi:(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\psi:(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}),\quad\tau:(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to(L^{\infty}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}).

The following hold.

(1)

The forcing map
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
given via

(4.3)

\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta)=(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}(\Upomega_{\phi}(\eta)+\nabla((1+\beta+\eta)\Upomega_{\psi}(\eta))+\Upomega_{\tau}(\eta)\nabla\eta)

is well-defined and real analytic.

(2)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restriction
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
maps bounded sets to bounded sets.

(3)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2
the restriction
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}:\mathbf{O}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
maps bounded sets to precompact sets.

Proof.

Proposition
A.9
guarantees that the unital Banach algebra
(C^{0}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{C})
is admissible in the sense of Definition
A.3
; consequently, we may apply Corollary
A.5
to learn that that the maps

(4.4)

\Upomega_{\phi}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\Upomega_{\psi}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}),\\
\Upomega_{\tau}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to(L^{\infty}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}})

are all well-defined and real analytic, and that their restrictions to
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
all map bounded sets to bounded sets. On the other hand, due to Corollary
A.6
, the mappings

(4.5)

\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\ni\eta\mapsto(1+\beta+\eta),\;(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\in(C^{0}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{C})

are also well-defined and real analytic, with their restrictions to
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
mapping bounded sets to bounded sets.

The map
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
of (
4.3
) combines the maps of (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) (and
\nabla\eta
) via simple products, which can been handled with Theorem
A.8
. Thus,
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
is well-defined and real analytic. The same argument also shows that the second item holds.

Finally, we prove the third item. Thanks to the Rellich–Kondrachov theorem, the inclusion map
\mathring{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\hookrightarrow(C^{0}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
is compact, from which it follows that the inclusion map
\mathbf{O}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{O}^{\varepsilon/2}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
maps bounded sets to precompact sets. The second item requires
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
to be at least continuous, so it maps these precompact sets to precompact sets.
∎

We now turn our attention to the solitary case. In contrast with the periodic case, we additionally study an analytic change of unknowns. We remind the reader that the definition of the extended weighted gradient spaces is given in Definition
2.19
.

Lemma 4.3

(Free surface domains - solitary case)
.

Let
\delta,\rho\in(0,1)
and
\beta\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\inf\beta=0
. The following hold.

(1)

The sets
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}
,
\mathbf{U}_{\beta}
, and for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}
given by

(4.6)

\mathbf{O}_{\beta}=\{{\eta\in(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\;:\;\inf(1+\beta+\eta)>0}\},\quad\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}=\{{\eta\in\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\;:\;\inf(1+\beta+\eta)>\varepsilon}\},

and

(4.7)

\mathbf{U}_{\beta}=\{{\eta\in(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\;:\;\inf((1+\beta)^{2}+2\eta)>0}\},\quad\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}=\{{\eta\in\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\;:\;\inf((1+\beta)^{2}+2\eta)>\varepsilon^{2}}\}

are open in
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and satisfy
\bigcup_{0<\varepsilon<1}\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{O}_{\beta}
and
\bigcup_{0<\varepsilon<1}\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{U}_{\beta}
.

(2)

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2
,
r\in[2,\infty]
, and
\mathcal{X}\in\{{\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\}
. The sets
\mathbf{O}\cap\mathcal{X}
,
\mathbf{O}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{X}
,
\mathbf{U}\cap\mathcal{X}
, and
\mathbf{U}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{X}
are all open in
\mathcal{X}
.

(3)

The functions
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\to\mathbf{U}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}:\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\to\mathbf{O}_{\beta}
given by

(4.8)

\mathcal{T}_{\beta}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}((1+\beta+\eta)^{2}-(1+\beta)^{2}),\quad\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}(\eta)=\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta)

are well-defined, real analytic, and mutually inverse; moreover, for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
we have that the restrictions satisfy
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}:\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}
and map bounded sets to bounded sets.

(4)

Let
\mathcal{X}
be one of the spaces from the second item. Then
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathcal{X}\to\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}:\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap\mathcal{X}\to\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathcal{X}
are well-defined and real analytic.

(5)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\mathcal{X}
as in the second item the maps
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}:\mathbf{O}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap\mathcal{X}\to\mathbf{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}:\mathbf{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap\mathcal{X}\to\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{X}
map bounded sets to bounded sets.

Proof.

The first item is trivial. The second item follows from the first and the fact that each choice of
\mathcal{X}
embeds into
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(see the third item of Proposition
2.20
). The third item is an easy consequence of Proposition
A.9
(with
s=0
) and Corollary
A.6
.

For the forth and fifth items we consider the various cases of
\mathcal{X}
separately. The case
\mathcal{X}=(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the easiest. Thanks to Proposition
A.10
we see that
\mathcal{X}
is the real-valued subspace of an admissible unital Banach algebra in the sense of Definition
A.3
; moreover, the hypotheses on
\beta
and
r
ensure that
\beta\in\mathcal{X}
in this case. Therefore, we readily read off the mapping properties of
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}
claimed in the fourth and fifth items for this specific
\mathcal{X}
as a consequence of Corollary
A.6
.

Next, we handle the case
\mathcal{X}=\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We will rewrite
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}
and its inverse
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}
in certain ways to read off the correct mapping properties. For the forward mapping note that we may write

(4.9)

\mathcal{T}_{\beta}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}+(\beta-\mathbf{c}(\beta))\eta+(1+\mathbf{c}(\beta))\eta

where
\mathbf{c}
is the function in Definition
2.19
. As
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}
is a polynomial, real analyticity and the mapping of bounded sets to bounded sets follow as soon as we verify the continuity of each constituent product. The first and second terms on the right hand side of (
4.9
) are handled with the second item of Proposition
2.18
, while the final term is linear.

To study
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}
we write

(4.10)

\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}(\eta)=2(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta))^{-1}\eta.

Let
\mu=\min\{{\delta,\rho}\}\in(0,1)
. By noting the continuity of the product map
{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(which is a consequence of the second item of Proposition
2.18
and the fourth item of Proposition
2.20
) we reduce to proving that

(4.11)

\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ni\eta\mapsto(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta))^{-1}\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

is well-defined, real analytic, and its restriction to
\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, maps bounded sets to bounded sets. Now thanks to Proposition
2.21
we are assured that
{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the real-valued subspace of an admissible unital Banach algebra. This permits repeated applications of Corollary
A.6
to yield the sought after properties.
∎

We now come to the solitary case analog of Proposition
4.2
where again
\Upomega
is the composition operator from Corollary
A.5
.

Proposition 4.4

(Data map - solitary case)
.

Let
\delta,\rho\in(0,1)
,
\beta\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\inf\beta=0
, and set
a_{\beta}=1+\sup\beta
. Fix a trio of (vector valued) real analytic mappings

(4.12)

\phi:(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to L^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\psi:(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to H^{1}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\tau:(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to(L^{\infty}_{\rho}\cap H^{1}_{\rho})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}).

The following hold.

(1)

For any
r\in[2,\infty]
the forcing map
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}:\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
given via

(4.13)

\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta)=\Upomega_{\phi}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}(\eta)+\nabla(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}\Upomega_{\psi}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}(\eta))+\Upomega_{\tau}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}(\eta)\nabla(\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}(\eta))

is well-defined and real analytic.

(2)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restriction
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}:\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
maps bounded sets to bounded sets.

(3)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2
the restriction
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}:\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
maps bounded sets to precompact sets.

Proof.

Due to the properties of the bi-analytic change of unknowns
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}
from the third, fourth, and fifth items of Lemma
4.3
we may reduce to studying the map
\mathcal{G}_{\beta}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
given by

(4.14)

\mathcal{G}_{\beta}(\eta)=\mathcal{F}_{\beta}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\beta}(\eta)=\Upomega_{\phi}(\eta)+\nabla((1+\beta+\eta)\Upomega_{\psi}(\eta))+\Upomega_{\tau}(\eta)\nabla\eta.

Our first claim is that
\mathcal{G}_{\beta}
is well-defined, real analytic, and obeys the restriction bounded sets mapping property. We may proceed with the same strategy as that of Proposition
4.2
. The unital Banach algebra
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
is admissible thanks to Proposition
A.10
and so Corollary
A.5
is in play and tells us that each of the maps
\Upomega_{\phi}
,
\Upomega_{\psi}
, and
\Upomega_{\tau}
are real analytic and for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
they map bounded subsets of
\mathbf{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to bounded subsets of their respective image spaces. The map
\mathcal{G}_{\beta}
then combines these compound Nemytskii operators via simple bounded products.

By using these established properties of
\mathcal{G}_{\beta}
, the identity
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}=\mathcal{G}_{\beta}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}
, and the mapping properties of
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}
from Lemma
4.3
, we readily deduce the first and second items.

The third item is a consequence of the compact embedding
\mathbf{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{s}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{U}^{\varepsilon/2}_{\beta}\cap(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,2})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(which holds due to the third item of Proposition
2.16
) and the first and second items.
∎

4.2.
Operator decomposition

Our goal now is to reformulate the stationary, variable bathymetry, viscous shallow water system (
1.8
) with (
1.11
) as an equation for an operator acting between open subsets of Banach spaces. We then decompose this operator in a semilinear fashion - writing it as the sum of a top order linear part and a properly nonlinear and lower order remainder. For technical reasons, we do not do this in a unified way; rather, the periodic and solitary settings are given slightly different operators and decompositions.

We first discuss the periodic setting. Recall from Lemma
3.3
that the operator
P_{0}
is the projection onto the space of mean zero functions and the periodic data maps
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
are given in Proposition
4.2
.

Proposition 4.5

(Operator formulation - periodic case)
.

Let
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
satisfy
\min\beta=0
,
A>0
,
G\geqslant 0
, and
\kappa\in[0,\infty)
. Consider the mapping
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}:H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})]\to\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined via

(4.15)

\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=(\nabla\cdot u+P_{0}(u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta)),u\cdot\nabla u+A(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}u-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u-\mathbb{S}u\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta)+(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta).

The following hold.

(1)

\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
is well-defined and real-analytic.

(2)

For all
(u,\eta)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})]
the identity
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=(0,\kappa\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta))
holds if and only if system (
1.8
) is satisfied with velocity
\boldsymbol{u}=u
, free surface
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\eta
, and forcing
\Phi=(1+\beta+\eta)\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta)
.

Proof.

The operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
is composed of linear terms and of products of the functions
u
,
\eta
,
\log(1+\beta+\eta)
, and
(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}
, so the first item follows as soon as we verify boundedness of these products and analyticity of the latter two nonlinearities. The combination of Corollary
A.6
and Proposition
A.9
shows that the mappings

(4.16)

\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\in\eta\mapsto(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1},\log(1+\beta+\eta)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})

are real analytic. For the boundedness of the products of
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
, we make repeated use Theorem
A.8
to see that for any
\zeta\in H^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
we have the estimates:

(4.17)

\lVert P_{0}(u\cdot\nabla\zeta)\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\nabla\zeta\rVert_{H^{1}}+\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}\lVert\nabla\zeta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{3}},

(4.18)

\lVert u\cdot\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}}^{2},\quad\lVert\zeta u\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{3}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}},

(4.19)

\lVert\mathbb{S}u\nabla\zeta\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}\lVert\nabla\zeta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{3}}.

This completes the proof of the first item.

We now prove the second item. By using Lemma
3.3
and the bound
\min(1+\beta+\eta)>0
, we see that
\nabla\cdot u+P_{0}(u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta))=0
if and only if
\nabla\cdot u+u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta)=0
if and only if
\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\eta)u)=0
. To check the equivalence in the momentum equation we merely multiply the second component of
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)
by
(1+\beta+\eta)
and then perform algebraic manipulations until the left hand side of system (
1.8
) is revealed.
∎

In the next result we take the operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
of (
4.15
) and write is as the sum of the linear operators
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
from Proposition
3.4
and a compact nonlinear remainder.

Proposition 4.6

(Operator decomposition - periodic case)
.

Let
\beta
,
A
,
G
, and
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
be as in Proposition
4.5
. The following hold.

(1)

The map
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}:W^{1,4}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})]\to\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
given via

(4.20)

\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\Big(P_{0}\Big(u\cdot\nabla\log\Big(1+\frac{\eta}{1+\beta}\Big)\Big),u\cdot\nabla u-\frac{Au\eta}{(1+\beta)(1+\beta+\eta)}-\mathbb{S}u\nabla\log\Big(1+\frac{\eta}{1+\beta}\Big)\Big)

is well-defined and real analytic; moreover,
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(0,0)=0
and
D\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(0,0)=0
.

(2)

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restriction of
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
to
W^{1,4}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})]
maps bounded sets to bounded sets.

(3)

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restriction of
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
to
H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})]
maps bounded sets to precompact sets.

(4)

For all
(u,\eta)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})]
we have the operator decomposition identity

(4.21)

\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\mathcal{P}(u,\eta)+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}(u,\eta)+\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta),

where
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
,
\mathcal{P}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
are the operators given by (
4.15
), (
3.18
), and (
3.19
), respectively.

Proof.

We prove the first and second items in tandem. The nonlinear maps that comprise
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
are simple products of the functions
u
,
\eta
,
(1+\beta)^{-1}
,
(1+\eta+\beta)^{-1}
, and
\log(1+(1+\beta)^{-1}\eta)
. The expressions
(1+\eta+\beta)^{-1}
and
\log(1+(1+\beta)^{-1}\eta)
are real analytic functions of
\eta
valued in
H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
, as can be deduced from Proposition
A.9
and Corollary
A.6
, which also map bounded subsets of
\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
to bounded subsets of
H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
. So given
\zeta\in\mathring{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
and
u\in W^{1,4}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
it remains only to observe the following product bounds (which follow from Theorem
A.8
and the Sobolev embedding)

(4.22)

\lVert P_{0}(u\cdot\nabla\zeta)\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{2}}+\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{2}},

(4.23)

\lVert u\cdot\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{4}}\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\leqslant\lVert u\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,4}},\quad\lVert\zeta u\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\lVert\zeta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{2}}\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}},

(4.24)

\lVert\mathbb{S}u\nabla\zeta\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{2}}.

To complete the proof of the first and second items we note that that the vanishing of
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
and its derivative at the origin follow from elementary calculations.

The third item is a consequence of the previous items and of the compactness of the embedding

(4.25)

H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})]\hookrightarrow W^{1,4}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon/2}\cap\mathring{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})].

The fourth item is another elementary calculation which follows from the identities

(4.26)

\frac{1}{1+\beta}-\frac{\eta}{(1+\beta)(1+\beta+\eta)}=\frac{1}{1+\beta+\eta},\quad\log(1+\beta)+\log(1+(1+\beta)^{-1}\eta)=\log(1+\beta+\eta).

∎

We next turn our attention to the solitary setting and derive analogs of Proposition
4.5
and
4.6
. Recall that the solitary case’s data map
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
is given in Proposition
4.4
. Note also that we are again using the notation for solenoidal vector fields introduced in (
3.8
).

Proposition 4.7

(Operator formulation - solitary case)
.

Let
\delta,\rho\in(0,1)
,
\beta\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\inf\beta=0
,
A,G>0
, and
\kappa\in[0,\infty)
. Consider the mapping
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}:{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})]\to L^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined via

(4.27)

\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\nabla\cdot\Big(\frac{u\otimes u}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}}\Big)+\frac{Au}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}}-\nabla\cdot\Big(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}\mathbb{S}\Big(\frac{u}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}}\Big)\Big)\\
+\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}(G-\Delta)\nabla(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta)).

The following hold.

(1)

\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
is well-defined and real analytic.

(2)

For all
(u,\eta)\in H^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})]
the identity
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\kappa\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta)
holds if and only if system (
1.8
) is satisfied with velocity
\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{u}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}}
, free surface
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta)
, and forcing
\Phi=\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta)
.

Proof.

We observe that
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\mathcal{R}_{\beta}(u,\mathcal{T}_{\beta}^{-1}(\eta))
where
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}
is the analytic change of unknowns mapping from Proposition
4.3
and
\mathcal{R}_{\beta}:{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})]\to L^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the map

(4.28)

\mathcal{R}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\nabla\cdot\Big(\frac{u\otimes u}{1+\beta+\eta}\Big)+\frac{Au}{1+\beta+\eta}-\nabla\cdot\Big((1+\beta+\eta)\mathbb{S}\Big(\frac{u}{1+\beta+\eta}\Big)\Big)+(1+\beta+\eta)(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta.

Thus, to prove the first item it suffices to establish that
\mathcal{R}_{\beta}
is well-defined and analytic. The map
\mathcal{R}_{\beta}
is comprised of linear terms and simple products interacting with the Nemytskii operator

(4.29)

\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ni\eta\mapsto(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\mu=\min\{{\delta,\rho}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

which is well-defined and analytic thanks to Proposition
2.21
and Corollary
A.6
. Therefore, it remains to study the boundedness of these simple products. We note that as a consequence of Propositions
2.7
,
2.14
, and
2.18
we have the bounds for
\zeta,\theta\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

(4.30)

\lVert\nabla\cdot(\zeta u\otimes u)\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mu}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}_{\rho}}^{2},\quad\lVert\zeta u\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mu}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}_{\rho}},

(4.31)

\lVert\nabla\cdot(\theta\mathbb{S}(\zeta u))\rVert_{L_{\rho}^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\theta\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\lVert\mathbb{S}(\zeta u)\rVert_{H^{1}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\theta\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mu}}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mu}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}_{\rho}},

(4.32)

\lVert\theta(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\theta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\theta\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mu}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\rho}}.

These bounds then conclude the proof of the first item.

The second item follows directly from the simple calculation
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\mathcal{R}_{\beta}((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})
.
∎

Remark 4.8

(Differences between the solitary and periodic cases)
.

Upon comparison of the operator formulation in the periodic case (Proposition
4.5
) with that of the solitary case (Proposition
4.7
), one notices that there are the following important distinctions. First, in the solitary case the domain of
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
hard codes the velocity variable to be divergence free; this is not a feature of the periodic case. Second, the codomain of
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
in the periodic case includes the ‘extra’ factor
\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
in the Cartesian product of function spaces to tabulate the source term of the continuity equation; the solitary case does not require such an addition to the codomain (due to the built in restriction to solenoidal velocity fields).

We remind the reader that the linear operators
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
of the solitary case are studied in Proposition
3.5
, where the quadratic forms
Q_{m}
for
m\in\{{1,2}\}
also make their first appearance. Our next result relates these linear maps to
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
and a compact nonlinear remainder. The corresponding result in the periodic case is Proposition
4.6
.

Proposition 4.9

(Operator decomposition - solitary case)
.

Let
\delta
,
\rho
,
\beta
,
A
,
G
, and
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
be as in Proposition
4.7
. The following hold.

(1)

For
\rho/2<\mu<\rho
the map
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}:({{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{1}_{\mu}\cap L^{\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap(\widetilde{H}_{\mu}^{2}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2})]\to L^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
given by

(4.33)

\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\nabla\cdot\Big(\frac{u\otimes u}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}}\Big)-\frac{2Au\eta}{(1+\beta)\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}((1+\beta)+\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}})}\\
+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot Q_{2}\Big(u,\nabla\log\Big(1+\frac{2\eta}{(1+\beta)^{2}}\Big)\Big)+\frac{\eta^{2}(G-\Delta)\nabla(1+\beta)}{(1+\beta)^{2}\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)((1+\beta)^{2}+\eta)}\\
\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot Q_{1}\Big(\nabla\Big(\frac{2\eta}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)}\Big)\Big)\\
-\nabla\cdot Q_{1}\Big(\nabla(1+\beta),\nabla\Big(\frac{\eta^{2}}{(1+\beta)^{2}\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)((1+\beta)^{2}+\eta)}\Big)\Big).

is well-defined and real analytic and satisfies
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(0,0)=0
and
D\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(0,0)=0
.

(2)

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restriction of
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
to
({{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{1}_{\mu}\cap L^{\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap(\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\mu}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2})]
maps bounded sets to bounded sets.

(3)

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restriction of
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
to
{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})]
maps bounded sets to precompact sets.

(4)

For all
(u,\eta)\in{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})]
we have the operator decomposition identity

(4.34)

\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(u,\eta)+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}(u,\eta)+\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta),

where
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
are the operators given by (
4.27
), (
3.25
), and (
3.26
), respectively.

Proof.

We again prove the first and second items in tandem. The nonlinearity
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
contains simple products of the velocity, free surface, and analytic nonlinear expressions in the free surface. We shall first study these latter expressions. Set
\chi=\min\{{\mu,\delta}\}\in(0,1)
and consider the auxiliary mappings

(4.35)

\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap({{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\chi}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\chi})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ni\eta\mapsto\begin{cases}f_{0}(\eta)=\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}},\\
f_{1}(\eta)=1/f_{0}(\eta),\\
f_{2}(\eta)=(1+\beta+f_{0}(\eta))^{-1},\\
f_{3}(\eta)=((1+\beta)f_{0}(\eta)+f_{0}(\eta)^{2}+(1+\beta)^{2}/2)^{-1}\end{cases}\in({{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\chi}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\chi})(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

The space
({{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\chi}\cap W^{1,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
is easily seen to be an admissible unital Banach algebra in the sense of Definition
A.3
by combining Propositions
A.10
and
2.21
; consequently, an application of Corollary
A.6
shows that
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are real analytic and, when restricted to their domain intersected with
\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}
, map bounded sets to bounded sets. Since
\inf\beta=0
and
f_{0}>0
on its domain, we compute the lower bounds
1/f_{2}>1
and
1/f_{3}>1/2
, which mean that the denominators in
f_{2}
and
f_{3}
stay well away from the singularity of the pointwise inversion map. Hence, we can use Corollary
A.6
again along with the preservation of real analyticity under composition to deduce that
f_{2}
and
f_{3}
are well-defined, real analytic, and when restricted to their domain intersected with
\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}
, map bounded sets to bounded sets. This Nemytskii operator analysis is sufficient to handle all but one term in (
4.33
), namely the third one on the right hand side. In this instance we consider the mapping

(4.36)

\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap(\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\mu}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ni\eta\mapsto\log\Big(1+\frac{2\eta}{(1+\beta)^{2}}\Big)\in(\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\mu}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

By quoting the same results as previously mentioned, the boundedness of the product map

(4.37)

({{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\delta}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\delta})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times(\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\mu}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to(\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\mu}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

which is a consequence of Proposition
2.18
, along with the fact that
\log(1)=0
we deduce that the mapping (
4.36
