) is well-defined, analytic, and maps bounded sets to bounded sets when restricted to the intersection of its domain and
\mathbf{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}
.

The previous analysis then allows us to reduce the proof of the first and second items to a series of simple product bounds. Suppose that
u\in({{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{1}_{\mu}\cap L^{\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\eta\in(\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\mu}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and
\zeta\in({{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\chi}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\chi})
. Through repeated applications of the product bounds of Propositions
2.7
and
2.18
we deduce that

(4.38)

\lVert\nabla\cdot(\zeta u\otimes u)\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}_{\mu}},\quad\lVert\zeta u\eta\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}_{\mu}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}},

(4.39)

\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{2}(u,\nabla\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}}\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}_{\mu}}+\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}_{\mu}}\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}},

(4.40)

\lVert\zeta\eta^{2}(G-\Delta)\nabla(1+\beta)\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}}^{2},

(4.41)

\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{1}(\nabla(\zeta\eta))\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla(\zeta\eta)\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}}\lVert\nabla(\zeta\eta)\rVert_{H^{1}_{\mu}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}_{\mu}}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\chi}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\mu}},

(4.42)

\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{1}(\nabla(1+\beta),\nabla(\zeta\eta^{2}))\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla(\zeta\eta^{2})\rVert_{H^{1}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\zeta\rVert_{{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\chi}}\lVert\eta\rVert^{2}_{\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\mu}}.

We then read off by the embeddings of the weighted spaces (Propositions
2.14
and
2.16
) that each of the polynomial nonlinearities above (given within the norms on the left hand sides of (
4.38
), (
4.39
), (
4.40
), (
4.41
), and (
4.42
)) are a continuous function of
u
,
\eta
,
\zeta
for the norms on their respective spaces. By synthesizing the previous work we complete the proof of the first and second items.

The third item follows from the first two items and the compactness of the embedding

(4.43)

{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow({{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}\widetilde{H}^{1}_{\mu}\cap L^{\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{U}^{\varepsilon/2}_{\beta}\cap(\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\mu}\cap W^{1,\infty}_{\mu})(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Finally, we prove the fourth item. Recall that in the proof of Proposition
3.5
we computed

(4.44)

(\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta})(u,\eta)=\frac{Au}{1+\beta}-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u+\nabla\cdot Q_{2}(\nabla\log(1+\beta),u)+(1+\beta)(G-\Delta)\nabla\Big(\frac{\eta}{1+\beta}\Big).

To add
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
to the above expression and result in
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
, we observe the following identities:

(4.45)

\frac{1}{1+\beta}-\frac{2\eta}{(1+\beta)\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}((1+\beta)+\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}},

(4.46)

\log(1+\beta)+\frac{1}{2}\log\Big(1+\frac{2\eta}{(1+\beta)^{2}}\Big)=\log\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}},

(4.47)

-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u+\nabla\cdot Q_{2}(u,\nabla\log\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}})=-\nabla\cdot\Big(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}\mathbb{S}\Big(\frac{u}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}}\Big)\Big).

These are sufficient to match the drag term, the viscous stress tensor, and the advective derivative pieces of the expression for
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
(
4.27
). It remains to study the gravity-capillary term (which is the final summand in (
4.27
)). For this we crucially use the following divergence identity which holds for scalars
\phi
:

(4.48)

\phi(G-\Delta)\nabla\phi=\frac{1}{2}(G-\Delta)\nabla(\phi^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot Q_{1}(\nabla\phi,\nabla\phi).

This allows us to write

(4.49)

\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}(G-\Delta)\nabla(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta))=(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta\\
+\nabla\cdot Q_{1}(\nabla(1+\beta),\nabla(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta)))+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot Q_{1}(\nabla(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta)))\\
-(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta))(G-\Delta)\nabla(1+\beta).

Notice that the first term on the right hand side of (
4.49
) is the principal linear part’s gravity-capillary contribution. To remove the lower order bathymetric linear remainder we rewrite the second and fourth terms on the right side of (
4.49
), employing the identity

(4.50)

\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta)=\frac{2\eta}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)}\\
=\frac{\eta}{1+\beta}-\frac{\eta^{2}}{(1+\beta)^{2}\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)((1+\beta)^{2}+\eta)}

to leave behind only terms that are linear in
\eta
or at least quadratic in
\eta
. Once this is done, the identity
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}+\mathcal{N}_{\beta}=\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
follows readily.
∎

4.3.
Fixed point reformulation and consequences

Our goal now is to use the analysis developed in the previous sections to recast the system (
1.8
) with (
1.11
) as a fixed point equation for a compact perturbation of the identity. Once this is done, we will be able to read off some important consequences. We begin with a definition that serves to unify the periodic and solitary cases at an abstract level. In it we will use the invertibility of the linear maps
\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
, which are consequences of Propositions
3.4
and
3.5
, the data map notation
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
form Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
, and the nonlinear map
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
notation from Propositions
4.6
and
4.9
.

Definition 4.10

(Periodic and solitary unification)
.

We make the following definitions which have an implicit dependence on either the periodic or solitary cases.

(1)

The set of admissible drag, gravity, and bathymetry parameters is given by

(4.51)

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}=\begin{cases}\{{(A,G,\beta)\;:\;A>0,\;G\geqslant 0,\;\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}),\;\min\beta=0}\}&\text{in the }\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\text{ case},\\
\{{(A,G,\beta)\;:\;A,G>0,\;\exists\;\delta\in(0,1),\;\beta\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}\widetilde{H}^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\;\inf\beta=0}\}&\text{in the }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ case}.\end{cases}

Henceforth we shall assume that the above inclusions are satisfied in their respective cases.

(2)

We define the domain Banach spaces (recall (
3.8
))

(4.52)

\boldsymbol{X}=\begin{cases}H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})&\text{in the }\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\text{ case},\\
{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\text{in the }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ case}.\end{cases}

(3)

We endow the domain Banach spaces with a (Lipschitz) continuous functional
\varrho:\boldsymbol{X}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

(4.53)

\varrho(u,\eta)=\begin{cases}\min(1+\beta+\eta)&\text{in the }\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\text{ case},\\
\inf((1+\beta)^{2}+2\eta)&\text{in the }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ case}.\end{cases}

We then define the open sets
U=\varrho^{-1}(0,\infty)
and, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
U_{\varepsilon}=\varrho^{-1}(\varepsilon,\infty)
.

(4)

Finally, we define the perturbation operator
\mathcal{K}:U\times\mathbb{R}\to\boldsymbol{X}
via

(4.54)

\mathcal{K}(u,\eta,\kappa)=\begin{cases}(\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta})^{-1}(\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta)-\kappa(0,\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta)))&\text{in the }\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\text{ case},\\
(\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta})^{-1}(\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta)-\kappa\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta))&\text{in the }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ case}.\end{cases}

Remark 4.11
.

In equation (
4.54
) (and later in equation (
4.55
)) we observe that in the periodic case the form of the forcing is
\kappa(0,\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta))
whereas in the solitary case we only have
\kappa\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta)
. This discrepancy stems from our analysis of the periodic case requiring bookkeeping of source terms for the continuity equation. See also Remark
4.8
.

We may now summarize our findings of Sections
3
and
4
with the following result. Recall that the operators
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
are studied in the periodic and solitary cases in Propositions
4.5
and
4.7
, respectively.

Theorem 4.12

(Unified fixed point reformulation)
.

The following hold.

(1)

The operator
\mathcal{K}:U\times\mathbb{R}\to\boldsymbol{X}
from the fourth item of Definition
4.10
is well-defined and real analytic, and satisfies
\mathcal{K}(0,0)=0
and
D_{1}\mathcal{K}(0,0)=0
, where
D_{1}
is the derivative with respect to the
\boldsymbol{X}
-factor in the domain.

(2)

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restricted operator
\mathcal{K}:U_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}\to\boldsymbol{X}
maps bounded sets to precompact sets; moreover, at every
(x,\kappa)\in U_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}
the linear map
D\mathcal{K}(x,\kappa):\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{X}
is compact.

(3)

For all
(x,\kappa)\in U\times\mathbb{R}
we have
x+\mathcal{K}(x,\kappa)=0
if and only if
x=(u,\eta)\in\boldsymbol{X}
with

(4.55)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\kappa(0,\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta))&\text{in the }\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\text{ case},\\
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\kappa\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta)&\text{in the }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ case}.\end{cases}

(4)

For all
(x,\kappa)\in U\times\mathbb{R}
, the linear operator
I_{\boldsymbol{X}}+D_{1}\mathcal{K}(x,\kappa):\boldsymbol{X}\to\boldsymbol{X}
is Fredholm with index zero.

(5)

Denote the set of solutions by

(4.56)

S=\{{(x,\kappa)\in U\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;x+\mathcal{K}(x,\kappa)=0}\}.

If
E\subseteq S
satisfies

(4.57)

\sup\{\lVert x\rVert_{\boldsymbol{X}}+|\kappa|+1/\varrho(x)\;:\>(x,\kappa)\in E\}<\infty,

then
\overline{E}\subset\boldsymbol{X}\times\mathbb{R}
is a compact subset of
U\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Theorem 1.40 in Schwartz
[
58
]
establishes that the derivatives of compact maps are compact linear operators. Hence, in the periodic case the first and second items are a synthesis of Propositions
3.4
,
4.2
, and
4.6
. On the other hand, in the solitary case we combine Propositions
3.5
,
4.4
, and
4.9
.

The third item is a consequence of the operator formulation Propositions
4.5
and
4.7
in addition to the final items of the operator decomposition Propositions
4.6
and
4.9
.

To prove the fourth item fix
(x,\kappa)\in U\times\mathbb{R}
and note that there exists
\epsilon\in(0,1)
such that
(x,\kappa)\in U_{\epsilon}\times\mathbb{R}
. The second item then implies that that
D_{1}\mathcal{K}(x,\kappa)
is a compact operator on
\boldsymbol{X}
, and hence the sum
I_{\boldsymbol{X}}+D_{1}\mathcal{K}(x,\kappa)
is a Fredholm operator with index zero.

We now prove the fifth item. The bound (
4.57
) ensures the existence of
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
such that
\{{x:(x,\kappa)\in E}\}\subset U_{2\varepsilon}
, and hence
\overline{E}\subset U_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}
. The compactness of
\mathcal{K}:U_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}\to\boldsymbol{X}
then guarantees that
\overline{\mathcal{K}(E)}
is a compact set. By definition,
x=-\mathcal{K}(x,\kappa)
for every
(x,\kappa)\in E
, so
\overline{\{{x\;:\;(x,\kappa)\in E}\}}\subset\boldsymbol{X}
is compact as well. Finally, the set
\overline{\{\kappa\;:\;(x,\kappa)\in E\}}
is compact by Heine-Borel. The fifth item follows.

∎

5.
Large solutions and blowup scenarios

Our aim now is to combine the semilinearization analysis of Section
4
with an analytic global implicit function theorem to produce curves of solutions in (
4.56
), which will ultimately lead to the existence of large solutions. In addition, we prove a host of a priori estimates for solutions to (
1.8
) that provide a refined understanding of the large solutions.

5.1.
Analytic global implicit function theorem and solution curves

One of our main tools to study the solution set (
4.56
) from Theorem
4.12
is the analytic global implicit function theorem of Chen, Walsh, and Wheeler, Theorem B.1 in
[
14
]
. We state a minor retooling of this result here, which allows for more customization in the ‘blowup’ scenario.

Theorem 5.1

(Analytic global implicit function theorem)
.

Let
\mathscr{W}
and
\mathscr{Z}
be Banach spaces,
\rho:\mathscr{W}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
a continuous function,
\mathcal{W}=\rho^{-1}(0,\infty)\subset\mathscr{W}\times\mathbb{R}
,
(w_{0},\lambda_{0})\in\mathcal{W}
, and
\mathscr{G}:\mathcal{W}\to\mathscr{Z}
a real analytic function satisfying

(5.1)

\mathscr{G}(w_{0},\lambda_{0})=0\text{ with }D_{1}\mathscr{G}(w_{0},\lambda_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{W};\mathscr{Z})\text{ an isomorphism}.

Then there exists a zero solution curve
\mathscr{K}\subset\mathcal{W}\cap\mathscr{G}^{-1}\{{0}\}
, admitting the global continuous parametrization
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(w(s),\lambda(s))\in\mathscr{K}
with
(w(0),\lambda(0))=(w_{0},\lambda_{0})
, for which the following hold.

(1)

At each
s\in\mathbb{R}
the linear operator
D_{1}\mathscr{G}(w(s),\lambda(s))
is Fredholm with index zero.

(2)

The curve
\mathscr{K}
is locally real analytic: for each
(w,\lambda)\in\mathscr{K}
there exists an open set
(w,\lambda)\in U\subseteq\mathcal{W}
such that
\mathscr{K}\cap U
admits a real analytic reparameterization.

(3)

The curve
\mathscr{K}
is maximal in the sense that if
\mathscr{J}\subset\mathcal{W}\cap\mathscr{G}^{-1}\{{0}\}
is a locally real analytic curve containing
(w_{0},\lambda_{0})
and along which
D_{1}\mathscr{G}
is a Fredholm operator with index equal to zero, then
\mathscr{J}\subseteq\mathscr{K}
.

(4)

Either the curve
\mathscr{K}
is a
closed loop
in the sense that there exists a period
T\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
we have
(w(s),\lambda(s))=(w(s+T),\lambda(s+T))
or, for each of the limits as
s\to\pm\infty
, one of the following alternatives holds:

(A)

Blowup
- The quantity

(5.2)

\boldsymbol{N}(s)=\lVert w(s)\rVert_{\mathscr{W}}+|\lambda(s)|+1/\rho(w(s),\lambda(s))

tends to
\infty
as
s\to\pm\infty
.

(B)

Loss of compactness
- There exists a sequence
\{{s_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}
with
s_{n}\to\pm\infty
as
n\to\infty
and
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{N}(s_{n})<\infty
but
\{{(w(s_{n}),\lambda(s_{n}))}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has no convergent subsequence in
\mathcal{W}
.

(C)

Loss of Fredholm index zero
- There exists
(w_{\star},\lambda_{\star})\in\mathcal{W}
and a sequence
\{{s_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}
with
s_{n}\to\pm\infty
as
n\to\infty
satisfying
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{N}(s_{n})<\infty
and
(w(s_{n}),\lambda(s_{n}))\to(w_{\star},\lambda_{\star})
as
n\to\infty
but
D_{1}\mathscr{G}(w_{\star},\lambda_{\star})
is not a Fredholm operator with index equal to zero.

Proof.

The proof follows from a very minor adaptation of that of Chen, Walsh, and Wheeler’s Theorem B.1 in
[
14
]
, which itself is based on Theorem 6.1 in Chen, Walsh, Wheeler
[
13
]
and Theorem 9.1.1 in Buffoni and Toland
[
11
]
. As such, we only point out the singular place where there is a difference.

The theorem assertions here are identical to those of B.1 in
[
14
]
except for the definition of the blowup quantity
\boldsymbol{N}
in alternative
(A)
of the fourth item. In the last term of (
5.2
) we have
1/\rho
, where
\rho^{-1}(0,\infty)=\mathcal{W}
, whereas Chen, Walsh, and Wheeler use the pointwise inversion of the distance to the boundary of
\mathcal{W}
. In their proof this term serves to guarantee that if a sequence
\{{(w(s_{n}),\lambda(s_{n}))}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{W}
satisfies

(5.3)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[{\lVert w(s_{n})\rVert_{\mathscr{W}}+|\lambda(s_{n})|+1/\mathrm{dist}((w(s_{n}),\lambda(s_{n})),\partial\mathcal{W}])}]<\infty,

then every subsequential limit lies within the open set
\mathcal{W}
. The same conclusion holds if we replace (
5.3
) with the condition
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{N}(s_{n})<\infty
; indeed, if this holds, then there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\{{w(s_{n}),\lambda(s_{n})}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}
, where
\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}=\rho^{-1}(\varepsilon,\infty)
, and
\overline{\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon/2}\subset\mathcal{W}
. Hence, any subsequential limit belongs to
\mathcal{W}
, as claimed.
∎

Our next task is to apply Theorem
5.1
within the framework established in Definition
4.10
and Theorem
4.12
. Recall that the set of solutions in our fixed point reformulation is given by
S
in (
4.56
). We will now obtain a solution curve within
S
and dispense with the possibility of the
closed loop
,
loss of compactness
, and
loss of Fredholm index zero
alternatives, which means that the function
\boldsymbol{N}
of (
5.2
) must blowup at the endpoints of this curve.

Proposition 5.2

(Maximal locally analytic curve of solutions)
.

There exists a maximal locally analytic curve
\mathscr{S}\subseteq S
with
(0,0)\in\mathscr{S}
, parametrized by the continuous map
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(x(s),\kappa(s))\in\mathscr{S}
sending
0\mapsto(0,0)
and satisfying

(5.4)

\lim_{|s|\to\infty}[{\lVert x(s)\rVert_{\boldsymbol{X}}+|\kappa(s)|+1/\varrho(x(s))}]=\infty.

Proof.

According to Theorem
4.12
, the hypotheses of Theorem
5.1
are satisfied with
\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{Z}=\boldsymbol{X}
,
\rho=\varrho
,
\mathcal{W}=U\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathscr{G}=I_{\boldsymbol{X}}+\mathcal{K}
. The theorem then grants the solution curve
\mathscr{S}\subseteq S
with continuous parametrization
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(x(s),\kappa(s))\in\mathscr{S}
, and it guarantees that the curve
\mathscr{S}
is either a closed loop or else one of the three alternatives of the fourth item of Theorem
5.1
holds. We claim that
\mathscr{S}
is not a closed loop. Once this is established, we may invoke Theorem
4.12
to exclude the possibility of the loss of compactness (alternative B) or loss of Fredholm index zero (alternative C) scenarios from Theorem
5.1
. This leaves blowup (alternative A) as the only possibility, which will then complete the proof.

To prove the claim it suffices to show that
\mathscr{S}\setminus\{{(0,0)}\}
is disconnected. Consider the open sets
W_{\pm}=\{{(x,\kappa)\in U\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;\pm\kappa>0}\}
, which by definition satisfy
W_{+}\cap W_{-}=\varnothing
. We will prove that
\mathscr{S}\setminus\{{(0,0)}\}\subset W_{+}\cup W_{-}
, from which the claimed disconnectedness follows. For this we may further reduce to proving that if
(x,0)\in\mathscr{S}
, then
x=0
. Fix such an
(x,0)\in\mathscr{S}
and write
x=(u,\eta)\in\boldsymbol{X}
. We then know from the third item of Theorem
4.12
that
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=0
. We now break to cases based on the periodic or solitary settings.

Consider first the periodic setting. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=0
, the second item of Proposition
4.5
implies that system (
1.8
) is satisfied by
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})=(u,\eta)
with
\Phi=0
. By testing the second equation of (
1.8
) with
\boldsymbol{u}
, integrating by parts, and using the first equation to annihilate the gravity-capillary contribution, we derive the identity

(5.5)

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}A|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}+(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})(2^{-1}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathrm{t}}|^{2}+2|\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}|^{2})=0,

which implies that
u=0
. In turn, we find that
(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})(G-\Delta)\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}=0
, but since
(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})>0
and
\mathscr{F}[\boldsymbol{\eta}](0)=0
we must have
\boldsymbol{\eta}=0
as well. This completes the proof of the claim in the periodic case.

Next, we consider the solitary setting, in which case almost the same argument works. We use the second item of Proposition
4.7
to again find that (
1.8
) holds, but this time by the velocity
\boldsymbol{u}=(2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2})^{-1/2}u
and free surface
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta)
. Lemma
4.3
and Proposition
2.18
imply that
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})\in H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, so the same integration by parts argument as in the periodic case reveals that

(5.6)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}A|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}+(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})(2^{-1}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathrm{t}}|^{2}+2|\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}|^{2})=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})(G-\Delta)\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\Big|.

By using the density of
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\subset\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(see Proposition
2.17
) as in the proof of Proposition
3.2
we find that the right hand side of (
5.6
) vanishes, and so
\boldsymbol{u}=0
. Next we deduce from
1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}>0
and
(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})(G-\Delta)\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}
that
\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}=0
and so
\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{3}_{1/2}}=0
. Finally, we deduce from the expressions
\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
that necessarily
u=0
and
\eta=0
as well. This proves the claim in the solitary case.
∎

5.2.
Blowup scenarios in the periodic case

We now aim to more carefully analyze what happens along the solution curve
\mathscr{S}
from Proposition
5.2
as the parametrization parameter
s
tends to
\pm\infty
. In this subsection we restrict our attention to the periodic case, which is much simpler, and delay the solitary case to the next subsection. Our main strategy is to derive a priori estimates for system
1.8
, starting with control of relatively weak quantities. These quantities can then replace the strong norm in the blowup quantity (
5.4
).

Proposition 5.3

(A priori estimates - periodic case)
.

Suppose that for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
we have
(u,\eta)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})]
with
\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
a solution to system (
1.8
) for data
\kappa=1
and a generic forcing
\Phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then, for implicit constants depending only on
\varepsilon>0
and the physical parameters we have the a priori bounds

(5.7)

\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{1}\times H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{3}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\rangle^{16/3}.

Proof.

We begin by testing the second equation in (
1.8
) with
u
and integrating by parts to see that

(5.8)

\lVert u\rVert^{2}_{H^{1}}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}A|u|^{2}+(1+\beta+\eta)(2^{-1}|\nabla u+\nabla u^{\mathrm{t}}|^{2}+2|\nabla\cdot u|^{2})=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}\Phi\cdot u\leqslant\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}},

from which it follows that
\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}
.

Next, we test the same equation with the vector field
(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, isolate the gravity-capillary contribution, and recall that
\mathscr{F}[\eta](0)=0
:

(5.9)

\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{H^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}G|\nabla\eta|^{2}+|\nabla^{2}\eta|^{2}\\
=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}(1+\beta+\eta)(u\otimes u-\mathbb{S}u):\nabla((1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta)+(\Phi-Au)\cdot(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta.

To estimate the right hand side, we claim the following preliminary bound:

(5.10)

\lVert(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}

with an implicit constant dependent on
\varepsilon
. To see why (
5.10
) holds, we first note that
\lVert(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}
and then use the product rule to compute

(5.11)

\nabla((1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta)=(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla^{2}\eta-(1+\beta+\eta)^{-2}\nabla\eta\otimes\nabla\eta+(1+\beta+\eta)^{-2}\nabla\eta\otimes\nabla\beta.

By taking the norm in
L^{2}
of the above expression, we then derive the bound

(5.12)

\lVert\nabla((1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}+\lVert\eta\rVert_{W^{1,4}}^{2}.

To handle the final term here we employ Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation (Theorem
A.7
):

(5.13)

\lVert\eta\rVert_{W^{1,4}}^{2}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}.

Synthesizing these estimates proves the claimed estimate (
5.10
), which then allows us to bound the terms on the right side of (
5.9
):

(5.14)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}(1+\beta+\eta)(u\otimes u-\mathbb{S}u):\nabla((1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta)\Big|\lesssim(\lVert u\rVert_{L^{4}}^{2}+\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}})\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\rangle\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}

and

(5.15)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}(\Phi-Au)\cdot(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta\Big|\lesssim(\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}+\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}})\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}.

We then combine (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) with (
5.9
) to deduce the remaining
\eta
estimate on the left of (
5.7
).

We next prove the higher regularity estimate. The system (
1.8
) is equivalent to the semilinear form

(5.16)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot u=-P_{0}(u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta)),\\
Au-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u+(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta=(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}(\Phi+A(\beta+\eta)u)-u\cdot\nabla u+\mathbb{S}u\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta).\end{cases}

The linear operator on the left hand side of (
5.16
) is
\mathcal{P}
, which is an isomorphism
H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
thanks to the first item of Proposition
3.4
. Therefore, we may deduce the right bound in (
5.7
) by bounding the
H^{1}
and
L^{2}
norms of the right hand sides of the first and second equations in (
5.16
), respectively. We begin with the first term, using Theorem
A.8
(and (
5.10
)) to estimate

(5.17)

\lVert P_{0}(u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta))\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\eta\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\rangle+\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\langle\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\rangle\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\rangle\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\rangle^{2}.

We then bound the second term via

(5.18)

\lVert(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}(\Phi+A(\beta+\eta)u)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}},

(5.19)

\lVert u\cdot\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{4}}\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}},

and

(5.20)

\lVert\mathbb{S}u\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\eta\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\rangle\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\rangle^{2}.

Using the sum of (
5.17
), (
5.18
), (
5.19
), and (
5.20
) with the
\mathcal{P}
isomorphism then shows that

(5.21)

\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{3}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\rangle^{2}.

Again using Theorem
A.7
, Sobolev embeddings, and interpolation, we find that

(5.22)

\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{1/2}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}}^{1/2},\quad\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{5/4}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}^{3/4}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}}^{1/4}\;\Rightarrow\;\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1/4}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}^{3/8}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}}^{5/8},

so Young’s inequality implies that for any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
there exists a
C_{\mu}<\infty
such that

(5.23)

\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{3}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}+C_{\mu}\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\rangle^{16/3}+\mu\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}}.

Taking
\mu
sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and the physical parameters), we can absorb and obtain the right hand side of (
5.7
).
∎

The a priori estimates of Proposition
5.3
lead us to the following blowup refinement.

Corollary 5.4

(Blowup refinement - periodic case)
.

Let
\mathscr{S}\subset S
be the maximal locally analytic curve of solutions granted by Proposition
5.2
in the periodic case, parametrized by the continuous map
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(u(s),\eta(s),\kappa(s))\in\mathscr{S}\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times[\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})]
. Then

(5.24)

\lim_{|s|\to\infty}[{\lVert\eta(s)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+1/\inf(1+\beta+\eta(s))+|\kappa(s)|}]=\infty.

Proof.

The argument for the limits
s\to\infty
and
s\to-\infty
are identical, so we will only address the former case here. We argue by contradiction, supposing that (
5.24
) is false as
s\to\infty
. Then we can find a sequence
\{{s_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
s_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and a number
1<C<\infty
such that upon setting
\eta_{n}=\eta(s_{n})
and
\kappa_{n}=\kappa(s_{n})
we have

(5.25)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[{\lVert\eta_{n}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+1/\inf(1+\beta+\eta_{n})+|\kappa_{n}|}]\leqslant C.

Let us also set
u_{n}=u(s_{n})
. According to Proposition
5.2
, the solution curve
\mathscr{S}
is contained in the set
S
from (
4.56
); in turn, thanks to the third item of Theorem
4.12
and the definition of the forcing map
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
in the periodic case (see Proposition
4.2
), for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

(5.26)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\eta_{n})u_{n})=0,\\
\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\eta_{n})u_{n}\otimes u_{n})+Au_{n}-\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\eta_{n})\mathbb{S}u_{n})+(1+\beta+\eta_{n})(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta_{n}=\Phi_{n},\\
\Phi_{n}=\kappa_{n}[{\Upomega_{\phi}(\eta_{n})+\nabla((1+\beta+\eta_{n})\Upomega_{\psi}(\eta_{n}))+\Upomega_{\tau}(\eta_{n})\nabla\eta_{n}}].\end{cases}

Our first goal is to invoke the left a priori estimate of (
5.7
), and for this we require uniform
H^{-1}
bounds on the forcing sequence
\{{\Phi_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. The hypothesis (
5.25
) implies that for
\varepsilon=C^{-1}\in(0,1)
we have
\{{\eta_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap B_{C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}}(0,\varepsilon^{-1})
. Corollary
A.5
implies that the functions
\Upomega_{\phi}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\Upomega_{\psi}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
map bounded sets to bounded sets, so we may estimate

(5.27)

\lVert\Phi_{n}\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lesssim\lVert\Upomega_{\phi}(\eta_{n})\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert(1+\beta+\eta_{n})\Upomega_{\psi}(\eta_{n})\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert\Upomega_{\tau}(\eta_{n})\nabla\eta_{n}\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lesssim 1+\lVert\Upomega_{\tau}(\eta_{n})\nabla\eta_{n}\rVert_{H^{-1}}

for implicit constants depending only on
C
. To handle the final term using only the
L^{\infty}
bounds on
\eta_{n}
, we aim to pull out a derivative and obtain a nice remainder. To this end, we define the real analytic primitive

(5.28)

\widetilde{\tau}:(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to(L^{\infty}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}})\text{ via }\widetilde{\tau}(z)=\int_{0}^{z}\tau(\mathfrak{z})\;\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{z}

and claim the identity:

(5.29)

\Upomega_{\tau}(\eta_{n})\nabla\eta_{n}=\nabla\cdot(\Upomega_{\widetilde{\tau}}(\eta_{n}))-\Upomega_{\nabla\cdot\widetilde{\tau}}(\eta_{n}).

To establish (
5.29
), we begin by noting that Corollary
A.5
ensures that the mapping
\Upomega_{\widetilde{\tau}}:\mathbf{O}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}})
maps bounded sets to bounded sets while the proof of Corollary
A.5
and Theorem
A.1
(specifically (
A.7
)) provides the Cauchy integral formula

(5.30)

\Upomega_{\widetilde{\tau}}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{\eta}}(z-\eta)^{-1}\widetilde{\tau}(z)\;\mathrm{d}z\text{ for }\eta\in\mathbf{O}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}),

where
\widetilde{\tau}
is analytically continued into an open and simply connected domain in
\mathbb{C}
containing the compact interval
\overline{\mathrm{img}(\eta)}
and
\gamma_{\eta}
is a simple closed contour that loops around this image once within the domain of
\widetilde{\tau}
. Due to the integrand of (
5.30
) being a holomorphic function of
z
belonging to the compact image of the contour
\gamma_{\eta}
with values in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
, we are free to take the divergence, commute with the integral, and use the standard calculus of weak derivatives on the integrand to derive the initial identity

(5.31)

\nabla\cdot(\Upomega_{\widetilde{\tau}}(\eta))=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{\eta}}(z-\eta)^{-1}\nabla\cdot\widetilde{\tau}(z)\;\mathrm{d}z+\Big[{\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{\eta}}(z-\eta)^{-2}\widetilde{\tau}(z)\;\mathrm{d}z}\Big]\nabla\eta.

Thanks to equation (
A.7
), the first term on the left of (
5.31
) is none other than
\Upomega_{\nabla\cdot\widetilde{\tau}}(\eta)
. To handle the second term we note that

(5.32)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}((z-\eta)^{-1}\widetilde{\tau}(z))=(z-\eta)^{-1}\tau(z)+(z-\eta)^{-2}\widetilde{\tau}(z),\quad z\in\gamma_{\eta}

and the contour integral over
\gamma_{\eta}
of the left hand side of (
5.32
) vanishes. Therefore (after again using (
A.7
))

(5.33)

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{\eta}}(z-\eta)^{-2}\widetilde{\tau}(z)\;\mathrm{d}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{\eta}}(z-\eta)^{-1}\tau(z)\;\mathrm{d}z=\Upomega_{\tau}(\eta).

By synthesizing (
5.31
) with (
5.33
) the claimed identity of (
5.29
) is established.

Now by using Corollary
A.5
again, we find that the functions
\Upomega_{\widetilde{\tau}}:\mathbf{O}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}})
and
\Upomega_{\nabla\cdot\widetilde{\tau}}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}})
map bounded sets to bounded sets, and hence from (
5.29
)

(5.34)

\lVert\Upomega_{\tau}(\eta_{n})\nabla{\eta_{n}}\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lesssim\lVert\Upomega_{\widetilde{\tau}}(\eta_{n})\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert\Upomega_{\nabla\cdot\widetilde{\tau}}(\eta_{n})\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1.

By combining (
5.27
) and (
5.34
) we then find that
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\Phi_{n}\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lesssim 1
. Pairing this fact with the low regularity estimate of Proposition
5.3
then yields the bound

(5.35)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert u_{n},\eta_{n}\rVert_{H^{1}\times H^{2}}\lesssim 1.

The next step is to use the improved bounds (
5.35
) on
\{{\eta_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to deduce that the forcing sequence is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}
. This is a direct consequence of the second item of Proposition
4.2
and the identity
\Phi_{n}=\kappa_{n}(1+\beta+\eta_{n})\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta_{n})
. Therefore,
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\Phi_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1
and so the high regularity estimate of Proposition
5.3
tells us that

(5.36)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert u_{n},\eta_{n}\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{3}}\lesssim 1.

Together, the bounds (
5.25
) and (
5.36
) contradict the limit (
5.4
) from Proposition
5.2
, so it must be the case that the limit (
5.24
) holds as
s\to\infty
.
∎

5.3.
Blowup scenarios in the solitary case

In this subsection we derive results analogous to those of Section
5.2
, but in the solitary case. The situation here is significantly complicated by the fact that the blowup norm in (
5.4
) comes from weighted Sobolev spaces, but the natural ‘energy’ structure of the equations themselves is framed in unweighted spaces. As a result, our estimates in this section follow a multi-step procedure. We begin by deriving an unweighted
L^{2}
-based result analogous to Proposition
5.3
. The following lemma is an important ingredient.

Lemma 5.5

(Unweighted estimates for the principal part)
.

Assume that
A,G>0
. The following hold.

(1)

If
u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\eta\in\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\nabla\eta\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
are a solution to the linear system (
3.1
) with data
\psi\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
then we have the bound

(5.37)

\lVert u,\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\psi,\phi\rVert_{(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1})\times L^{2}}

for implicit constants depending only on the physical parameters.

(2)

If
1<q<2
,
u\in W^{2,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\eta\in L^{2q/(2-q)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\nabla\eta\in W^{2,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\nabla\cdot u=0
are a solution to the linear system (
3.1
) with data
\psi=0
and
\phi\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
then we have the estimate

(5.38)

\lVert u,\eta,\nabla\eta\rVert_{W^{2,q}\times L^{2q/(2-q)}\times W^{2,q}}\lesssim\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{q}},

for implicit constants depending only on
q
and the physical parameters.

Proof.

We begin with the first item. The proof strategy here is in the same spirit as that of Proposition
3.2
. We begin by defining the auxiliary function
w=\Delta^{-1}\nabla\psi\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Notice that

(5.39)

\lVert w\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\psi\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1}}\text{ and }\nabla\cdot w=\psi,

and hence the function
v=u-w
is divergence free and satisfies the PDE

(5.40)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot v=0,\\
(A-\Delta)v+(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta=\phi-Aw+\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}w.\end{cases}

To the second equation we apply the operator
(G-\Delta)^{-1}\Delta^{-1}\nabla\nabla\cdot
, which annihilates the
v
contribution, and gives us

(5.41)

\nabla\eta=(G-\Delta)^{-1}\Delta^{-1}\nabla\nabla\cdot(\phi-Aw+\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}w)\quad\text{and so}\quad\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert w\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\psi,\phi\rVert_{(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1})\times L^{2}}.

On the other hand, we can use the second equation in (
5.40
) to write

(5.42)

v=(A-\Delta)^{-1}(\phi-Aw+\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}w-(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta)\quad\text{from which the bound}\quad\lVert v\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\psi,\phi\rVert_{(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1})\times L^{2}}

follows.

The second item is also simple. We derive the
\psi=0
analogs of formulas (
5.41
) and (
5.42
) which are

(5.43)

\nabla\eta=(G-\Delta)^{-1}\Delta^{-1}\nabla\nabla\cdot\phi,\quad u=(A-\Delta)^{-1}(\phi-(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta).

From the boundedness of
(G-\Delta)^{-1}:L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to W^{2,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\Delta^{-1}\nabla\nabla\cdot:L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(which are consequences of the Mikhlin multiplier theorem) we deduce the bound

(5.44)

\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{W^{2,q}}\lesssim\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{q}}\quad\text{from which it follows}\quad\lVert u\rVert_{W^{2,q}}\lesssim\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{q}}.

Finally, the subcritical Sobolev embedding provides the estimate
\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{q}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

We now prove the unweighted estimate.

Proposition 5.6

(Unweighted a priori estimates)
.

Suppose that for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
we have
(u,\eta)\in H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
a solution to system (
1.8
) for data
\kappa=1
and a generic forcing
\Phi\in L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then, for implicit constants depending only on
\varepsilon>0
and the physical parameters, we have the a priori bounds

(5.45)

\lVert u,\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}\times H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\rangle,\quad\lVert u,\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\rangle^{16/3}.

Proof.

By arguing via density of smooth and compactly supported functions in the weighted gradient spaces as in the proof of Proposition
5.2
(and elsewhere) to annihilate the gravity-capillary contribution, we derive the energy bound:

(5.46)

\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}A|u|^{2}+(1+\beta+\eta)(2^{-1}|\nabla u+\nabla u^{\mathrm{t}}|^{2}+2|\nabla\cdot u|^{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi\cdot u\leqslant\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}.

Hence
\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}
. The next step is again to test the equation with
(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta\in H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
as in (
5.9
). We require a solitary analog of the preliminary bound (
5.10
). We still have the estimate
\lVert(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{2}}
and we still have the expression
5.11
for the derivative. By taking the
L^{2}
norm of this expression, we obtain the following bound (which happens to be slightly shaper than (
5.12
))

(5.47)

\lVert\nabla((1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}+\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{4}}^{2}.

Now instead of using (
5.13
), we instead use the homogeneous form of the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation estimates (see (
A.43
) of Theorem
A.7
) to see that

(5.48)

\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert^{2}_{L^{4}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}.

Therefore, we deduce the estimate

(5.49)

\lVert(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}.

We combine (
5.49
) with the integral identity

(5.50)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}G|\nabla\eta|^{2}+|\nabla^{2}\eta|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(1+\beta+\eta)(u\otimes u-\mathbb{S}u):\nabla((1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta)+(\Phi-Au)\cdot(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\nabla\eta,

and the bounds

(5.51)

\lVert(1+\beta+\eta)(u\otimes u-\mathbb{S}u)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{4}}^{2}+\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\rangle,

(5.52)

\lVert\Phi-Au\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}},\quad\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}G|\nabla\eta|^{2}+|\nabla^{2}\eta|^{2}

to deduce that
\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\rangle
. This proves the left hand inequality in (
5.45
).

To prove the higher regularity estimate, we will again move to a semilinear form of the equations. Fortunately, we will not be using the semilinear system suggested by the decomposition
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}+\mathcal{N}_{\beta}=\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
from Proposition
4.9
; rather, we rewrite system (
1.8
) in the following more pleasant form (which is analogous to (
5.16
)):

(5.53)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot u=-u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta),\\
Au-\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}u+(G-\Delta)\nabla\eta=(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}(\Phi+A(\beta+\eta)u)-u\cdot\nabla u+\mathbb{S}u\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta).\end{cases}

The goal now is to use the linear estimate from Lemma
5.5
, and for this we are tasked with bounding the first equation’s right hand side in
(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the second equation’s in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Estimating the negative Sobolev norm is trivial:

(5.54)

\lVert u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta)\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}}=\lVert\nabla\cdot u\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}.

To estimate the
H^{1}
norm we use Theorem
A.8
(and (
5.49
)):

(5.55)

\lVert u\cdot\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta)\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{4}}\rangle+\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\langle\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}\rangle\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\rangle^{2}.

We then make bounds analogous to (
5.18
), (
5.19
), and (
5.20
):

(5.56)

\lVert(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}(\Phi+A(\beta+\eta)u)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}},\quad\lVert u\cdot\nabla u\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}},

and

(5.57)

\lVert\mathbb{S}u\nabla\log(1+\beta+\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\rangle^{2}.

By combining (
5.54
), (
5.55
), (
5.56
), and (
5.57
) with the bounds of Lemma
5.5
we find that

(5.58)

\lVert u,\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}}\rangle^{2}.

The very same interpolation and absorption argument at the end of the proof of Proposition
5.3
applies here as well and we can close the right hand estimate in (
5.45
).
∎

Our next result shows that if we know some improved integrability on either the velocity or the free surface then we may promote all the up to full weighted control. Recall the notation for solenoidal vector fields introduced in (
3.8
).

Proposition 5.7

(Weighted a priori estimates)
.

Suppose that for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
4/3<q<2
, and
4\leqslant r<\infty
we have
(u,\eta)\in{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
and
\min\{{\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}},\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{r}}}\}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
a solution to identity
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\Phi
for a generic
\Phi\in L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then, for implicit constants depending only on
\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1/2}}}
,
\varepsilon
,
q
,
r
, and the physical parameters, we have the a priori bounds

(5.59)

\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}_{1/2}\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{1/2}}\lesssim 1.

Proof.

The first step is to unpack the unweighted a priori bounds on the solution that we get from Proposition
5.6
. To do this we require Propositions
4.7
and
4.9
to deduce that the functions
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined via

(5.60)

\boldsymbol{u}=(2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2})^{-1/2}u\text{ and }\boldsymbol{\eta}=\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta)

are a solution to system (
1.8
) for
\kappa=1
and forcing
\Phi\in L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. It is clear that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
; therefore, we can invoke Proposition
5.6
to deduce the bounds
\lVert\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{2}}\lesssim 1
. We claim that the same estimate holds for
u
and
\nabla\eta
. We compute that

(5.61)

\nabla\eta=(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{\eta}\nabla\beta,\quad\nabla^{2}\eta=(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta}+\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\otimes\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}+\nabla\beta\otimes\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}+\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\otimes\nabla\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}\nabla^{2}\beta.

Now we use Theorem
A.8
and the bounds
\lVert\eta,\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}\times H^{2}}\lesssim 1
to estimate

(5.62)

\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{L^{2}\times L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1,\quad\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\langle\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\rangle\lVert\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{1}}+\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,4}}+\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{1}}+\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\lesssim 1.

It now follows that
\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim 1
. To obtain the sought after estimates on
u
we use the identity
u=(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\boldsymbol{u}
:

(5.63)

\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert\nabla(\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{H^{1}}+\lVert(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim 1+\langle\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{W^{1,4}}\rangle\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{W^{1,4}}+\langle\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\rangle\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim 1.

Thus, we can indeed transfer the unweighted bounds
\lVert\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{2}}\lesssim 1
to get
\lVert u,\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{2}\times H^{2}}\lesssim 1
. Notice that we have thus far not used the assumption that
\min\{{\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}},\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{r}}}\}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
.

The next step of the proof is to show that if
\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
, then
\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}\lesssim 1
. In other words, knowledge of improved velocity integrability implies finite integrability for the free surface. So let us now assume that
\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
. We look to the semilinearized version of the equations granted by Proposition
4.9
, i.e

(5.64)

\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\Phi-\mathcal{L}_{\beta}(u,\eta)-\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta),

where we recall that
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
are defined in Proposition
3.5
, while
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
is defined in (
4.33
). We take the norm in
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
on both sides of this equation. The
\Phi
contribution is estimated through the embedding
L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
(which holds since
4/3<q<2
). For the left hand side it is the second item of Lemma
5.5
that gives us a coercive bound. This leaves us with

(5.65)

\lVert u,\eta,\nabla\eta\rVert_{W^{2,q}\times L^{2q/(2-q)}\times W^{2,q}}\lesssim 1+\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\beta}(u,\eta)\rVert_{L^{q}}+\lVert\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta)\rVert_{L^{q}}.

The idea is now that each of the expressions on the right consists of products, either with
\beta
or the solution itself, and so by using the assumed
u\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
control paired with the unweighted
L^{2}
control on
u
and
\nabla\eta
each term is then promoted to the improved
L^{q}
integrability.

The expression for
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
consists of four terms. The bound of the first of these is one of two places where the
L^{q}
-integrability of
u
is used (the other is within the expression for
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
). We estimate this term simply as

(5.66)

\Big\lVert\frac{A(\beta-\mathbf{c}(\beta))}{(1+\beta)(1+\mathbf{c}(\beta))}u\Big\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim 1

while the remaining terms in
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
are treated to either Hölder’s inequality or Theorem
A.8
. We have

(5.67)

\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{2}(\nabla\log(1+\beta),u)\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\log(1+\beta)\rVert_{W^{1,2q/(2-q)}}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim 1.

We also use
\beta\in{{}_{\mathrm{e}}}H^{\infty}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(
\delta>0
) with the embedding
L^{2}_{\updelta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{4/(2+\updelta)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and interpolation to bound (for
\updelta<\delta
sufficiently small as to satisfy
0<\updelta<2/q-1
)

(5.68)

\lVert((1+\beta)^{-1}(G-\Delta)\nabla(1+\beta))\eta\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\beta\rVert_{W^{2,\frac{4}{2+\updelta}}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\frac{4q}{4-(2+\updelta)q}}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}^{1-\frac{\updelta q}{4-2q}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{\updelta q}{4-2q}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}^{\theta},

where
\theta=\updelta q(4-2q)^{-1}\in(0,1)
. We bound the final term in
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
according to

(5.69)

\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{1}(\nabla(1+\beta),\nabla((1+\beta)^{-1}\eta))\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\beta\rVert_{W^{1,2q/(2-q)}}\lVert\nabla((1+\beta)^{-1}\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\eta,\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}\times L^{2}}\lesssim 1.

Next, we bound the six expressions within
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
by implementing similar tricks. We have

(5.70)

\Big\lVert\nabla\cdot\Big(\frac{u\otimes u}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}}\Big)\Big\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim\lVert(2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2})^{-1/2}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,2q}}^{2}\lesssim 1.

The second term in
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
is where we shall again use
\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim 1
:

(5.71)

\left\lVert\frac{2Au\eta}{(1+\beta)\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}((1+\beta)+\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}})}}\right\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1.

For the third, fifth, and sixth terms, we just use Theorem
A.8
:

(5.72)

\Big\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{2}\Big(u,\nabla\log\Big(1+\frac{2\eta}{(1+\beta)^{2}}\Big)\Big)\Big\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}\Big\lVert\nabla\log\Big(1+\frac{2\eta}{(1+\beta)^{2}}\Big)\Big\rVert_{W^{1,2q/(2-q)}}\lesssim 1,

(5.73)

\Big\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{1}\Big(\nabla\Big(\frac{2\eta}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)}\Big)\Big)\Big\rVert_{L^{q}}\\
\lesssim\Big\lVert\nabla\Big(\frac{2\eta}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)}\Big)\Big\rVert_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}\Big\lVert\nabla\Big(\frac{2\eta}{\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)}\Big)\Big\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim 1,

and

(5.74)

\left\lVert\nabla\cdot Q_{1}\Big(\nabla(1+\beta),\nabla\Big(\frac{\eta^{2}}{(1+\beta)^{2}\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)((1+\beta)^{2}+\eta)}\Big)\Big)\right\rVert_{L^{q}}\\
\lesssim\lVert\nabla\beta\rVert_{W^{1,2q/(2-q)}}\left\lVert\nabla\Big(\frac{\eta^{2}}{(1+\beta)^{2}\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)((1+\beta)^{2}+\eta)}\Big)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim 1.

For the fourth term we instead argue as in (
5.68
):

(5.75)

\left\lVert\frac{\eta^{2}(G-\Delta)\nabla(1+\beta)}{(1+\beta)^{2}\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta)((1+\beta)^{2}+\eta)}\right\rVert_{L^{q}}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}^{\theta}\lesssim\lVert\eta\rVert^{\theta}_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}.

By synthesizing the above estimates on
\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
and returning to (
5.65
) we obtain

(5.76)

\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}\lesssim 1+\lVert\eta\rVert^{\theta}_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}\quad\text{and so we can absorb in the standard way to get}\quad\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{2q/(2-q)}}\lesssim 1.

This completes the part of the proof in which we show that improved integrability on
u
leads to finite integrability on
\eta
.

We now come to the final step of the proof. In light of the previous step, it now suffices to show that if
\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{r}}\lesssim 1
for some
4\leqslant r<\infty
then we can obtain the weighted estimate (
5.59
). Initially, we need to transform back into the variables
\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
from (
5.60
) as these solve system (
1.8
) which has a more exploitable energy structure. Note that by writing

(5.77)

\boldsymbol{\eta}=2(\sqrt{2\eta+(1+\beta)^{2}}+(1+\beta))^{-1}\eta

we can transfer the
L^{r}
bound on
\eta
to
\boldsymbol{\eta}
:
\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{L^{r}}\lesssim 1
. Let us now define the weight
\rho=1/r\in(0,1/4]
. By utilizing the energy structure of (
1.8
) we show initially that
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
are controlled inclusions. We test the second equation in (
1.8
) with
\langle X\rangle^{2\rho}\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and integrate by parts to deduce the following identity.

(5.78)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle X\rangle^{\rho}\Phi\cdot\langle X\rangle^{\rho}\boldsymbol{u}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle X\rangle^{2\rho}\big[{A|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}+(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\mathbb{S}\boldsymbol{u}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\big]\\
+\rho\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X\cdot\boldsymbol{u}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}+2(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\mathbb{S}\boldsymbol{u}:\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X-2(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})(G-\Delta)\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X.

The first line’s right hand side gives us our weighted control on
\boldsymbol{u}
. We shall need to estimate the terms appearing in the second line. Since
\rho<1/2
we have
\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X\in L^{\infty}
and so thanks to Hölder we have

(5.79)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X\cdot\boldsymbol{u}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}+2(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\mathbb{S}\boldsymbol{u}:\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X\Big|\lesssim\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}}(\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{4}}^{2}+\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{H^{1}})\lesssim 1.

We may also estimate the capillary contribution with ease:

(5.80)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\Delta\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X\Big|\lesssim\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{1}}\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1.

The gravity contribution of (
5.78
) is where the finite integrability on
\boldsymbol{\eta}
and the exponent relation
\frac{2r}{r-2}(1-\rho)>2
are used:

(5.81)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-2G(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X\Big|\lesssim\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{L^{r}}\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\rho-1}\rVert_{L^{2r/(r-2)}}\lesssim\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}.

We combine (
5.78
), (
5.79
), (
5.80
), and (
5.81
) to gain

(5.82)

\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert^{2}_{H^{1}_{\rho}}\lesssim 1+\langle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\rangle\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\quad\text{which leads us to}\quad\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{H^{1}_{\rho}}\lesssim 1.

The next thing to do is use this new weighted bound on
\boldsymbol{u}
to prove a
\rho
-weighted bound on
\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}
; to achieve this we test the equation (
1.8
) with
\langle X\rangle^{2\rho}(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})^{-1}\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and integrate by parts as appropriate to derive the following identity.

(5.83)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle X\rangle^{2\rho}(G\char 12\relax{\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{2}+\char 12\relax{\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})^{-1}\langle X\rangle^{\rho}(\Phi-A\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\langle X\rangle^{\rho}\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}-2\rho\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\otimes\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle X\rangle^{\rho}(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}-\mathbb{S}\boldsymbol{u}):\langle X\rangle^{\rho}\nabla((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})^{-1}\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta})+2\rho(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}-\mathbb{S}\boldsymbol{u}):\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\otimes\langle X\rangle^{2\rho-2}X.

By implementing Hölder’s inequality with the weighted bounds on
\boldsymbol{u}
and
\Phi
, the unweighted bounds on
\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, and

(5.84)

\lVert\nabla((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})^{-1}\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta})\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}+\langle\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\rangle\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{1}_{\rho}}

we derive from (
5.83
) the estimate

(5.85)

\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{1}_{\rho}}^{2}\lesssim 1+\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{1}_{\rho}}\quad\text{and hence}\quad\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{1}_{\rho}}\lesssim 1.

We now have established the control
\lVert\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta}\rVert_{H^{1}_{\rho}\times\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim 1
and it is time to again swap back to the variables
u
and
\eta
determined from the inverse transformation in (
5.60
). From the identity

(5.86)

\eta=2^{-1}((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})^{2}-(1+\beta)^{2})=2^{-1}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{2}+(1+\beta)\boldsymbol{\eta}

and the product estimates of Proposition
2.18
we deduce immediately that
\lVert\eta\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim 1
. Also, by using
u=(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})\boldsymbol{u}
and the boundedness of the product map
W^{1,\infty}\times H^{1}_{\rho}\to H^{1}_{\rho}
we find
\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}_{\rho}}\lesssim 1
.

Next, we write the equation
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)=\Phi
satisfied by
u
and
\eta
into the form (which is permissible by Propositions
3.5
and
4.9
)

(5.87)

(u,\eta)=(\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta})^{-1}(\Phi-\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta))

and so for any weight
\rho\leqslant\widetilde{\rho}\leqslant 1/2
we have the estimate

(5.88)

\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}_{\widetilde{\rho}}\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\widetilde{\rho}}}\lesssim 1+\lVert\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\rho}}}.

We first shall examine (
5.88
) with
\widetilde{\rho}=\rho
so that we may promote our initial weighted estimate up a derivative. Thanks to repeated applications of the known bounds with the product estimates of Proposition
2.7
we may derive an estimate in the spirit of the proof of Proposition
4.9
on the nonlinearity of the form

(5.89)

\lVert\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim 1+\lVert u\rVert_{W^{1,4}_{\rho}}+\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{W^{1,4}_{\rho}}\lesssim 1+\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\rho}u\rVert_{H^{2}}^{1/2}+\lVert\langle X\rangle^{\rho}\nabla\eta\rVert^{1/2}_{H^{2}}.

Then upon combining (
5.88
) and (
5.89
) and implementing the standard absorption argument we arrive at
\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}_{\rho}\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\rho}}\lesssim 1
.

We are finally in a position where we can iterate the conclusion from the section item of Proposition
4.9
. By the
\rho
-weighted estimate we have just derived we find that for every
\widetilde{\rho}<2\rho
we now learn that
\lVert\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(u,\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\rho}}}\lesssim 1
and so through (
5.88
) we gain the control
\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}_{\widetilde{\rho}}\times\widetilde{H}^{2}_{\widetilde{\rho}}}\lesssim 1
. In fact, if we let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
be the smallest positive integer such that
(3/2)^{-n}(1/2)<2\rho
and set for
j\in\{{1,\dots,n}\}
the weight strength
\mathrm{w}_{j}=(3/2)^{-j}(1/2)
. By what we have just argued we know that
\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}_{\mathrm{w}_{n}}\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{w}_{n}}}\lesssim 1
. Furthermore, the combination of (
5.88
) with the aforementioned bounds on
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
show that if for some
j\in\{{2,\dots,n}\}
we know that

(5.90)

\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}_{\mathrm{w}_{j}}\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{w}_{j}}}\lesssim 1\quad\text{then we actually find that}\quad\lVert u,\eta\rVert_{H^{2}_{\mathrm{w}_{j-1}}\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{w}_{j-1}}}\lesssim 1.

Therefore the obvious induction argument leads us at last to the bound (
5.59
).
∎

We now come to the solitary analog of Corollary
5.4
.

Corollary 5.8

(Blowup refinement - solitary case)
.

Let
\mathscr{S}\subset S
be the maximal locally analytic curve of solutions granted by Proposition
5.2
in the solitary case, parametrized by the continuous map
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(u(s),\eta(\eta),\kappa(s))\in\mathscr{S}\subset H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then for every choice of
q\in(4/3,2)
and
r\in[4,\infty)
we have

(5.91)

\lim_{|s|\to\infty}[{\lVert\eta(s)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\min\{{\lVert u(s)\rVert_{L^{q}},\lVert\eta(s)\rVert_{L^{r}}}\}+1/\inf((1+\beta)^{2}+2\eta(s))+|\kappa(s)|}]=\infty.

Proof.

The arguments for the limits
s\to\infty
and
s\to-\infty
are identical, so we only handle the former via the following contradiction argument. If the limit
5.91
were not true, then we could find a sequence
\{{s_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
s_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and a number
1<C<\infty
such that upon setting
u_{n}=u(s_{n})
,
\eta_{n}=\eta(s_{n})
,
\kappa_{n}=\kappa(s_{n})
, and
\varepsilon=C^{-2}\in(0,1)
we have the bound

(5.92)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[{\lVert\eta_{n}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\min\{{\lVert u_{n}\rVert_{L^{q}},\lVert\eta_{n}\rVert_{L^{r}}}\}+1/\inf((1+\beta)^{2}+2\eta_{n})+|\kappa_{n}|}]\leqslant C.

Due to Proposition
5.2
the solution curve
\mathscr{S}
is a subset of
S
from (
4.56
); then, by the third item of Theorem
4.12
we find that the identity
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u_{n},\eta_{n})=\kappa_{n}\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta_{n})
is satisfied for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We are then led to study in conjunction the auxiliary variables
\{{(\boldsymbol{u}_{n},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(5.93)

\boldsymbol{u}_{n}=(2\eta_{n}+(1+\beta)^{2})^{-1/2}u_{n},\quad\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n}=\sqrt{2\eta_{n}+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta),\quad\Phi_{n}=\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta_{n})

which, thanks to Propositions
4.4
and
4.7
solve the system of equations

(5.94)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})\boldsymbol{u}_{n})=0,\\
\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{n})+A\boldsymbol{u}_{n}-\nabla\cdot((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})\mathbb{S}\boldsymbol{u}_{n})+(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})(G-\Delta)\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n}=\Phi_{n},\\
\Phi_{n}=\kappa_{n}[{\Upomega_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})+\nabla((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})\Upomega_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n}))+\Upomega_{\tau}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n}}].\end{cases}

Our first task is to consider the consequences of the unweighted a priori estimates of Proposition
5.6
on the auxiliary variables. We then introduce the real analytic primitive

(5.95)

\widetilde{\tau}:(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to(L^{\infty}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}\cap H^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}),\quad\widetilde{\tau}(z)=\int_{0}^{z}\tau(\mathfrak{z})\;\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{z}

and argue as in Corollary
5.4
(see (
5.29
) and what follows) to see that the maps

(5.96)

\Upomega_{\phi}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\to L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\Upomega_{\psi}:\mathbf{O}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\to L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\Upomega_{\widetilde{\tau}}:\mathbf{O}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\to(L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}\cap L^{\infty}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}})

send bounded sets to bounded sets and obey the identity

(5.97)

\Phi_{n}=\kappa_{n}[{\Upomega_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})+\nabla((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})\Upomega_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n}))+\nabla\cdot(\Upomega_{\widetilde{\tau}}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n}))-\Upomega_{\nabla\cdot\widetilde{\tau}}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n})}].

It then follows that
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\Phi_{n}\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lesssim 1
for an implicit constant depending only on
C
. We are then permitted to invoke the left a priori estimate of equation (
5.45
) to deduce that
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\boldsymbol{u}_{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}_{n}\rVert_{H^{1}\times H^{1}}\lesssim 1
. Now we combine the third item of Lemma
4.3
with the second item of Proposition
4.4
and these new bounds to deduce further that
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\Phi_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}_{1/2}}\lesssim 1
.

With these uniform forcing bounds in the weighted space, we have reached a position where Proposition
5.7
is in play. Since we also know that
\{{(u_{n},\eta_{n})}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{U}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and (
5.92
) are true we can indeed invoke this result to learn that
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert u_{n},\eta_{n}\rVert_{H^{2}_{1/2}\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{1/2}}\lesssim 1
. But now this is a contradiction of the limit (
5.4
) from Proposition
5.2
. Thus, our assumption that (
5.91
) does not hold as
s\to\infty
must be false, and the proof is complete.
∎

6.
Conclusions

The purpose of this section is to complete the proofs of the main results, Theorems
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
, from Section
1.4
.

6.1.
Small solutions

Our analysis thus far has been geared toward understanding the behavior of large solutions to system (
1.8
) with fixed forcing profile constituents
\phi
,
\psi
, and
\tau
determining
\Phi
as in (
1.11
) while increasing the variable
\kappa
. It is also natural to study small solutions with small data and, as it turns out, our previous work is also capable of quickly felling the problem of developing such a theory. In this subsection we will consider
\kappa=1
and study the data to solution map near zero.

We need to introduce some notation for the spaces of forcing data. Let us fix an open and bounded rectangle
R\subset\mathbb{C}
with sides parallel to the real and imaginary axes and center
0\in R
. Given a real-valued function space
\mathcal{Y}
, e.g.
\mathcal{Y}=L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})
or
\mathcal{Y}=L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, with complexification
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{C}}
, we define the function space and norm

(6.1)

\boldsymbol{A}(\mathcal{Y})=\{{\phi\in C^{0}(\overline{R};\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{C}})\;:\;\phi\text{ holomorphic in }R\text{ and }\phi(\mathbb{R}\cap\overline{R})\subset\mathcal{Y}}\},\quad\lVert\phi\rVert_{\boldsymbol{A}(\mathcal{Y})}=\sup_{z\in\overline{R}}\lVert\phi(z)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{C}}}.

A standard argument involving the Cauchy integral formula shows that space
\boldsymbol{A}(\mathcal{Y})
is Banach. We now make the following expansion of Definition
4.10
. Let us denote the spaces
\boldsymbol{Y}
,
\boldsymbol{W}
, and
\boldsymbol{Z}
(in both the periodic and solitary cases) by

(6.2)

\boldsymbol{Y}=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{A}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times\boldsymbol{A}(H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}))\times\boldsymbol{A}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}))&\text{in the }\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\text{ case},\\
\boldsymbol{A}(L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times\boldsymbol{A}(H^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times\boldsymbol{A}(L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}))&\text{in the }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ case},\par\end{cases}

and

(6.3)

\boldsymbol{W}=\begin{cases}H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathring{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})&\text{in the }\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\text{ case},\\
H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\text{in the }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ case},\end{cases}\quad\boldsymbol{Z}=\begin{cases}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})&\text{in the }\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\text{ case},\\
L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})&\text{in the }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ case}.\end{cases}

Note the similarity between the spaces
\boldsymbol{W}
and
\boldsymbol{X}
(where the latter are defined in (
4.52
)). Let us also fix
\iota\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
sufficiently small so that the open set
B_{\boldsymbol{W}}(0,\iota)\subset\boldsymbol{W}
has the property that for all
(u,\eta)\in B_{\boldsymbol{W}}(0,\iota)
we have
1+\beta+\eta>1/2
and
\mathrm{diam}(\overline{\mathrm{img}(\eta)})<\mathrm{diam}(\overline{R}\cap\mathbb{R})/2
. Our last piece of notation we introduce is the following variable data map, which is a generalization of fixed data maps of Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
.

Lemma 6.1

(Variable data map)
.

The map
\mathcal{D}:B_{\boldsymbol{W}}(0,\iota)\times\boldsymbol{Y}\to\boldsymbol{Z}
given via

(6.4)

\mathcal{D}(\eta,(\phi,\psi,\tau))=\Upomega_{\phi}(\eta)+\nabla((1+\beta+\eta)\Upomega_{\psi}(\eta))+\Upomega_{\tau}(\eta)\nabla\eta

is well-defined and real analytic.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of simple product estimates and Corollary
A.2
.
∎

At last we arrive at our main result regarding small data well-posedness of system (
1.8
) with (
1.11
) in both the periodic and solitary cases.

Theorem 6.2

(Theory of small solutions)
.

There exists radii
r_{\boldsymbol{W}},r_{\boldsymbol{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
r_{\boldsymbol{W}}<\iota
and
B_{\boldsymbol{Y}}(0,r_{\boldsymbol{Y}})\subset\boldsymbol{Y}
with the property that there exists a unique function
\text{\char 23\relax}:B_{\boldsymbol{Y}}(0,r_{\boldsymbol{Y}})\to B_{\boldsymbol{W}}(0,r_{\boldsymbol{W}})
that satisfies
\text{\char 23\relax}(0)=0
and for all
(\phi,\psi,\tau)\in B_{\boldsymbol{Y}}(0,r_{\boldsymbol{Y}})
the pair
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})=\text{\char 23\relax}(\phi,\psi,\tau)
are a solution to the system of equations (
1.8
) with
\kappa=1
and
\Phi=\mathcal{D}(\eta,(\phi,\psi,\tau))
. Moreover, the mapping
˚
is real analytic.

Proof.

We will verify these assertions via the implicit function theorem; however, the arguments in the periodic and solitary cases are slightly different, so we must break to cases. For the periodic case we consider the mapping
\digamma:B_{\boldsymbol{W}}(0,\iota)\times\boldsymbol{Y}\to\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times\boldsymbol{Z}
given via

(6.5)

\digamma((u,\eta),(\phi,\psi,\tau))=\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\eta)-(0,(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\eta,(\phi,\psi,\tau))),

where
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
is the operator from Proposition
4.5
. That
\digamma
is well-defined and real analytic is a consequence of the aforementioned result and Lemma
6.1
. Due to the decomposition of
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
from Proposition
4.6
we compute that
D_{1}\digamma(0,0)=\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}
, where the latter two linear maps are studied in Proposition
3.4
. In particular, the final item of that result shows that
\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta}:\boldsymbol{W}\to\mathring{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times\boldsymbol{Z}
is an isomorphism. Therefore, the implicit function theorem applies for the map
\digamma
and we obtain the claimed radii and the unique and analytic map
˚
satisfying
\digamma(\text{\char 23\relax}(\phi,\psi,\tau),(\phi,\psi,\tau))=0
for all
(\phi,\psi,\tau)\in B_{\boldsymbol{Y}}(0,r_{\boldsymbol{Y}})
. Thanks to
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
being an operator encoding of the left hand side of the PDE (
1.8
) we easily deduce that
˚
has the claimed solution operator properties.

Next, we prove the result in the solitary case. First recall that the space
\boldsymbol{X}
defined in (
4.52
) has the same norm as
\boldsymbol{W}
but enforces a diverge free condition on its first component; in particular,
B_{\boldsymbol{W}}(0,r)\cap\boldsymbol{X}=B_{\boldsymbol{X}}(0,r)
for
r>0
. In the solitary case we use
\boldsymbol{X}
to initially define the operator
\digamma:B_{\boldsymbol{X}}(0,\iota)\times\boldsymbol{Y}\to\boldsymbol{Z}
given via

(6.6)

\digamma((u,\eta),(\phi,\psi,\tau))=\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u,\mathcal{T}_{\beta}(\eta))-\mathcal{D}(\eta,(\phi,\psi,\tau))

where
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
is the solitary encoding operator from Proposition
4.7
and
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}
is the analytic change of unknowns from Lemma
4.3
. These two results combine with Lemma
6.1
to show that
\digamma
is well-defined and real analytic. We then compute (using the final item of Proposition
4.9
) that
D_{1}\digamma(0,0)(u,\eta)=(\mathcal{P}_{\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{\beta})(u,(1+\beta)\eta)
, so by Proposition
3.5
we see that
D_{1}\digamma(0,0)
is the composition of invertible maps. We have now verified the hypotheses of the implicit function theorem, so it grants us
\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{X}},\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\leqslant\iota
and a unique and analytic mapping
\text{\char 27\relax}:B_{\boldsymbol{Y}}(0,\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{Y}})\to B_{\boldsymbol{X}}(0,\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{X}})
with the property that for all
(\phi,\psi,\tau)\in B_{\boldsymbol{Y}}(0,\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{Y}})
we have
\digamma(\text{\char 27\relax}(\phi,\psi,\tau),(\phi,\psi,\tau))=0
. We now define the map
\text{\char 23\relax}:B_{\boldsymbol{Y}}(0,\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{Y}})\to B_{\boldsymbol{W}}(0,\iota)
via
\text{\char 23\relax}(\phi,\psi,\tau)=(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})
for
\boldsymbol{u}=(1+\beta+\eta)^{-1}u
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\eta
where
(u,\eta)=\text{\char 27\relax}(\phi,\psi,\tau)
. By the properties of
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}
we deduce that
˚
is indeed a solution operator to the PDE (
1.8
); moreover, by restricting its domain and codomain as appropriate balls
B_{\boldsymbol{Y}}(0,r_{\boldsymbol{Y}})
and
B_{\boldsymbol{W}}(0,r_{\boldsymbol{W}})\subset B_{\boldsymbol{W}}(0,\iota)
it is seen to inherit the uniqueness assertion from
œ
. Finally,
˚
is real analytic as it is the composition of real analytic maps.
∎

6.2.
Large solutions

Our goal here is to summarize and recontextualize the results of Section
5
. Previously, we have handled the solitary case of system (
1.8
) through the change of unknowns described in, e.g., equation (
5.60
). It is important to restate our results in terms of the natural variables of the parent system. We do this in a unified fashion for both the periodic and solitary cases.

Given any choice of real analytic data map constituents (as in Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
)

(6.7)

\text{\char 107\relax}=(\phi,\psi,\tau):(-a_{\beta},\infty)\to\begin{cases}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times(L^{\infty}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}}),\\
L^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times(L^{\infty}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}\cap H^{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2})(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{sym}})\end{cases}

we form the corresponding solution space (analogous to (
4.56
))

(6.8)

S_{\text{\char 107\relax}}=\{{(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta},\kappa)\in\boldsymbol{W}\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;\inf(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta})>0,\;\text{system~\eqref{stationary nondimensionalized equations} is satisfied by the triple with }\Phi=\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{\eta},\text{\char 107\relax})}\},

where we have extended the definition of
\mathcal{D}
as in (
6.4
) to act on such
\boldsymbol{\eta}
and
k
. Notice that there exists some rectangle
Q
as in the definitions (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) such that the restriction of the unique maximal analytic extension satisfies
\text{\char 107\relax}\in\boldsymbol{Y}
. It is in this sense in which the following result discusses the nature of the solution operator from Theorem
6.2
past its local definition. We shall show that the set (
6.8
) either contains solutions with arbitrarily large forcing strength, free surfaces arbitrarily close to touching the bottom, or arbitrarily ‘large’ solutions. In the periodic case we can take ‘large’ to mean the maximum height of the free surface while in the solitary case ‘large’ refers to the sum of this aforementioned quantity with a quantification of the solution’s locality.

Theorem 6.3

(Theory of large solutions)
.

There exists a locally analytic curve
\mathscr{S}_{\text{\char 107\relax}}\subseteq S_{\text{\char 107\relax}}
with
0\in\mathscr{S}_{\text{\char 107\relax}}
that is parametrized by a continuous mapping
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(\boldsymbol{u}(s),\boldsymbol{\eta}(s),\kappa(s))\in\mathscr{S}_{\text{\char 107\relax}}
and satisfies
0\mapsto 0
and for every choice of
q\in(4/3,2)
and
r\in[4,\infty)

(6.9)

\begin{cases}\lim_{|s|\to\infty}[{\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+(\inf(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)))^{-1}+|\kappa(s)|}]=\infty&\text{in the }\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\text{ case},\\
