\lim_{|s|\to\infty}[{\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\min\{{\lVert\boldsymbol{u}(s)\rVert_{L^{q}},\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)\rVert_{L^{r}}}\}+(\inf(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)))^{-1}+|\kappa(s)|}]=\infty&\text{in the }\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ case}.\end{cases}

Proof.

In the periodic case the result follows immediately from combining Proposition
5.2
and Corollary
5.4
. It remains to handle the solitary case.

Proposition
5.2
grants us a continuous and locally analytic solution curve
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(u(s),\eta(s))\in{{}_{\mathrm{sol}}}H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
which (according to Theorem
4.12
) satisfies
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}(u(s),\eta(s))=\kappa(s)\mathcal{F}_{\beta}(\eta(s))
(where
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}
are defined in Propositions
4.4
and
4.7
) and (thanks to Corollary
5.8
) obeys the limits (
5.91
). We define the new curve
\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(\boldsymbol{u}(s),\boldsymbol{\eta}(s))\in H^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbf{O}_{\beta}\cap\widetilde{H}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
via

(6.10)

\boldsymbol{u}(s)=(2\eta(s)+(1+\beta)^{2})^{-1/2}u(s),\quad\boldsymbol{\eta}(s)=\sqrt{2\eta(s)+(1+\beta)^{2}}-(1+\beta)

and note that this is also manifestly continuous and locally analytic. Due to the third item of Proposition
4.7
and the definition of the map
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
from (
4.13
) it is evident that for each
s\in\mathbb{R}
we have
(\boldsymbol{u}(s),\boldsymbol{\eta}(s))\in S_{\text{\char 107\relax}}
where we recall that the latter set is defined in (
6.8
).

To complete the proof we only need to verify that the second limit in (
6.9
) holds along this curve. We examine the inverse identities of (
6.10
), namely:

(6.11)

u(s)=(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}(s))\boldsymbol{u}(s),\quad\eta(s)=((1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}(s))^{2}-(1+\beta)^{2})/2.

Now, if the claimed limit were false then we could find
\{{s_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}
with
s_{n}\to\pm\infty
as
n\to\infty
with

(6.12)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[{\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s_{n})\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\min\{{\lVert\boldsymbol{u}(s_{n})\rVert_{L^{q}},\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}(s_{n})\rVert_{L^{r}}}\}+(\inf(1+\beta+\boldsymbol{\eta}(s_{n})))^{-1}+|\kappa(s_{n})|}]<\infty.

We may then insert this information into (
6.11
) to contradict the limit from Corollary
5.8
. Thus, our assumption that the claimed limit does not hold is false.
∎

Appendix A
Tools from analysis

We record here important results from analysis that are used throughout the paper.

A.1.
Analytic composition in unital Banach algebras

We begin by setting some notation. Recall that if
\mathcal{X}
is a unital Banach algebra over
\mathbb{C}
(with unit
1
) then the spectrum of an element
x\in\mathcal{X}
is the set

(A.1)

\mathrm{spec}(x)=\{{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\;:\;\lambda-x\text{ is not invertible}}\}.

The set
\mathrm{spec}(x)
is nonempty and compact, and on the complement the resolvent map

(A.2)

\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{spec}(x)\ni\lambda\mapsto(\lambda-x)^{-1}\in\mathcal{X}

is holomorphic (see, for example, Theorem 1.4, Lemma 1.5, and Proposition 1.6 in Folland
[
24
]
). Given an open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{C}
we let

(A.3)

\mathcal{X}(U)=\{{x\in\mathcal{X}\;:\;\mathrm{spec}(x)\subset U}\}.

The set
\mathcal{X}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{X}
is open, as shown, for instance, in Theorem 10.20 in Rudin
[
57
]
.

The main abstract tool of this appendix is the holomorphic functional calculus, which is otherwise known as the Dunford operational calculus. We record the form of these ideas that are most useful to us in the following result. We write
C^{\omega}(\mathfrak{A};\mathfrak{B})
to denote the space of complex analytic mappings defined on
\mathfrak{A}
with values in
\mathfrak{B}
.

Theorem A.1

(Holomorphic functional calculus)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{X}
is a unital Banach algebra and
\mathcal{Y}
is a Banach space, both over
\mathbb{C}
. Suppose further that we have an embedding of unital Banach algebras
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y})
. Let
\varnothing\neq U\subset\mathbb{C}
be open and simply connected. Then there exists a unique linear map
\Upomega:C^{\omega}(U;\mathcal{Y})\to C^{\omega}(\mathcal{X}(U);\mathcal{Y})
, written
\Phi\mapsto\Upomega_{\Phi}
, obeying the following properties.

(1)

If
P(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{k}Y_{n}z^{n}
for
z\in U
and
\{{Y_{n}}\}_{n=0}^{k}\subset\mathcal{Y}
, then

(A.4)

\Upomega_{P}(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{k}x^{n}Y_{n}\text{ for }x\in\mathcal{X}(U).

(2)

For all
x\in\mathcal{X}(U)
there exists a constant
C_{x}<\infty
and a compact set
\varnothing\neq K_{x}\subset U
such that for all
\Phi\in C^{\omega}(U;\mathcal{Y})
we have the estimate

(A.5)

\lVert\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}\leqslant C_{x}\max_{z\in K_{x}}\lVert\Phi(z)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}.

Proof.

Let us begin by proving uniqueness. Suppose that
\Upomega^{i}
, for
i\in\{{1,2}\}
, are linear maps
C^{\omega}(U;\mathcal{Y})\to C^{\omega}(\mathcal{X}(U);\mathcal{Y})
satisfying the first and second items above. We shall establish that for all
\Phi\in C^{\omega}(U;\mathcal{Y})
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}(U)
we have the equality
\Upomega^{1}_{\Phi}(x)=\Upomega^{2}_{\Phi}(x)
. To this end, fix such
x
and
\Phi
. Due to the second item’s estimate (
A.5
) we may select a constant
C_{x}<\infty
and a compact set
K_{x}\subset U
which work for both of the
\Upomega^{i}
.

The domain
U
is simply connected, so by the
\mathcal{Y}
-valued variant of Runge’s theorem (the proof of the standard
\mathbb{C}
-valued version in Chapter 12 of Remmert
[
55
]
also works for
\mathcal{Y}
-valued maps once one establishes the Cauchy integral formula for these) there exists a sequence of polynomials
\{{P_{\ell}}\}_{\ell=0}^{\infty}
mapping
\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{Y}
that satisfy
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\max_{z\in K_{x}}\lVert P_{\ell}(z)-\Phi(z)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}=0
. Due to the first item’s equation (
A.4
) we must have that
\Upomega_{P_{\ell}}^{1}(x)=\Upomega_{P_{\ell}}^{2}(x)
for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. In turn, we may use the second item and linearity of
\Upomega
to estimate

(A.6)

\lVert\Upomega^{1}_{\Phi}(x)-\Upomega^{2}_{\Phi}(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}\leqslant\lVert\Upomega^{1}_{\Phi}(x)-\Upomega_{P_{\ell}}^{1}(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}+\lVert\Upomega^{2}_{P_{\ell}}(x)-\Upomega^{2}_{\Phi}(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}\leqslant 2C_{x}\max_{z\in K_{x}}\lVert P_{\ell}(z)-\Phi(z)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}.

Sending
\ell\to\infty
completes the proof of uniqueness.

Next, we turn our attention to the existence of the map
\Upomega
. The idea is based on the Cauchy integral formula, so we first need a family of compact contours. By the Riemann mapping theorem there exists a biholomorphic function
\Psi:B(0,1)\to U
. Given any
\mu\in(0,1)
we then let
\Gamma_{\mu}\subset U
be the compact, counterclockwise oriented contour whose image agrees with
\Psi(\partial B(0,\mu))
. If
x\in\mathcal{X}(U)
then there exists
\mu_{x}\in(0,1)
such that
\mathrm{spec}(x)\subset\overline{\Psi(B(0,\mu_{x}))}
; we then define
\Upomega
via the Bochner contour integral

(A.7)

\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}}(z-x)^{-1}\Phi(z)\;\mathrm{d}z,\quad\Phi\in C^{\omega}(U;\mathcal{Y}).

By (
A.2
) the integrand of (
A.7
) is a
\mathcal{Y}
-valued holomorphic function on the domain
U\setminus\mathrm{spec}(x)
. Therefore the choice of the contour
\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}
is not so important in (
A.7
), as the contour integral has the same value when taken over any simple closed curve winding once around
\mathrm{spec}(x)
within
U
(see, e.g., Chapter VII of Dunford and Schwartz
[
22
]
for arguments in a similar context that may establish this fact).

Armed with the definition (
A.7
), the estimate of the second item holds by simply taking the norm of the integral with

(A.8)

K_{x}=\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}\text{ and }C_{x}=\widetilde{C}\cdot\mathrm{length}(\Gamma_{\mu_{x}})\cdot\max_{z\in\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}}\lVert(z-x)^{-1}\rVert_{\mathcal{X}},

where
\widetilde{C}
is the embedding constant for
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}(Y)
.

To prove that
\Upomega
takes values in
C^{\omega}(\mathcal{X}(U);\mathcal{Y})
we may argue in a standard way by expanding into power series (see, e.g., Proposition 10 in Section 4 of Chapter 1 in Bourbaki
[
7
]
) to prove that for each
\Phi
, the map
x\mapsto\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)
is analytic.

To complete the proof, we need establish (
A.4
) for any polynomial
P:\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{Y}
. This fact also follows from classical arguments; we refer the reader to, for example, the proof of Theorem 10.25 in Rudin
[
57
]
.
∎

It will occasionally be useful to examine the holomorphic functional calculus
\Upomega
when drawing the holomorphic maps
\Phi
from a Banach space. Given an open and simply connected set
\varnothing\neq U\subset\mathbb{C}
and a Banach space
\mathcal{Y}
we note that as a consequence of the Cauchy integral formula the normed function space

(A.9)

\boldsymbol{A}(\overline{U};\mathcal{Y})=C^{0}(\overline{U};\mathcal{Y})\cap C^{\omega}(U;\mathcal{Y})\text{ with norm }\lVert\Phi\rVert_{\boldsymbol{A}}=\sup_{z\in\overline{U}}\lVert\Phi(z)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}

is complete.

Corollary A.2

(Joint analyticity)
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
A.1
the induced map
\boldsymbol{A}(\overline{U};\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{X}(U)\ni(\Phi,x)\mapsto\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)\in\mathcal{Y}
is analytic.

Proof.

Analyticity will be verified by showing the agreement of the stated map with a convergent power series. Let us fix
x\in\mathcal{X}(U)
and let
\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}
be the contour used in the Cauchy integral formula (
A.7
). There exists a
\delta>0
such that for all
y\in\overline{B_{\mathcal{X}}(0,\delta_{0})}
we have that
x+y\in\mathcal{X}(U)
, the contour
\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}
winds once around
\mathrm{spec}(x+y)
,

(A.10)

\mathrm{dist}(\Gamma_{\mu_{x}},\mathrm{spec}(x+y))\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{dist}(\Gamma_{\mu_{x}},\mathrm{spec}(x)),\text{ and }\lVert y\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y})}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\max_{z\in\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}}\lVert(z-x)^{-1}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y})}.

Now let
\Phi,\phi\in\boldsymbol{A}(\overline{U};\mathcal{Y})
. When paired with the above estimates, the contour invariance of (
A.7
) yields the power series representation

(A.11)

\Upomega_{\Phi+\phi}(x+y)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}y^{n}\int_{\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}}(z-x)^{-(n+1)}(\Phi+\phi)(z)\;\mathrm{d}z\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}}(z-x)^{-1}\Phi(z)\;\mathrm{d}z+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}}(z-x)^{-n}(y^{n}(z-x)^{-1}\Phi(z)+y^{n-1}\phi(z))\;\mathrm{d}z,

where in the last equality we have re-indexed the sum to make evident the multilinear maps acting on
(\phi,y)
. In fact, by taking the norm in
\mathcal{Y}
and using estimate (
A.10
) and the definition of the norm (
A.9
), we readily deduce that above series is uniformly absolutely convergent for all
y\in\overline{B_{\mathcal{X}}(0,\delta_{0})}
and for all
\phi\in\overline{B_{\boldsymbol{A}}(0,1)}
.
∎

We now aim to specialize to the case in which the Banach algebras
\mathcal{X}
consists of continuous functions. For this we will need to impose the auxiliary conditions of the following definition.

Definition A.3

(Admissibility)
.

We say that a unital Banach algebra
\mathcal{X}
over
\mathbb{C}
is admissible if

(1)

\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow(L^{\infty}\cap C^{0})(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})

(2)

For all
x\in\mathcal{X}
it holds that
\mathrm{spec}(x)=\overline{\mathrm{img}(x)}
(where the former set is the spectrum as in (
A.1
) and the set
\mathrm{img}(x)
denotes the forward image of
x
), and for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathrm{img}(x)}
the resolvent
(z-x)^{-1}\in\mathcal{X}
agrees with the function
\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni\xi\mapsto(z-x(\xi))^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}
.

(3)

There exists a locally bounded function
\mathbf{C}:[0,\infty)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
increasing in both arguments such that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{spec}(x)
we have the resolvent estimate

(A.12)

\lVert(z-x)^{-1}\rVert_{\mathcal{X}}\leqslant\mathbf{C}(\mathrm{dist}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathrm{img}(x),z)^{-1},\lVert x\rVert_{\mathcal{X}}).

To get a sense of the utility of this definition, suppose that
\mathcal{X}
is admissible and let
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}
, which allows us to apply Theorem
A.1
with the natural embedding
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
. Then for a polynomial map
P(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{k}Y_{n}z^{n}
as in the first item of the theorem, we have that
\Upomega_{P}:\mathcal{X}(U)\to\mathcal{X}
is analytic, and is in fact also a polynomial map:
\Upomega_{P}(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{k}x^{n}Y_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
. In particular,
[{\Upomega_{P}(x)}](\xi)=\sum_{n=0}^{k}(x(\xi))^{n}Y_{n}(\xi)
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. On the other hand, the composition
P\circ x:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{X}
satisfies
[{P\circ x}](\xi)=\sum_{n=0}^{k}(x(\xi))^{n}Y_{n}
. We thus arrive at the identity

(A.13)

[{\Upomega_{P}(x)}](\xi)=P(x(\xi))(\xi),

which shows that
x\mapsto\Upomega_{P}(x)
is a Nemytskii operator in this special case. Our goal now is to show that this realization of the abstract holomorphic functional calculus of Theorem
A.1
as Nemytskii operators carries over to a more general context.

In what follows we say that
\{{S_{\epsilon}}\}_{0<\epsilon<1}
are standard regularizing operators if there exists a continuous and compactly supported function
\text{\char 21\relax}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\text{\char 21\relax}=1
such that for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
, for a finite dimensional complex vector space
V
, we have the formula

(A.14)

(S_{\epsilon}f)(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\epsilon^{-d}\text{\char 21\relax}(\epsilon^{-1}(\xi-\eta))f(\xi)\;\mathrm{d}\xi.

We note that there exists positive constants
C
and
c
such that we have pointwise bound

(A.15)

\sup_{0<\epsilon<1}\char 12\relax{S_{\epsilon}f(\xi)}\leqslant C\mathcal{M}_{\leqslant c}[{f}](\xi)

for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
\mathcal{M}_{\leqslant c}
denotes the maximal function taken over balls of radius at most
c
.

Proposition A.4

(Complex analytic Nemytskii operators)
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional complex vector space, let
\mathcal{Y}
be a complex Banach space for which we have the continuous embedding
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
, and let
\mathcal{X}
be an admissible unital Banach algebra in the sense of Definition
A.3
. Assume that the pointwise product mapping
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is continuous. Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be nonempty, open, and simply connected with
\Phi:U\to\mathcal{Y}
holomorphic. For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
we denote
U_{\varepsilon}=\{{z\in U\;:\;\mathrm{dist}(z,\partial U)>\varepsilon}\}
. The following hold.

(1)

For all
x\in\mathcal{X}(U)
there exists
\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)\in\mathcal{Y}
such that for any choice of standard regularizing operators
\{{S_{\epsilon}}\}_{0<\epsilon<1}
and any sequence
\{{\epsilon_{n}}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\subset(0,1)
with
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
we have that for almost every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(A.16)

\lim_{n\to\infty}[{S_{\epsilon_{n}}\Phi}](x(\xi))(\xi)=[{\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)}](\xi).

(2)

The induced Nemytskii map
\Upomega_{\Phi}:\mathcal{X}(U)\to\mathcal{Y}
is complex analytic.

(3)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restricted mapping
\Upomega_{\Phi}:\mathcal{X}(U_{\varepsilon})\to\mathcal{Y}
maps bounded sets to bounded sets.

(4)

For each
x\in\mathcal{X}(U)
and
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
there exits
\delta>0
such that in the set
B(\xi_{0},\delta)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have the a.e. equality

(A.17)

\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{n!}(x-x(\xi_{0}))^{n}\Phi^{(n)}(x(\xi_{0})),

where
\Phi^{(n)}(z)\in\mathcal{Y}
denotes the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
derivative of
\Phi
at
z\in U
, and the series on the right is absolutely convergent in
L^{1}(B(\xi_{0},\delta);V)
and almost everywhere pointwise absolutely convergent.

(5)

Suppose we have the continuous embedding
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
, where the latter space denotes
C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
endowed with the Fréchet structure of local uniform convergence. Then

(A.18)

[{\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)}](\xi)=\Phi(x(\xi))(\xi)\text{ for all }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }x\in\mathcal{X}(U).

Proof.

The boundedness of the product map
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
yields the embedding
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y})
. Theorem
A.1
then grants us a unique linear Dunford calculus map
\Upomega:C^{\omega}(U;\mathcal{Y})\to C^{\omega}(\mathcal{X}(U);\mathcal{Y})
which satisfies the first and second items of that aforementioned result; from this map we define
\Upomega_{\Phi}\in C^{\omega}(\mathcal{X}(U);\mathcal{Y})
, which automatically satisfies the analyticity assertion of the second item.

We now prove the third item. The contour invariance of (
A.7
) and equation (
A.8
), when combined with the resolvent estimates (
A.12
) from Definition
A.3
, yield the bound for

(A.19)

\lVert\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}\leqslant\widetilde{C}\cdot\mathrm{length}(\Gamma_{\mu(\varepsilon)})\cdot\mathbf{C}(2/\varepsilon,\lVert x\rVert_{\mathcal{X}})\text{ for }\varepsilon\in(0,1)\text{ and }x\in\mathcal{X}(U_{\varepsilon}),

where
\Gamma_{\mu(\varepsilon)}
is the contour constructed in the proof of Theorem
A.1
corresponding to
\mu=\mu(\varepsilon)
with
\mu(\varepsilon)\in(0,1)
chosen sufficiently close to
1
so that
\Gamma_{\mu(\varepsilon)}\cap U_{\varepsilon/2}=\varnothing
. Armed with (
A.19
), the third item now follows.

We next prove the first item. Fix a sequence
\{{S_{\varepsilon}}\}_{0<\varepsilon<1}
of standard regularizing operators and also fix a closed cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Due to the embedding
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
we find that the maps
S_{\varepsilon}\Phi|_{Q}:U\to C^{0}(Q;V)
are analytic, where for
\xi\in Q
and
z\in U
we have denoted

(A.20)

[{S_{\varepsilon}\Phi|_{Q}(z)}](\xi)=[{S_{\varepsilon}(\Phi(z))}](\xi).

As the evaluation maps are bounded linear operators
C^{0}(Q;V)\to V
we find that by composition for each
\xi\in Q
the map

(A.21)

U\ni z\mapsto[{S_{\varepsilon}\Phi|_{Q}(z)}](\xi)\in V

is also analytic. Therefore, the Cauchy integral formula applies and we learn that for all
\mu\in(0,1)
and all
w\in U
that are surrounded by the contour
\Gamma_{\mu}
, the identity

(A.22)

[{S_{\varepsilon}\Phi|_{Q}(w)}](\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mu}}[{S_{\varepsilon}\Phi(z)}](\xi)(z-w)^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}z,\quad\xi\in Q

holds. We let
x\in\mathcal{X}(U)
, suppose that
\mu=\mu_{x}
(as defined in the proof of Theorem
A.1
), take
w=x(\xi)
, and reinterpret the result (
A.22
) as a
C^{0}(Q;V)
-valued Bochner integral; this procedure yields the identity

(A.23)

\mathrm{diag}[{(S_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\circ x|_{Q}}]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mu}}S_{\varepsilon}\Phi(z)(z-x)^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}z\in C^{0}(Q;V)

where
\mathrm{diag}[{(S_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\circ x|_{Q}}](\xi)=(S_{\varepsilon}\Phi)(x(\xi))(\xi)
for
\xi\in Q
.

We now consider the limit of (
A.23
) as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Due to the embedding
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
we find that the right hand side integrand converges pointwise to
z\mapsto\Phi(z)(z-x)^{-1}
in the space
L^{1}(Q;V)
while also remaining bounded in norm by a constant function. The dominated convergence theorem now applies and we thus acquire that both sides of (
A.23
) have a limit in the space
L^{1}(Q;V)
, namely:

(A.24)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathrm{diag}[{(S_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\circ x|_{Q}}]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mu}}\Phi(z)(z-x)^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}z=\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)|_{Q}\in L^{1}(Q;V).

The final equality above follows from (
A.7
). This establishes that the limit in (
A.16
) holds in the space
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
. We now wish to establish pointwise almost everywhere convergence as well.

Let us fix again a closed cube
Q
and sequence
\{{\varepsilon_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{0,1}
with
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. We consider the measurable sets indexed by
m\in\mathbb{N}

(A.25)

F_{m}=\{{\xi\in Q\;:\;\limsup_{n,k\to\infty}\char 12\relax{S_{\varepsilon_{n}}\Phi(x(\xi))(\xi)-S_{\varepsilon_{k}}\Phi(x(\xi))(\xi)}>2^{-m}}\}.

Thanks to identity (
A.23
) we are free to estimate for any
\xi\in Q
and
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
:

(A.26)

\char 12\relax{S_{\varepsilon_{n}}\Phi(x(\xi))(\xi)-S_{\varepsilon_{k}}\Phi(x(\xi))(\xi)}\leqslant\frac{\mathrm{length}(\Gamma_{\mu})}{2\pi\mathrm{dist}(\Gamma_{\mu},\mathrm{img}(x))}\int_{0}^{1}\char 12\relax{(S_{\varepsilon_{n}}-S_{\varepsilon_{k}})\Phi(\gamma(\tau))(\xi)}\;\mathrm{d}\tau

where
\gamma
is a
\mathrm{length}(\Gamma_{\mu})
-speed parametrization of
\Gamma_{\mu}
. Now for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
we make the further estimates:

(A.27)

\int_{0}^{1}\char 12\relax{(S_{\varepsilon_{n}}-S_{\varepsilon_{k}})\Phi(\gamma(\tau))(\xi)}\;\mathrm{d}\tau\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\char 12\relax{(S_{\varepsilon_{n}}-S_{\varepsilon_{k}})S_{\varepsilon_{N}}\Phi(\gamma(\tau))(\xi)}\;\mathrm{d}\tau\\
+\int_{0}^{1}\char 12\relax{S_{\varepsilon_{n}}(1-S_{\varepsilon_{N}})\Phi(\gamma(\tau))(\xi)}\;\mathrm{d}\tau+\int_{0}^{1}\char 12\relax{S_{\varepsilon_{k}}(1-S_{\varepsilon_{N}})\Phi(\gamma(\tau))(\xi)}\;\mathrm{d}\tau.

Now we combine (
A.26
) and (
A.27
), take the limit superior as
n
and
k
tend to infinity, and use continuity of
S_{\varepsilon_{N}}\Phi
to deduce

(A.28)

\limsup_{n,k\to\infty}\char 12\relax{S_{\varepsilon_{n}}\Phi(x(\xi))(\xi)-S_{\varepsilon_{k}}\Phi(x(\xi))(\xi)}\leqslant\frac{\mathrm{length}(\Gamma_{\mu})}{\pi\mathrm{dist}(\Gamma_{\mu},\mathrm{img}(x))}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{1}\char 12\relax{S_{\varepsilon_{n}}(1-S_{\varepsilon_{N}})\Phi(\gamma(\tau))(\xi)}\;\mathrm{d}\tau\\
\leqslant C\mathcal{M}_{\leqslant c}\Big[{\int_{0}^{1}\char 12\relax{(1-S_{\varepsilon_{N}})\Phi(\gamma(\tau))(\cdot)}\;\mathrm{d}\tau}\Big](\xi),

where
\mathcal{M}_{\leqslant c}
is the maximal function over balls of radius at most
c
- see (
A.15
) - and
C
is a constant depending on
V
,
Q
,
x
,
\Gamma_{\mu}
, and
\{{S_{\varepsilon}}\}_{0<\varepsilon<1}
. From the above we may return to
F_{m}
from (
A.25
) and invoke the Hardy–Littlewood maximal inequality and gain the estimate

(A.29)

|F_{m}|\leqslant C\cdot 2^{m}\int_{Q+B(0,c)}\int_{0}^{1}\char 12\relax{(1-S_{\varepsilon_{N}})\Phi(\gamma(\tau))(\xi)}\;\mathrm{d}\tau\;\mathrm{d}\xi.

This constant above is independent of
N
and so we may send
N\to\infty
and deduce that
|F_{m}|=0
. As this holds for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
we then deduce that for almost every
\xi\in Q
the sequence
\{{S_{\varepsilon_{n}}\Phi(x(\xi))(\xi)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset V
is Cauchy and hence convergent. The
L^{1}(Q;V)
-convergence in equation (
A.24
) forces the a.e. limit of the sequence
\{{\xi\mapsto S_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x(\xi))(\xi)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
L^{1}(Q;V)
-functions to agree with
\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)
. This completes the proof of the first item.

We now prove the fourth item. Let us fix an
x\in\mathcal{O}
and
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The function
\Phi:U\to\mathcal{Y}
is analytic near
x(\xi_{0})\in U
while
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
and so there exists
\delta_{1}>0
such that
\overline{B(x(\xi_{0}),2\delta_{1})}\Subset U
and for all
z\in\overline{B(x(\xi_{0}),2\delta_{1})}
we have the identity

(A.30)

\Phi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-x(\xi_{0}))^{n}\frac{\Phi^{(n)}(x(\xi_{0}))}{n!}\in L^{1}(B(x(\xi_{0}),2\delta_{1});V)

where the series is uniformly absolutely convergent in the space
L^{1}(B(x(\xi_{0}),2\delta_{1});V)
. Thanks to the embedding
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
we have that
x
is continuous at
\xi_{0}
and so there exists
0<\delta<\delta_{1}
with the property that if
\xi\in B(\xi_{0},\delta)
, then
|x(\xi)-x(\xi_{0})|<\delta_{1}/2
.

Now we consider the formula (
A.7
) for
\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)
and let
E\subseteq B(\xi_{0},\delta)
be any measurable subset; upon integrating over
E
, invoking Fubini’s theorem, and using invariance of choice of contour we find

(A.31)

\int_{E}\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}}\int_{E}\Phi(z)(\xi)(z-x(\xi))^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\;\mathrm{d}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial B(x(\xi_{0}),\delta_{1})}\int_{E}\Phi(z)(\xi)(z-x(\xi))^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\;\mathrm{d}z.

Implicitly we have used that the function

(A.32)

U\setminus\overline{B(x(\xi_{0}),\delta_{1}/2)}\ni z\mapsto\int_{E}\Phi(z)(\xi)(z-x(\xi))^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\in V

is holomorphic thanks to
E\subseteq B(\xi_{0},\delta)
and so the change of contour in (
A.31
) is valid. Now in the final integral of (
A.31
) due to
z\in\partial B(x(\xi_{0}),\delta_{1})\Subset B(x(\xi_{0}),2\delta_{1})
we can replace
\Phi(z)
by its local expansion (
A.30
). Once this is done, we get

(A.33)

\int_{E}\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)=\int_{E}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\Phi^{(n)}(x(\xi_{0}))(\xi)\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial B(x(\xi_{0}),\delta_{1})}(z-x(\xi_{0}))^{n}(z-x(\xi))^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}z\;\mathrm{d}\xi.

The Cauchy integral formula allows us to equate for every
\xi\in E
:

(A.34)

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial B(x(\xi_{0}),\delta_{1})}(z-x(\xi_{0}))^{n}(z-x(\xi))^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}z=(x(\xi)-x(\xi_{0}))^{n}

and hence the combination of (
A.33
) and (
A.34
) gives

(A.35)

\int_{E}\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)=\int_{E}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{n!}(x-x(\xi_{0}))^{n}\Phi^{(n)}(x(\xi_{0})).

Since this holds for every such
E\subseteq B(\xi_{0},\delta)
, the claims of the fourth item follow.

At last, we justify the fifth item. Due to the embedding
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the linear map given by evaluation at
\xi
is continuous. In turn, we may compose formula (
A.7
) (for any
x\in\mathcal{X}(U)
) with any such bounded linear evaluation map and then invoke the Cauchy integral formula to equate

(A.36)

[{\Upomega_{\Phi}(x)}](\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mu_{x}}}\Phi(z)(\xi)(z-x(\xi))^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}z=\Phi(x(\xi))(\xi).

This completes the proof.

∎

We next specialize the previous result to the case of real analytic functions. If
\mathcal{X}
is an admissible unital Banach algebra in the sense of Definition
A.3
we denote the
\mathbb{R}
-subspace of
\mathbb{R}
-valued functions according to
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}=\{{x\in\mathcal{X}\;:\;\mathrm{img}(x)\subset\mathbb{R}}\}
; further, if
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is an open interval then we define the
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}-
open set
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)=\{{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}\;:\;\overline{\mathrm{img}(x)}\subset I}\}
.

Corollary A.5

(Real analytic Nemytskii operators)
.

Let
V_{\mathbb{R}}
denote a real finite dimensional vector space with complexification denoted by
V
. Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a complex Banach space for which we have the continuous embedding
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
and let
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathcal{Y}\cap L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V_{\mathbb{R}})
denote the real subspace of real-valued functions. Also let
\mathcal{X}
be an admissible unital Banach algebra in the sense of Definition
A.3
in which the pointwise product
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is continuous. Let
a,b\in[-\infty,\infty]
with
a<b
,
I=(a,b)
,
I_{\varepsilon}=(a+\varepsilon,b-\varepsilon)
for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and
\phi:I\to\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{R}}
be real analytic. The following hold.

(1)

For all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)
, there exists
\Upomega_{\phi}(x)\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that for any choice of standard regularizing operators
\{{S_{\epsilon}}\}_{0<\epsilon<1}
and any sequence
\{{\epsilon_{n}}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\subset(0,1)
satisfying
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
we have that for almost every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(A.37)

\lim_{n\to\infty}[{S_{\epsilon_{n}}\phi}](x(\xi))(\xi)=[{\Upomega_{\phi}(x)}](\xi).

(2)

The induced Nemytskii map
\Upomega_{\phi}:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)\to\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{R}}
is real analytic.

(3)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restricted mapping
\Upomega_{\phi}:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I_{\varepsilon})\to\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{R}}
maps bounded sets to bounded sets.

(4)

For each
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)
and
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
there exists
\delta>0
such that in the set
B(\xi_{0},\delta)
we have the a.e. equality

(A.38)

\Upomega_{\phi}(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{n!}(x-x(\xi_{0}))^{n}\phi^{(n)}(x(\xi_{0}))

where the series on the right is absolutely convergent in
L^{1}(B(\xi_{0},\delta),V_{\mathbb{R}})
and almost everywhere pointwise absolutely convergent;
\phi^{(n)}(t)\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{R}}
denotes the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
derivative of
\phi
at
t\in(a,b)
.

(5)

Suppose we have the continuous embedding
\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
as in the fifth item of Proposition
A.4
. Then

(A.39)

[{\Upomega_{\phi}(x)}](\xi)=\phi(x(\xi))(\xi)\text{ for all }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I).

Proof.

The stated claims follow from the complex version, Proposition
A.4
with minor additional arguments.

Indeed, we may find a holomorphic extension of
\phi
,
\Phi:U\to\mathcal{Y}
with
(a,b)\subset U\subset\mathbb{C}
open and simply connected and
\Phi=\phi
on
(a,b)
. The function
\phi
is
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{R}}
-valued real analytic and thus at each point
q\in(a,b)
, there exists
\rho_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and a sequence
\{{c_{q,n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that for all
t\in(q-\rho_{q},q+\rho_{q})\cap(a,b)
we have

(A.40)

\phi(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{q,n}(t-q)^{n}

where the series is, in fact, absolutely and uniformly convergent for (complex-valued)
t\in B_{\mathbb{C}}(q,\rho_{q})
. In this way we obtain a holomorphic extension of
\phi

(A.41)

\Phi:U\to\mathbb{C},\quad U=\bigcup_{q\in(a,b)}B_{\mathbb{C}}(q,\rho_{q})\quad\text{with}\quad\Phi=\phi\text{ on }(a,b)\subset U.

The domain
U
is manifestly simply connected as it retracts to an interval.

Proposition
A.4
applies for
\Phi
and we obtain a complex analytic operator
\Upomega_{\Phi}
on the set
\mathcal{X}(U)
with values in
\mathcal{Y}
.
\Upomega_{\phi}
is then defined via restriction to
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)
. The limiting formula (
A.16
) shows actually that if
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)
, then
\Upomega_{\phi}(x)\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Hence
\Upomega_{\phi}
is real analytic; the remaining claims are directly inherited from the corresponding properties of
\Upomega_{\Phi}
and simple
\mathbb{R}
-valued considerations.
∎

The previous result may be further specialized to study the outer composition of
\mathbb{R}
-valued real analytic functions with
\mathbb{R}
-valued members of admissible Banach algebras.

Corollary A.6

(Real analytic Nemytskii operators - simple case)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an admissible unital Banach algebra in the sense of Definition
A.3
,
a,b\in[-\infty,\infty]
with
a<b
,
I=(a,b)
,
I_{\varepsilon}=(a+\varepsilon,b-\varepsilon)
for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and
\phi:I\to\mathbb{R}
be real analytic. The following hold.

(1)

For all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)
the pointwise composition
\phi(x)=\phi\circ x:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(2)

The induced Nemytskii map
\phi:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)\to\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
given by
x\mapsto\phi(x)
is real analytic.

(3)

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the restricted mapping
\phi:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I_{\varepsilon})\to\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
maps bounded sets to bounded sets to bounded sets.

Proof.

There is the natural embedding of
\mathbb{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
given by
t\mapsto t\cdot 1
where
1\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the unit (the constant function identically unity). In this way we may view the given function
\phi
as a real analytic mapping
I\to\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
. This allows us to invoke Corollary
A.5
with the target space
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
and obtain a real analytic Nemytskii map
\Upomega_{\phi}:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)\to\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
which maps bounded subsets of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I_{\varepsilon})
into bounded subsets of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

To complete the proof we need only establish that
\Upomega_{\phi}(x)=\phi\circ x
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}(I)
. But this is an immediate consequence of the fifth item of Corollary
A.5
.
∎

A.2.
Basic nonlinear analysis in Sobolev spaces

We begin with the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation estimates.

Theorem A.7

(Gagliardo-Nirenberg)
.

Let
\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
\Gamma=\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Let
1\leqslant p,q,t\leqslant\infty
and
s,r\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
with
1\leqslant r<s
and suppose

(A.42)

\frac{r}{s}\frac{1}{p}+\Big(1-\frac{r}{s}\Big)\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{t}.

For all
f\in(L^{q}\cap\dot{W}^{s,p})(\Gamma)
we have that
f\in\dot{W}^{r,t}(\Gamma)
with the estimate

(A.43)

\lVert\nabla^{r}f\rVert_{L^{t}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{q}}^{1-r/s}\lVert\nabla^{s}f\rVert^{r/s}_{L^{p}}

where the implicit constant depends on
d
,
p
,
q
,
t
,
s
, and
r
.

Proof.

For the case
\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{d}
see, for instance, Theorem 12.85 in Leoni
[
37
]
. The case
\Gamma=\mathbb{T}^{d}
can be derived from the
\mathbb{R}^{d}
case by considering an ‘extension’ mapping of the form

(A.44)

(\mathfrak{E}f)(x)=\varphi(x)f(x\;\mathrm{mod}\;\mathbb{Z}^{d}),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

with
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying
\varphi=1
on
[0,1]^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This mapping has the property that for any
\mathfrak{k}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathfrak{p}\in[1,\infty]
, and
g\in W^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
it not only holds that
\mathfrak{E}g\in W^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, but also

(A.45)

\lVert g\rVert_{W^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lesssim\lVert\mathfrak{E}g\rVert_{W^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{W^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}

for implicit constants depending only on
d
,
\mathfrak{k}
,
\mathfrak{p}
, and the choice of
\varphi
. So applying the
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality to
\mathfrak{E}f
gives an initial ‘inhomogeneous’
\mathbb{T}^{d}
estimate of the form

(A.46)

\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r,t}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{q}}^{1-r/s}\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}}^{r/s}.

The homogeneous form (
A.43
) then follows by applying (
A.46
) with
f
replaced by
f-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}f
and using mean-zero Poincaré inequality.
∎

Armed with Theorem
A.7
, we can now establish some important product estimates.

Theorem A.8

(High-low product estimates)
.

Let
\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
\Gamma=\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Suppose that
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
1\leqslant p_{0},p_{1},q_{0},q_{1},t\leqslant\infty
satisfy

(A.47)

\frac{1}{t}=\frac{1}{p_{0}}+\frac{1}{q_{1}}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{0}}.

Let
f_{0}\in(L^{q_{0}}\cap W^{s,p_{0}})(\Gamma)
and
f_{1}\in(L^{q_{1}}\cap W^{s,p_{1}})(\Gamma)
. The pointwise product
f_{0}f_{1}
belongs to
W^{s,t}(\Gamma)
and the following estimate holds

(A.48)

\lVert f_{0}f_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,t}}\lesssim\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{L^{q_{0}}}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,p_{1}}}+\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{W^{s,p_{0}}}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{L^{q_{1}}}.

with implicit constants depending only on
d
,
s
,
t
,
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
,
q_{0}
, and
q_{1}
.

Proof.

We note first that thanks to an equivalent norm and the Leibniz rule we have the bound

(A.49)

\lVert f_{0}f_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,t}}\lesssim\lVert f_{0}f_{1}\rVert_{L^{t}}+\sum_{|\alpha|=s}\sum_{\beta\leqslant\alpha}\lVert\partial^{\alpha-\beta}f_{0}\partial^{\beta}f_{1}\rVert_{L^{t}}.

By the Hölder inequality we have
\lVert f_{0}f_{1}\rVert_{L^{t}}\leqslant\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{L^{p_{0}}}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{L^{q_{1}}}
and we have

(A.50)

\lVert\partial^{\alpha-\beta}f_{0}\partial^{\beta}f_{1}\rVert_{L^{t}}\leqslant\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{W^{|\alpha|-|\beta|,t_{0}}}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{W^{|\beta|,t_{1}}}

with
t_{0},t_{1}\in[1,\infty]
are defined according to

(A.51)

\frac{1}{t_{0}}=\frac{|\alpha|-|\beta|}{s}\frac{1}{p_{0}}+\Big(1-\frac{|\alpha|-|\beta|}{s}\Big)\frac{1}{q_{0}},\quad\frac{1}{t_{1}}=\frac{|\beta|}{s}\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\Big(1-\frac{|\beta|}{s}\Big)\frac{1}{q_{1}}\quad\text{and satisfy}\quad\frac{1}{t}=\frac{1}{t_{0}}+\frac{1}{t_{1}}

thanks to hypothesis (
A.47
). Then, by Theorem
A.7
we have

(A.52)

\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{W^{|\alpha|-|\beta|,t_{0}}}\lesssim\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{L^{q_{0}}}^{|\beta|/s}\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{W^{s,p_{0}}}^{1-|\beta|/s},\quad\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{W^{|\beta|,t_{1}}}\lesssim\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{L^{q_{1}}}^{1-|\beta|/s}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,p_{1}}}^{|\beta|/s}

and so by Young’s inequality for products (in the case that
|\beta|\not\in\{{0,s}\}
)

(A.53)

\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{W^{|\alpha|-|\beta|,t_{0}}}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{W^{|\beta|,t_{1}}}\lesssim\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{L^{q_{0}}}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{W^{s,p_{1}}}+\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{W^{s,p_{0}}}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{L^{q_{1}}}.

Synthesizing the above yields the claimed estimate.
∎

We now establish important results which relates to the analytic Nemytskii operator material in Appendix
A.1
.

Proposition A.9

(Admissibility in the periodic case)
.

Given any
s\in\mathbb{N}
the
p\in[1,\infty]
the function space
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap W^{s,p})(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{C})
is an admissible unital Banach algebra in the sense of Definition
A.3
.

Proof.

Thanks to Theorem
A.8
we are assured that
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap W^{s,p})(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{C})
is indeed a Banach algebra - moreover it is unital where the constant function
1
is the unit. Now given
f\in(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap W^{s,p})(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{C})
it is easy to see that
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{spec}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)}
and so we only need to focus on establishing the opposite inclusion with the required structured estimates. Let
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)}
and set
\varepsilon=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathbb{C}}(\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)},z)>0
. Evidently the function
(z-f)^{-1}
belongs to
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{C})
with
\lVert(z-f)^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
. Since
(L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{s,p})(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow W^{s,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, it therefore remains to estimate the
\dot{W}^{s,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{C})
seminorm of
(z-f)^{-1}
. We do this by first invoking Faà di Bruno’s formula to obtain the estimate

(A.54)

\lVert(z-f)^{-1}\rVert_{\dot{W}^{s,p}}\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{s}\sum_{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{j}=s}\varepsilon^{-j}\lVert\nabla^{k_{1}}f\otimes\cdots\otimes\nabla^{k_{j}}f\rVert_{L^{p}}.

Next, we write
1/p=\sum_{i=1}^{j}1/t_{k_{i}}
with
t_{k_{i}}=(s/k_{i})p\in[1,\infty]
and use Hölder’s inequality followed by Theorem
A.7
to deduce

(A.55)

\lVert\nabla^{k_{1}}f\otimes\cdots\otimes\nabla^{k_{j}}f\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{j}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{1-k_{i}/s}\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}}^{k_{i}/s}=\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{j-1}\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}}.

Combining (
A.54
) with (
A.55
) and the aforementioned reduction gives the resolvent estimate

(A.56)

\lVert(z-f)^{-1}\rVert_{W^{s,p}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}+\max\{{1,\varepsilon^{-s}}\}\langle\lVert f\rVert_{W^{s,p}}\rangle^{s}

with an implicit constant depending only on
s
,
p
, and
d
. This is a bound of the form (
A.12
) and so the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition A.10

(Admissibility in the solitary case)
.

Given any
r\in[1,\infty]
the function space
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
is an admissible unital Banach algebra in the sense of Definition
A.3
.

Proof.

This is a simple calculation as the derivative of the resolvent obeys the formula
\nabla((z-f)^{-1})=(z-f)^{-2}\nabla f
for any
f\in(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap\dot{W}^{1,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
z\not\in\overline{\mathrm{img}(f)}
and so it is obvious that
(z-f)^{-1}
belongs to the same space with the correct structured estimates.
∎

A.3.
Harmonic analysis in weighted square summable Sobolev spaces

In this appendix we record a selection of important tools from Harmonic analysis adapted to the weighted Sobolev spaces of Definition
2.1
in the Hilbert case of
p=2
. We begin with an elementary multiplier theorem which lends us some rather important estimates on band limited functions.

Proposition A.11

(Multiplier bounds and weighted Bernstein inequalities)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in[0,\infty)
. The following hold.

(1)

Let
k=\lceil\delta\rceil\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
. For all
f\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
we have
m(D)f\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
with the estimate

(A.57)

\lVert m(D)f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert m\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}.

(2)

Now suppose that
f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
and
\lambda\in[1,\infty)
.

(a)

If
\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[f]\subset B(0,\lambda)
then
f\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
and
\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lambda^{s}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}
.

(b)

If
\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[f]\subset B(0,\lambda)\setminus\overline{B(0,\lambda/2)}
, then
f\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
and
\lambda^{s}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lambda^{s}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}
.

All implicit constants in the inequalities above depend only on
d
,
s
, and
\delta
.

Proof.

Let us begin by proving the first item. By using the norm equivalence of the first item of Proposition
2.6
followed by the boundedness of the product map
W^{k,\infty}\times H^{\delta}\to H^{\delta}
we obtain

(A.58)

\lVert m(D)f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\asymp\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\lVert m\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f]\rVert_{H^{\delta}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\lVert m\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}.

This proves the first item.

Let us now prove the second item. We fix
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(B(0,2))
with
\varphi=1
on
\overline{B(0,1)}
. If
f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
satisfies
\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[f]\subset B(0,\lambda)
, then
f=\varphi(D/\lambda)f
. By using the equivalent norm of Proposition
2.3
we then find that

(A.59)

\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\asymp\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\lVert\varphi(D/\lambda)\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}=\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\lVert m_{\alpha,\lambda}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}

for the multipliers
m_{\alpha,\lambda}(\xi)=\varphi(\xi/\lambda)(2\pi\mathrm{i}\xi)^{\alpha}
. It is elementary to estimate
\lVert m_{\alpha,\lambda}\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}\lesssim\lambda^{s}
and so by invoking the bounds (
A.57
) on each term in the right hand side’s sum in (
A.59
) we obtain
\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lambda^{s}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}
. Thus part (a) of the second item is justified.

To prove part (b) it only remains to show the complementary lower bound. If
f\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
satisfies
\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[f]\subset B(0,\lambda)\setminus\overline{B(0,\lambda/2)}
, then we have the identity
f=(1-\varphi(D/4\lambda))\varphi(D/\lambda)f
. We can decompose this multiplier via

(A.60)

n_{\alpha,\lambda}(\xi)=(1-\varphi(\xi/4\lambda))\varphi(\xi/\lambda)\frac{\xi^{\alpha}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{s}|\xi|^{2s}}\quad\text{which satisfy}\quad\sum_{|\alpha|=s}n_{\alpha,\lambda}(D)\partial^{\alpha}=(1-\varphi(D/4\lambda))\varphi(D/\lambda).

Therefore, through this decomposition, the elementary bounds
\lVert n_{\alpha,\lambda}\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}\lesssim\lambda^{-s}
, and estimate (
A.57
) we find

(A.61)

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\leqslant\sum_{|\alpha|=s}\lVert n_{\alpha,\lambda}(D)\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lambda^{-s}\sum_{|\alpha|=s}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}.

Now, thanks to Proposition
2.3
again, the right hand side of (
A.61
) is bounded above by
\lambda^{-s}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}
.
∎

The weighted inequalities for band-limited functions in the second item of Proposition
A.11
are the basis of the following Littlewood-Paley results.

Proposition A.12

(Littlewood-Paley)
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional complex inner product space,
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in[0,\infty)
, and set
k=\lceil\delta\rceil
. Assume that
\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
satisfy
\mathrm{supp}(\varphi_{0})\subset B(0,4)
, for
j>0

\mathrm{supp}(\varphi_{j})\subset B(0,2^{j+2})\setminus\overline{B(0,2^{j-2})}
, and
\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}<\infty
. The following hold.

(1)

For all
f\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
we have the estimate

(A.62)

\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}4^{js}\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim(\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}})\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}

for an implicit constant depending only on the dimension,
s
, and
\delta
.

(2)

Assume additionally that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi_{j}=1
; then we may bound for all
f\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)

(A.63)

\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}4^{js}\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}

again with implicit constants depending only on the dimension,
s
, and
\delta
.

(3)

Again suppose that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi_{j}=1
. For all
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
it holds that
f\in H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
if and only if the quantity on the right hand side of (
A.63
) is finite; moreover, we have the equivalence of norms

(A.64)

\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\asymp\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}4^{js}\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}

with implicit constants depending only on the dimension,
s
,
\delta
, and
\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

Let us begin by proving the first item. For brevity, we shall restrict our attention to the case
\delta<1
, as the cases
\delta\geqslant 1
follow from a nearly identical, but more cumbersome argument. Thanks to the second item of Proposition
A.11
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
we have the estimate

(A.65)

2^{js}\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}.

Now to handle
\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\delta}}
, we shall use the specific choice of inner product from the second item of Proposition
2.6
, which leads us to

(A.66)

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\asymp\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\langle\varphi_{j}\mathscr{F}(\partial^{\alpha}f),\varphi_{j}\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f]\rangle^{\star}_{H^{\delta}}\\
=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(|\varphi_{j}(\xi)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)|^{2}+\Big(\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|\varphi_{j}(\xi+h)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi+h)-\varphi_{j}(\xi)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)|^{2}}{|h|^{d+2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}h\Big)\Big)\;\mathrm{d}\xi.

The integral over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
above has zeroth order terms and averaged difference quotient terms. For the former type, we gain by the support hypotheses on
\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
the bound

(A.67)

\Big\lVert\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\char 12\relax{\varphi_{j}}^{2}\Big\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim(\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}\rVert_{L^{\infty}})^{2}

and hence may conclude that

(A.68)

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\char 12\relax{\varphi_{j}(\xi)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\lesssim(\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}\rVert_{L^{\infty}})^{2}\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{\delta}}.

The averaged difference quotient terms in (
A.66
) are somewhat more involved - we initially split according to

(A.69)

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\varphi_{j}(\xi+h)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi+h)-\varphi_{j}(\xi)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)|^{2}\lesssim\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\varphi_{j}(\xi+h)|^{2}\Big)|\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi+h)-\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)|^{2}\\
+\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\varphi_{j}(\xi+h)-\varphi_{j}(\xi)|^{2}\Big)|\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)|^{2}.

Then, by using
|h|\leqslant 1
and the support conditions on
\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
one readily verifies the bounds

(A.70)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\\
|h|\leqslant 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\varphi_{j}(\xi+h)|^{2}\lesssim(\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}\rVert_{L^{\infty}})^{2},\quad\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\\
|h|\leqslant 1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|h|^{2}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\varphi_{j}(\xi+h)-\varphi_{j}(\xi)|^{2}\lesssim(\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}})^{2}

which lead us to the estimate

(A.71)

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\varphi_{j}(\xi+h)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi+h)-\varphi_{j}(\xi)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)|^{2}\\
\lesssim(\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}})^{2}\big[{|\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi+h)-\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)|^{2}+|h|^{2}|\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)|^{2}}\big].

The
|h|^{2}
term above introduces the integrable singularity
|h|^{-d+2-2\delta}
. Therefore, it holds

(A.72)

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|\varphi_{j}(\xi+h)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi+h)-\varphi_{j}(\xi)\mathscr{F}[\partial^{\alpha}f](\xi)|^{2}}{|h|^{d+2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}h\;\mathrm{d}\xi\lesssim(\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}})^{2}\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{\delta}}.

Upon synthesizing (
A.66
) with the bounds, (
A.65
), (
A.68
), (
A.71
), and (
A.72
) we arrive at estimate (
A.62
) and so the proof of the first item is complete.

In proving the second item the inner product of Proposition
2.6
will again be quite useful. By the hypotheses on
\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
we have that
f=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi_{j}(D)f
and hence

(A.73)

\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\asymp\langle f,f\rangle_{H^{s}_{\delta}}^{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\langle\varphi_{i}(D)f,\varphi_{j}(D)f\rangle_{H^{s}_{\delta}}^{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{|\iota|\leqslant 6}\langle\varphi_{j}(D)f,\varphi_{j+\iota}(D)\rangle^{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{H^{s}_{\delta}}

where the final equality follows from the third item of Proposition
2.6
(and we are using the convention that if
j+\iota<0
then
\varphi_{j+\iota}=0
). Therefore, by Cauchy-Schwarz and the second item of Proposition
A.11
we have

(A.74)

\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}4^{js}\lVert\varphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}.

This is exactly (
A.63
).

The third item follows by a simple synthesis of the first two items - we omit the details for brevity.
∎

Proposition
A.12
leads us to the following generalization of the spaces from Definition
2.1
in the case
p=2
to non integer regularity indices
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Definition A.13

(Generalized weighted square summable Sobolev spaces)
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional complex inner product space,
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\delta\in[0,\infty)
, and
k=\lceil\delta\rceil
. We define

(A.75)

{{}^{\star}}H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)=\{{f\in\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)\;:\;\lVert f\rVert_{{{}^{\star}}H^{s}_{\delta}}<\infty}\},\quad\lVert f\rVert_{{{}^{\star}}H^{s}_{\delta}}=\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}4^{js}\lVert\upvarphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2},

where
\{{\upvarphi_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
satisfy the dyadic annular support conditions as in Proposition
A.12
,
\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert\upvarphi_{j}\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}<\infty
, and
1=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\upvarphi_{j}
.

Remark A.14
.

The third item of Proposition
A.12
shows that whenever
s\in\mathbb{N}
we have the coincidence
{{}^{\star}}H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)=H^{s}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
.

The final part of this subsection is dedicated to proving a strengthening of the multiplier theorem from the first item of Proposition
A.11
. For this we require the following lemma.

Lemma A.15

(Hardy’s inequality)
.

Let
\delta\in[0,d/2)
. There exists a
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for any function
f\in H^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
we have the estimate

(A.76)

\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x)|^{2}}{|x|^{2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{H^{\delta}}.

Proof.

Theorem 2.57 in Bahouri, Chemin, and Danchin
[
2
]
proves inequality (
A.76
) for the subspace of functions
f\in H^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[f]
. In the general case, we can take
f\in H^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
and split
f=\varphi(D)f+(1-\varphi(D))f
with
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(B(0,2))
a radial function satisfying
\varphi=1
on
\overline{B(0,1)}
. Then by using the triangle inequality and the fact that
|x|^{-2\delta}\leqslant 1
for
|x|\geqslant 1
:

(A.77)

\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x)|^{2}}{|x|^{2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant\Big(\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|\varphi(D)f(x)|^{2}}{|x|^{2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/2}+\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,1)}|\varphi(D)f|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}+C\lVert(1-\varphi(D))f\rVert_{H^{\delta}}.

Hence

(A.78)

\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x)|^{2}}{|x|^{2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\lVert\varphi(D)f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\lVert f\rVert_{H^{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{\delta}}.

Note in the above we are tacitly using Bernstein’s inequalities.
∎

Proposition A.16

(Improved multiplier bounds)
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional complex inner product space,
s,r\in\mathbb{R}
,
\delta\in[0,d/2)
,
k=\lceil\delta\rceil
, and
m\in W^{k,\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{0}\};\mathbb{C})
a symbol obeying the estimates

(A.79)

\underset{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{esssup}}\Big[{\langle\xi\rangle^{-r}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\min\{{1,|\xi|^{j}}\}|\nabla^{j}m(\xi)|}\Big]=[m]_{k,r}<\infty.

Then for any
f\in{{}^{\star}}H^{s+r}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
we have that
m(D)f\in{{}^{\star}}H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
along with the estimate

(A.80)

\lVert m(D)f\rVert_{{{}^{\star}}H^{s}_{\delta}}\lesssim[m]_{k,r}\lVert f\rVert_{{{}^{\star}}H^{s+r}_{\delta}},

with implicit constants depending only on the dimension,
\delta
,
s
, and
r
.

Proof.

Let
\{{\upvarphi_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
denote the dyadic partition of unity as in Definition
A.13
and denote the ‘fattened projectors’ by

(A.81)

\widetilde{\upvarphi}_{j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathbb{N}\\
|i-j|\leqslant 6\end{subarray}}\upvarphi_{i}\quad\text{and observe that}\quad\upvarphi_{j}=\widetilde{\upvarphi}_{j}\upvarphi_{j}.

This leads us to defining the localized multipliers:

(A.82)

m_{j}=\widetilde{\upvarphi}_{j}m\quad\text{and we compute using~\eqref{the hypotheses on the multiplier} that}\quad\begin{cases}\sum_{i=0}^{k}\lVert|X|^{i}\nabla^{i}m_{j}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim[m]_{k,r}&\text{if }j<10,\\
\lVert m_{j}\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}\lesssim 2^{jr}[m]_{k,r}&\text{if }j\geqslant 10.\end{cases}

So we learn from the above and the definition of the norm (
A.75
) that

(A.83)

\lVert m(D)f\rVert_{{{}^{\star}}H^{s}_{\delta}}=\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}4^{sj}\lVert m_{j}(D)\upvarphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{9}\lVert m_{j}(D)\upvarphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}\\
+\Big(\sum_{j=10}^{\infty}4^{sj}\lVert m_{j}(D)\upvarphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II}.

We shall estimate
\mathbf{II}
first, as it is simpler. Due to the estimates of (
A.82
) on
m_{j}
for
j\geqslant 10
we are free to invoke the first item of Proposition
A.11
to obtain

(A.84)

\mathbf{II}=\Big(\sum_{j=10}^{\infty}4^{sj}\lVert m_{j}(D)\upvarphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\Big(\sum_{j=10}^{\infty}4^{(s+r)j}\lVert\upvarphi_{j}(D)f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{{{}^{\star}}H^{s+r}_{\delta}}.

Now we turn our attention to estimating
\mathbf{I}
in (
A.83
). We shall prove that for each
j\in\{{0,\dots,9}\}
we have the bound

(A.85)

\lVert m_{j}(D)\upvarphi_{j}(D)f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert\upvarphi_{j}(D)f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\quad\text{from which it follows that}\quad\mathbf{I}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{{{}^{\star}}H^{s+r}_{\delta}}.

Evidently, it is sufficient to show that for any
n\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
satisfying
[n]_{k,0}<\infty
(recall (
A.79
)) then we have for all
g\in L^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)
the estimate
\lVert n(D)g\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim[n]_{k,0}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}
. We shall only explicitly write out the case
\delta<1
, as the cases
\delta\geqslant 1
can be felled with similar arguments. We use the equivalent norm from the first item of Proposition
2.6
to estimate

(A.86)

\lVert n(D)g\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\lVert n\mathscr{F}[g]\rVert_{L^{2}}+\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|n(\xi+h)\mathscr{F}[g](\xi+h)-n(\xi)\mathscr{F}[g](\xi)|^{2}}{|h|^{d+2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}h\;\mathrm{d}\xi\Big).

Clearly we have
\lVert n\mathscr{F}[g]\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim[n]_{1,0}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}
and so we only need to study further the averaged difference quotients term above. We make the usual splitting

(A.87)

n(\xi+h)\mathscr{F}[g](\xi+h)-n(\xi)\mathscr{F}[g](\xi)=n(\xi+h)(\mathscr{F}[g](\xi+h)-\mathscr{F}[g](\xi))+(n(\xi+h)-n(\xi))\mathscr{F}[g](\xi)

and so

(A.88)

\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|n(\xi+h)\mathscr{F}[g](\xi+h)-n(\xi)\mathscr{F}[g](\xi)|}{|h|^{d+2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}h\;\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)\lesssim[n]_{1,0}\lVert\mathscr{F}[g]\rVert_{H^{\delta}}\\
+\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[g](\xi)}^{2}\Big(\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|n(\xi+h)-n(\xi)|^{2}}{|h|^{d+2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}h\Big)\;\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)^{1/2}.

To continue, we shall prove the pointwise estimate

(A.89)

\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|n(\xi+h)-n(\xi)|^{2}}{|h|^{d+2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}h\lesssim([n]_{1,0}|\xi|^{-\delta})^{2}.

Decompose
B(0,1)=A_{\xi}\cup B_{\xi}
with
A_{\xi}=\{{h\in B(0,1)\;:\;|h|\leqslant|\xi|/2}\}
and
B_{\xi}=\{{h\in B(0,1)\;:\;|h|>|\xi|/2}\}
. In the region
A_{\xi}
we exploit the difference with the fundamental theorem of calculus

(A.90)

|n(\xi+h)-n(\xi)|\lesssim[n]_{1,0}|h|\int_{0}^{1}|\xi+\tau h|^{-1}\;\mathrm{d}\tau\lesssim[n]_{1,0}|h||\xi|^{-1}

and so by
\delta<1
we have

(A.91)

\int_{A_{\xi}}\frac{|n(\xi+h)-n(\xi)|^{2}}{|h|^{d+2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}h\lesssim[n]^{2}_{1,0}|\xi|^{-2}\int_{B(0,|\xi|)}|h|^{-d-2\delta+2}\;\mathrm{d}h\lesssim([n]_{1,0}|\xi|^{-\delta})^{2}.

On the other hand, in the region
B_{\xi}
we simply bound
|n(\xi+h)-n(\xi)|\lesssim[n]_{1,0}
without exploiting the difference:

(A.92)

\int_{B_{\xi}}\frac{|n(\xi+h)-n(\xi)|^{2}}{|h|^{d+2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}h\lesssim[n]_{1,0}^{2}\int_{B(0,1)\setminus B(0,|\xi|/2)}|h|^{-d-2\delta}\;\mathrm{d}h\lesssim([n]_{1,0}|\xi|^{-\delta})^{2}.

Equations (
A.91
) and (
A.92
) give (
A.89
). Now we may return to the final term in (
A.88
) and invoke Lemma
A.15
:

(A.93)

\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[g](\xi)}^{2}\Big(\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|n(\xi+h)-n(\xi)|^{2}}{|h|^{d+2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}h\Big)\;\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim[n]_{1,0}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\mathscr{F}[g](\xi)|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2\delta}}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim[n]_{1,0}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}_{\delta}}.

By collecting the above the claimed estimate on
n(D)g
is verified and with that the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix B
Derivation of the shallow water equations with bathymetry

In this appendix we compute the shallow water system of equations (
1.1
) through a rescaling and asymptotic expansion procedure beginning with the free boundary Navier-Stokes equations over a smooth graphical bathymetry. Our derivation is largely inspired by those of Marche
[
43
]
, Bresch
[
9
]
, and Mascia
[
44
]
, but ours includes bathymetry, forcing, and a generalized parameter scaling. The latter means that we can view the shallow water system as an approximation of the Navier-Stokes system in the regimes of either long wavelength or small depth, but our technique actually allows for intermediate scaling regimes as well.

Sections
B.1
and
B.2
set up the Navier-Stokes equations in a form amenable to asymptotic expansion while in Section
B.3
we finally compute the system of shallow water equations. We note that throughout this appendix we frequently abuse notation by neglecting our variables’ time dependence.

B.1.
The free boundary Navier-Stokes system with bathymetry

The physical spatial dimension is denoted by
n\in\{{2,3}\}
. We decompose our differential operators into horizontal and vertical parts through the following notation
\boldsymbol{\nabla}=(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n-1},\partial_{n})
and
\nabla=(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{n-1})
so that
\boldsymbol{\nabla}=(\nabla,\partial_{n})
. We also set
\Delta=\partial_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\partial_{n-1}^{2}
,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}=\Delta+\partial_{n}^{2}
.

The free surface and the bathymetry shall be described graphically and are represented by the functions
\zeta,\beta:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
(respectively); these are assumed to satisfy a strict ‘no penetration’ condition:
\beta<\zeta
. Using these functions we define the bulk domain

(B.1)

\Omega[\beta,\zeta]=\{{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;\beta(x)<y<\zeta(x)}\}

along with its lower and upper boundaries

(B.2)

\Sigma[\beta]=\{{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;y=\beta(x)}\},\quad\Sigma[\zeta]=\{{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;y=\zeta(x)}\}.

The fluid velocity and pressure are defined in the bulk and are denoted by
w:\Omega[\beta,\zeta]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r:\Omega[\beta,\zeta]\to\mathbb{R}
.

We allow the fluid to be acted upon, in addition to the gravitational force in the
-e_{n}
direction with acceleration strength
g\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, generic bulk and interface forces represented by the vector
F:\Omega[\beta,\zeta]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the symmetric 2-tensor
T:\Sigma[\zeta]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\mathrm{sym}}
. The fluid also experiences the internal forces of viscosity and surface tension; the coefficients of which are
\mu>0
and
\varsigma\geqslant 0
. On the bottom the fluid experiences a frictional force which is encoded via the (laminar) Navier slip boundary condition with strength coefficient
\alpha\geqslant 0
.

The free boundary Navier-Stokes equations are given by:

(B.3)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}w+w\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}w+\boldsymbol{\nabla}r-\mu\boldsymbol{\Delta}w=F,\quad\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot w=0&\text{in }\Omega[\beta,\zeta],\\
-(r-\mu\boldsymbol{\mathbb{D}}w)\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}+(g\zeta-\varsigma\mathcal{H}(\eta))\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}=T\mathcal{N}_{\zeta},\quad\partial_{t}\zeta-w\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}=0&\text{on }\Sigma[\zeta],\\
(I-\nu_{\beta}\otimes\nu_{\beta})(\mu\boldsymbol{\mathbb{D}}w\nu_{\beta}-\alpha w)=0,\quad w\cdot\nu_{\beta}=0&\text{on }\Sigma[\beta].\end{cases}

In the above we set
\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}=(-\nabla\zeta,1)
,
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}=(-\nabla\beta,1)
,
\nu_{\beta}=-\mathcal{N}_{\beta}/|\mathcal{N}_{\beta}|
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{D}}w=\boldsymbol{\nabla}w+\boldsymbol{\nabla}w^{\mathrm{t}}
, and
\mathcal{H}(\eta)=\nabla\cdot((1+|\nabla\zeta|^{2})^{-1/2}\nabla\zeta)
.

Our task now is to rewrite system (
B.3
) into a horizontal-vertical decomposed form. We shall decompose the velocity vector field into its vertical and horizontal components labeled as
w=(U,V)
with
U:\Omega[\beta,\zeta]\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
V=\Omega[\beta,\zeta]\to\mathbb{R}
. The symmetrized gradient of
w
shall be decomposed according to

(B.4)

\boldsymbol{\mathbb{D}}w=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{D}U&\nabla V+\partial_{n}U\\
\nabla V+\partial_{n}U&2\partial_{n}V\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{where}\quad\mathbb{D}U=\nabla U+\nabla U^{\mathrm{t}}.

In terms of these decomposed variables, the equations (
B.3
) are equivalently written as

(B.5)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla U+V\partial_{n}U-\mu\Delta U-\mu\partial_{n}^{2}U+\nabla r=F_{\|}&\text{in }\Omega[\beta,\zeta],\\
\partial_{t}V+U\cdot\nabla V+V\partial_{n}V-\mu\Delta V-\mu\partial_{n}^{2}V+\partial_{n}r=F_{n}&\text{in }\Omega[\beta,\zeta],\\
\nabla\cdot U+\partial_{n}V=0&\text{in }\Omega[\beta,\zeta],\\
(-r+g\zeta-\varsigma\mathcal{H}(\zeta))\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla\zeta\\
1\end{pmatrix}\\
\quad+\mu\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbb{D}U\nabla\zeta+\nabla V+\partial_{n}U\\
-(\nabla V+\partial_{n}U)\cdot\nabla\zeta+2\partial_{n}V\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\Xi\nabla\zeta+\xi\\
-\nabla\zeta\cdot\xi+\varphi\end{pmatrix}&\text{on }\Sigma[\zeta],\\
\partial_{t}\zeta=V-U\cdot\nabla\zeta&\text{on }\Sigma[\zeta],\\
V-U\cdot\nabla\beta=0&\text{on }\Sigma[\beta],\\
\frac{2\nabla\beta}{1+|\nabla\beta|^{2}}\partial_{n}V+\big(I-2\frac{\nabla\beta\otimes\nabla\beta}{1+|\nabla\beta|^{2}}\big)(\partial_{n}U+\nabla V)&\\
\quad-\big(I-\frac{\nabla\beta\otimes\nabla\beta}{1+|\nabla\beta|^{2}}\big)\mathbb{D}U\nabla\beta=\frac{\alpha}{\mu}(1+|\nabla\beta|^{2})^{1/2}U&\text{on }\Sigma[\beta],\\
\frac{2|\nabla\beta|^{2}}{1+|\nabla\beta|^{2}}\partial_{n}V+\frac{1-|\nabla\beta|^{2}}{1+|\nabla\beta|^{2}}(\nabla V+\partial_{n}U)\cdot\nabla\beta&\\
\quad-\frac{1}{1+|\nabla\beta|^{2}}\mathbb{D}U\nabla\beta\cdot\nabla\beta=\frac{\alpha}{\mu}(1+|\nabla\beta|^{2})^{1/2}U\cdot\nabla\beta&\text{on }\Sigma[\beta],\end{cases}

where we have decomposed the force data as

(B.6)

F=(F_{\|},F_{n})\quad\text{and}\quad T=\begin{pmatrix}\Xi&\xi\\
\xi&\varphi\end{pmatrix}.

B.2.
Rescaled and flattened equations

We let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
denote a (small) dimensionless scaling factor,
\theta\in[0,1]
an interpolation parameter, and we introduce (using the notation
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
) the following new rescaled variables
u
,
v
,
p
,
\eta
,
b
,
\mathbf{g}
,
\mathbf{a}
,
\sigma
,
f_{\|}
,
f_{n}
,
\Upxi
,
\upxi
, and
\upvarphi
:

(B.7)

U(t,x,y)=\varepsilon^{\theta}u(\varepsilon^{2\theta}t,\varepsilon^{\theta}x,\varepsilon^{\theta-1}y),\;V(t,x,y)=\varepsilon^{1+\theta}v(\varepsilon^{2\theta}t,\varepsilon^{\theta}x,\varepsilon^{\theta-1}y),\\
r(t,x,y)=\varepsilon^{2\theta}p(\varepsilon^{2\theta}t,\varepsilon^{\theta}x,\varepsilon^{\theta-1}y),\;\zeta(t,x)=\varepsilon^{1-\theta}\eta(\varepsilon^{2\theta}t,\varepsilon^{\theta}x),\;\beta(x)=\varepsilon^{1-\theta}b(\varepsilon^{\theta}x),\\
g=\varepsilon^{3\theta-1}\mathbf{g},\;\alpha=\varepsilon^{1+\theta}\mathbf{a},\;\varsigma=\varepsilon^{\theta-1}\sigma,\;F_{\|}(t,x,y)=\varepsilon^{3\theta}f_{\|}(\varepsilon^{2\theta}t,\varepsilon^{\theta}x,\varepsilon^{\theta-1}y),\\
F_{n}(t,x,y)=\varepsilon^{1+3\theta}f_{n}(\varepsilon^{2\theta}t,\varepsilon^{\theta}x,\varepsilon^{\theta-1}y),\;\Xi(t,x,y)=\varepsilon^{2\theta}\Upxi(\varepsilon^{2\theta}t,\varepsilon^{\theta}x,\varepsilon^{\theta-1}y),\\
\xi(t,x,y)=\varepsilon^{1+2\theta}\upxi(\varepsilon^{2\theta}t,\varepsilon^{\theta}x,\varepsilon^{\theta-1}y),\;\varphi(t,x,y)=\varepsilon^{2\theta}\upvarphi(\varepsilon^{2\theta}t,\varepsilon^{\theta}x,\varepsilon^{\theta-1}y).

The parameter
\theta
is interpolating between a small depth approximation (
\theta=0
) and a long wavelength approximation (
\theta=1
).

The equivalent equations satisfied by the rescaled variables are given as follows.

(B.8)

\begin{cases}\big[{-\mu\partial_{n}^{2}u}\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[{\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u+v\partial_{n}u-\mu\Delta u+\nabla p-f_{\|}}\big]=0&\text{in }\Omega[b,\eta],\\
\big[{-\mu\partial_{n}^{2}v+\partial_{n}p}\big]+O(\varepsilon^{2})=0&\text{in }\Omega[b,\eta],\\
\nabla\cdot u+\partial_{n}v=0&\text{in }\Omega[b,\eta],\\
\big[{\mu\partial_{n}u}\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[{(p-\mathbf{g}\eta+\sigma\Delta\eta)\nabla\eta-\mu\mathbb{D}u\nabla\eta+\mu\nabla v+\Upxi\nabla\eta-\upxi}\big]+O(\varepsilon^{4})=0&\text{on }\Sigma[\eta],\\
\big[{-p+\mathbf{g}\eta-\sigma\Delta\eta-\mu\nabla\eta\cdot\partial_{n}u+2\mu\partial_{n}v-\upvarphi}\big]+O(\varepsilon^{2})=0&\text{on }\Sigma[\eta],\\
\partial_{t}\eta=v-u\cdot\nabla\eta&\text{on }\Sigma[\eta],\\
v-u\cdot\nabla b=0&\text{on }\Sigma[b],\\
\big[{\partial_{n}u}\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[{-(\mathbf{a}/\mu)u+\nabla v+2\nabla b\partial_{n}v-2\partial_{n}u\cdot\nabla b\nabla b-\mathbb{D}u\nabla b}\big]+O(\varepsilon^{4})=0&\text{on }\Sigma[b],\\
O(1)=O(1)&\text{on }\Sigma[b].\end{cases}

All terms hidden in the expressions
O(\varepsilon^{k})
for
k\in\{{0,2,4}\}
are easily computed, but we do not write them above as they play no role in the derivation that follows; in particular the last equation in (
B.8
) is completely irrelevant. Note that after this rescaling the dependence on the interpolation parameter
\theta
drops out.

Our next task is to flatten these rescaled equations of (
B.8
) to a simple slab domain
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,1)
. We utilize the linear homotopy flattening mapping

(B.9)

\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}:\Omega\to\Omega[b,\eta],\quad\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}(x,z)=(x,z\eta(x)+(1-z)b(x)),\quad(x,z)\in\Omega.

We then consider the equations as written in terms of the flattened unknowns
\mathbf{u}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
,
\mathbf{v}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbf{p}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}

(B.10)

\mathbf{u}=u\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b},\quad\mathbf{v}=v\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b},\quad\mathbf{p}=p\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}.

The following geometric quantities and differential operators are then derived from the flattening map:

(B.11)

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n-1}&0_{(n-1)\times 1}\\
z\nabla\eta+(1-z)\nabla b&\eta-b\end{pmatrix},\quad J_{\eta,b}=\det\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}=\eta-b,\;K_{\eta,b}=1/J_{\eta,b}=(\eta-b)^{-1},

and

(B.12)

\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b})^{-\mathrm{t}}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{(n-1)\times(n-1)}&-K_{\eta,b}(z\nabla\eta+(1-z)\nabla b)\\
0_{1\times(n-1)}&K_{\eta,b}\end{pmatrix}.

Write
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}=\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}\boldsymbol{\nabla}
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}=(\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}},\partial_{n}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}})
so that (for
j\in\{{1,\dots,n-1}\}
)

(B.13)

\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}=\nabla-K_{\eta,b}(z\nabla\eta+(1-z)\nabla b)\partial_{n},\quad\partial_{j}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}=\partial_{j}-K_{\eta,b}(z\partial_{j}\eta+(1-z)\partial_{j}b)\partial_{n},\quad\partial_{n}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}=K_{\eta,b}\partial_{n}.

We also set
\partial_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}=\partial_{t}-z\partial_{t}\eta K_{\eta,b}\partial_{n}
,
\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(\partial_{j}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}})^{2}
, and
(\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{u})_{ij}=\partial_{j}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{u}_{i}+\partial_{i}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{u}_{j}
.

We write the equations (equivalent to (
B.8
)) for the flattened variables as

(B.14)

\begin{cases}[-\mu K_{\eta,b}^{2}\partial_{n}^{2}\mathbf{u}]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[{\partial_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}K_{\eta,b}\partial_{n}\mathbf{u}-\mu\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{p}-f_{\|}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}}\big]=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\big[{-\mu K_{\eta,b}^{2}\partial_{n}^{2}\mathbf{v}+K_{\eta,b}\partial_{n}\mathbf{p}}\big]+O(\varepsilon^{2})=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\cdot\mathbf{u}+K_{\eta,b}\partial_{n}\mathbf{v}=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\big[{\mu K_{\eta,b}\partial_{n}\mathbf{u}}\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[{(\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{g}\eta+\sigma\Delta\eta)\nabla\eta-\mu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{u}\nabla\eta+\mu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{v}+\Upxi\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}\nabla\eta-\upxi\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}}\big]&\\
\quad+O(\varepsilon^{4})=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{1},\\
\big[{-\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{g}\eta-\sigma\Delta\eta-\mu K_{\eta,b}\nabla\eta\cdot\partial_{n}\mathbf{u}+2\mu K_{\eta,b}\partial_{n}\mathbf{v}-\upvarphi\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}}\big]+O(\varepsilon^{2})=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{1},\\
\partial_{t}\eta=\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\eta&\text{on }\Sigma_{1},\\
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla b=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0},\\
\big[{K_{\eta,b}\partial_{n}\mathbf{u}}\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[{-(\mathbf{a}/\mu)\mathbf{u}+\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{v}+2K_{\eta,b}\nabla b\partial_{n}\mathbf{v}-2K_{\eta,b}\nabla b\nabla b\cdot\partial_{n}\mathbf{u}-\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b}}\mathbf{u}\nabla b}\big]+O(\varepsilon^{4})=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0},\\
O(1)=O(1)&\text{on }\Sigma_{0},\end{cases}

where in the above we have labeled the top and bottom boundaries of
\Omega
as
\Sigma_{i}=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\{{i}\}
for
i\in\{{0,1}\}
.

B.3.
Expansion and shallow water equations

With system (
B.14
) in hand we have now set the stage for the derivation to truly begin. To continue, we make the asymptotic expansion ansatz

(B.15)

(\eta,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{p})=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{2\nu}(\eta_{\nu},u_{\nu},v_{\nu},p_{\nu})

for the solution to system (
B.14
). In other words we write each of the unknowns
\eta
,
\mathbf{u}
,
\mathbf{v}
, and
\mathbf{p}
as a power series in
\varepsilon^{2}
. Our goal now is to collect the resulting equations corresponding to like powers of
\varepsilon
and then perform manipulations until we isolate a closed system for the lowest order unknowns
\eta_{0}
,
u_{0}
,
v_{0}
, and
p_{0}
. As it turns out the equations for
v_{0}
and
p_{0}
‘trivialize’ and what we are effectively left with is a closed system for
\eta_{0}
and
u_{0}
.

After plugging in the ansatz (
B.15
) into system (
B.14
) we now collect the relevant zeroth order equations.

(B.16)

\begin{cases}-\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}^{2}u_{0}=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
-\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}^{2}v_{0}+K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}p_{0}=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta_{0},b}}\cdot u_{0}+K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}v_{0}=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}u_{0}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{1},\\
-p_{0}+\mathbf{g}\eta_{0}-\sigma\Delta\eta_{0}-\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\nabla\eta\cdot\partial_{n}u_{0}+2\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}v_{0}-\upvarphi\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta_{0},b}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{1},\\
\partial_{t}\eta_{0}=v_{0}-u_{0}\cdot\nabla\eta_{0}&\text{on }\Sigma_{1},\\
v_{0}-u_{0}\cdot\nabla b=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0},\\
K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}u_{0}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

The first and fourth equations of (
B.16
) imply that
\partial_{n}u_{0}=0
and so
u_{0}
is a function of
t
and
x
. Next, we combine the third and seventh equations to deduce that

(B.17)

\partial_{n}v_{0}=-(\eta_{0}-b)\nabla\cdot u_{0}\quad\text{and hence}\quad v_{0}=u_{0}\cdot\nabla b-z(\eta_{0}-b)\nabla\cdot u_{0}.

In particular, we deduce that
\partial_{n}^{2}v_{0}=0
; therefore, from this fact and the second equation of (
B.16
) we have that
\partial_{n}p_{0}=0
. Then, from the fifth equation we obtain the following expression for the pressure

(B.18)

p_{0}=\mathbf{g}\eta_{0}-\sigma\Delta\eta_{0}-2\mu\nabla\cdot u_{0}-\upvarphi(\cdot,\eta_{0}).

We come to the sixth equation of (
B.16
) and insert (
B.17
) to deduce

(B.19)

\partial_{t}\eta_{0}=u_{0}\cdot\nabla b-(\eta_{0}-b)\nabla\cdot u_{0}-u_{0}\cdot\nabla\eta_{0}=-\nabla\cdot((\eta_{0}-b)u_{0}).

This is the shallow water free surface transport equation.

At this point, we have used all of the equations in (
B.16
) and so it is time to see what information can be gleaned from the next order. The relevant second order equations of expansion (
B.15
) in system (
B.14
) are as follows:

(B.20)

\begin{cases}-\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}^{2}\partial_{n}^{2}u_{1}+\partial_{t}u_{0}+u_{0}\cdot\nabla u_{0}-\mu\Delta u_{0}+\nabla p_{0}-f_{\|}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta,b}=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}u_{1}+(p_{0}-\mathbf{g}\eta_{0}+\sigma\Delta\eta_{0})\nabla\eta_{0}-\mu\mathbb{D}u_{0}\nabla\eta_{0}+\mu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta_{0},b}}v_{0}+\Upxi(\cdot,\eta_{0})\nabla\eta_{0}-\upxi(\cdot,\eta_{0})=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{1},\\
\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}u_{1}-\mathbf{a}u_{0}+\mu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta_{0},b}}v_{0}+2\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\nabla b\partial_{n}v_{0}-\mu\mathbb{D}u_{0}\nabla b=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

Now we multiply the first equation in (
B.20
) by
J_{\eta_{0},b}
, integrate from
0
to
1
in the
z
-variable, and then use the fundamental theorem of calculus on the
\partial_{n}^{2}u_{1}
term. This results in

(B.21)

(\eta_{0}-b)\Big(\partial_{t}u_{0}+u_{0}\cdot\nabla u_{0}-\mu\Delta u_{0}+\nabla p_{0}-\int_{0}^{1}f_{\|}(\cdot,z\eta_{0}+(1-z)b)\;\mathrm{d}z\Big)=\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}(\partial_{n}u_{1}(\cdot,1)-\partial_{n}u_{1}(\cdot,0)).

This expression is the momentum equation of the shallow water equations in disguise. We need only unpack it by examining the boundary values of
\partial_{n}u_{1}
on the right hand side and plugging in the previously derived expressions for
p_{0}
and
v_{0}
(see (
B.18
) and (
B.17
)).

Now by the second equation in (
B.20
) we have (at
z=1
):

(B.22)

\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}u_{1}=(2\mu\nabla\cdot u_{0}+\upvarphi(\cdot,\eta_{0}))\nabla\eta_{0}+\mu\mathbb{D}u_{0}\nabla\eta_{0}+\mu\eta_{0}\nabla\nabla\cdot u_{0}-\mu\nabla\nabla\cdot(bu_{0})-\Upxi(\cdot,\eta_{0})\nabla\eta_{0}+\upxi(\cdot,\eta_{0})=0.

In the above we have tacitly used the identity for
z=1
:

(B.23)

\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta_{0},b}}v_{0}=\nabla v_{0}-K_{\eta_{0},b}\nabla\eta_{0}\partial_{n}v_{0}=\nabla(\nabla\cdot(bu_{0})-\eta_{0}\nabla\cdot u_{0})+\nabla\cdot u_{0}\nabla\eta_{0}=\nabla\nabla\cdot(bu_{0})-\eta_{0}\nabla\nabla\cdot u_{0}.

From the third equation in (
B.20
) we deduce that (at
z=0
)

(B.24)

\mu K_{\eta_{0},b}\partial_{n}u_{1}=\mathbf{a}u_{0}-\mu\nabla(u_{0}\cdot\nabla b)-\mu\nabla b\nabla\cdot u_{0}+2\mu\nabla b\nabla\cdot u_{0}+\mu\mathbb{D}u_{0}\nabla b=0.

In (
B.24
) we have used the identity for
z=0
:

(B.25)

\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\eta_{0},b}}v_{0}=\nabla(u\cdot\nabla b)+\nabla b\nabla\cdot u_{0}.

We isolate the coefficient of
\mu
in the synthesis of expressions (
B.21
), (
B.22
), and (
B.24
). This will give us the form of the dissipation term:

(B.26)

(\eta_{0}-b)\Delta u_{0}+2(\eta_{0}-b)\nabla\nabla\cdot u_{0}+2\nabla\cdot u_{0}\nabla\eta_{0}+\mathbb{D}u_{0}\nabla\eta_{0}+\eta_{0}\nabla\nabla\cdot u_{0}-\nabla\nabla\cdot(bu_{0})\\
+\nabla(u_{0}\cdot\nabla b)+\nabla b\nabla\cdot u_{0}-2\nabla b\nabla\cdot u_{0}-\mathbb{D}u_{0}\nabla b=(\eta_{0}-b)\Delta u_{0}+2(\eta_{0}-b)\nabla\nabla\cdot u_{0}+2\nabla(\eta_{0}-b)\nabla\cdot u_{0}\\
+\mathbb{D}u_{0}\nabla(\eta_{0}-b)+(\eta_{0}-b_{0})\nabla\nabla\cdot u_{0}=\nabla\cdot((\eta_{0}-b)\mathbb{S}u_{0}),

with
\mathbb{S}u_{0}=\mathbb{D}u_{0}+2(\nabla\cdot u_{0})I
. Therefore, we synthesize the above to arrive at the shallow water momentum equation:

(B.27)

(\eta_{0}-b)(\partial_{t}u_{0}+u_{0}\cdot\nabla u_{0}+\nabla(\mathbf{g}\eta_{0}-\sigma\Delta\eta_{0}))+\mathbf{a}u_{0}-\mu\nabla\cdot((\eta_{0}-b)\mathbb{S}u_{0})\\
=\int_{b}^{\eta_{0}}f_{\|}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y+(\eta_{0}-b)\nabla(\upvarphi(\cdot,\eta_{0}))+\upvarphi(\cdot,\eta_{0})\nabla\eta_{0}-\Upxi(\cdot,\eta_{0})\nabla\eta_{0}+\upxi(\cdot,\eta_{0}),

By combining (
B.27
) with the transport equation (
B.19
) we find the form of the equations (
1.1
) mentioned in Section
1.1
.
